Cruise ship pianist rescued from sea after nine hours in Pacific Ocean

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The man identified himself as a pianist on the Ovation of the Seas cruise ship

A cruise ship pianist was found treading water around 20 miles (32km) off the Californian coast after falling overboard, according to US media reports.

Two fishermen discovered the Royal Caribbean employee near Catalina Island on Friday and officials believe he may have been in the Pacific Ocean for nine hours, according to CBS News, the BBC's US news partner.

"I'm like, 'Yeah, it's a human being. Stop the boat; let's get all of our lines in," Grant Garrett was quoted as saying, recalling the moment he and Sean Norton spotted him.

The man, who has not yet been identified, was taken to hospital in a stable condition, the US Coast Guard told the BBC, adding it was investigating the incident.

The fishermen say the man identified himself as a pianist on a 14-deck cruise ship called the Ovation of the Seas.

"The only thing he said to me was, 'Thank you. I thought I was gonna die,'" Norton said.

The Coast Guard said in a statement it had received a report from "a Good Samaritan who recovered a conscious individual from the water".

The person was handed over to Newport Beach Lifeguards, who confirmed he was an employee of the Ovation of the Seas cruise ship, it said.

Lifeguard officials believe he may have fallen overboard at around 02:30, about nine hours before he was discovered.

"It was approximately 15 to 20 miles offshore, pretty much in the middle of the ocean," Newport Beach Lifeguard Battalion Chief Adam Yacenda told CBS.

Norton credited the man's survival to unusually warm ocean temperatures.

"You have this crazy super El NiÃ±o year. This guy was in the water for probably 10 hours, but the water's 76 degrees," Norton said, which equates to about 24C.

"A different year, no way he makes it," Norton said.

The developing El NiÃ±o climate phenomenon is now the strongest on record, according to scientists monitoring the Pacific, with ocean temperatures about 3.05C above average.

In a statement to the BBC, a Royal Caribbean Group spokesperson said: "We are providing support and assistance to our crew member during this difficult time.

"To respect the privacy of our employee, we have no additional details to share."

Homes and temples glow across India to mark Diwali

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Millions across India are celebrating Diwali, the Hindu festival of lights, illuminating homes, temples, offices and streets with oil lamps and colourful lights to symbolise the triumph of light over darkness.

The festival falls on the darkest night of the Hindu lunar month of Kartik, when the moon is not visible in the sky.

Although Diwali traditions vary across India's regions and communities, the festival is widely marked by prayers, family gatherings, the exchange of sweets and the lighting of firecrackers.

But the celebrations come amid growing concerns over air pollution, particularly in northern India.

Seasonal weather conditions and the burning of crop residue contribute to deteriorating air quality, while firecrackers set off during Diwali can make the situation worse.

Air pollution in the Indian capital Delhi has reached such hazardous levels that it is the most polluted Diwali festival in a decade. New measures to improve air quality do not seem to have been successful.

India's financial capital, Mumbai, has also experienced high pollution levels in recent weeks.

In an effort to limit pollution, India's Supreme Court on Monday permitted the manufacture, sale and use of certified "green" firecrackers in Delhi and surrounding areas for a limited period.

These firecrackers are designed to produce 20-30% fewer emissions than conventional fireworks. But critics have questioned how effective they are at reducing pollution and raised concerns that conventional firecrackers could still make their way into the capital from neighbouring states.

Repeated violations of firecracker restrictions leave cities grappling with worsening post-Diwali smog

Despite the debate over fireworks, Diwali is about much more than firecrackers.

The festival celebrates the return of Lord Ram to Ayodhya after his victory over Ravan, the main antagonist in the Hindu epic Ramayana, and symbolises the triumph of good over evil, light over darkness, and knowledge over ignorance.

In the days leading up to the festival, families clean and decorate their homes with flowers, colourful lights and traditional oil lamps known as diyas.

The celebrations also bring families and friends together for prayers, elaborate feasts and the exchange of gifts, and lots of traditional Indian sweets.

Colourful patterns known as rangoli are created at entrances to homes, while markets bustle with shoppers buying festive essentials beneath brightly lit decorations.

Potters make and paint clay lamps, or diyas, that are lit during the festival

The celebrations began on 6 November with Dhanteras, when buying gold, silver or new utensils is considered auspicious and believed to bring good fortune.

The second day, known as Chhoti Diwali, or small Diwali, is marked by the lighting of oil lamps and, in some households, setting off firecrackers.

The third day is the main celebration, when families offer evening prayers to Lakshmi, the Hindu goddess of wealth and prosperity, before gathering for festive meals and fireworks.

Offered during prayers and shared among family and friends, sweets are an integral part of the celebrations

The fourth day is celebrated differently across India, with family gatherings and New Year festivities in some communities. The fifth and final day, known as Bhai Dooj, celebrates the bond between brothers and sisters.

The festival is also celebrated by Sikhs and Jains along with other important religious observances.

Sikhs mark Bandi Chhor Divas, or Prisoner Release Day, commemorating the release of their sixth guru, Guru Hargobind, from imprisonment at Gwalior Fort in the early 17th Century.

For Jains, Diwali marks the attainment of moksha, or liberation from the cycle of rebirth, by Lord Mahavira, the 24th and last Tirthankara, or spiritual teacher, of the current era.

With Indian communities spread across the world, Diwali is also celebrated internationally, with families and friends coming together for festive gatherings and exchanging gifts.

Parts of north India are covered with lights of every colour and shade

The festival is considered an auspicious time to make new purchases, which many believe bring prosperity and good fortune. India's stock exchanges even hold a special one-hour trading session for investors who consider buying and selling shares on Diwali favourable. Known as Muhurat trading, it will take place on 8 November this year.

Although rooted in Hindu traditions, Diwali is celebrated by people across different faiths and communities.

Pets and strays are honoured in Nepal during Diwali, where the festival is known as Tihar

Seven dead and more than 100 injured after trains collide in Egypt

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Footage shows derailed train carriages near Giza in Egypt

At least seven people have died in a train crash near the Egyptian city of Giza, with more than 100 injured.

The country's railway authority said two trains travelling south from Giza and Cairo collided, causing several carriages to derail and fall down an embankment.

Images from the scene showed people looking inside overturned carriages for injured passengers as a massive emergency response was launched.

Egyptian President Abdul Fattah al-Sisi said he had ordered state authorities to "investigate the circumstances of the accident, and hold accountable anyone found to have been negligent".

The public prosecutor's office warned the death toll could rise.

Uninjured passengers and bystanders searched derailed carriages for injured people

The government said hospitals in the area had been put "on heightened alert" as they dealt with a massive influx of patients.

Egypt's health minister Dr Khaled Abdel Ghaffar said people were being treated for broken bones and head injuries.

The Egyptian Red Crescent said it was providing aid and psychological support to victims and their families at the scene.

The driver of one of the trains was among those who died in the crash, the ministry confirmed.

The first train involved in the crash was travelling from Giza to Fayoum, south of Cairo, when it crashed into the rear of another service heading south from Cairo to Aswan, which was stationary on the tracks.

Images from the scene showed some carriages had overturned, with the body of at least one appearing to have split in two.

Trains between Giza and the Upper Egypt region were suspended as cranes were dispatched to remove the wrecked trains. The railway authority has not said how long it expects this to take.

The collision occurred in the area between Kafr Ammar and Al-Raqqa stations in Al-Ayyat, close to where Egypt's deadliest train tragedy occurred in February 2002, when a fire engulfed a passenger train travelling from Cairo to Luxor.

In that incident, more than 370 people were officially reported killed, although some estimates put the toll considerably higher.

Two further crashes in the area in 2009 killed dozens more.

There were calls in Egypt on Sunday for the transport minister to resign over the latest deadly rail disaster.

Foreign leaders and dignitaries have shared condolences and support for the victims.

Saudi Arabia's ministry of foreign affairs expressed "solidarity" with Egypt and wished those hurt a speedy recovery.

Turkey's ambassador to Egypt wrote on X that he extended "heartfelt condolences and sincere sympathy" to the victims, their families, and the Egyptian people.

Surgeon having lunch near Krakow shooting undoubtedly saved officer's life, colleague says

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Watch: Doctor who treated victims of Krakow shooting speaks to the BBC

One of two surgeons who treated victims on the streets in the aftermath of a mass shooting in Krakow, Poland said his colleague "undoubtedly" saved the life of a gravely injured police officer.

The pair were eating near the city's Main Market Square on Saturday when a gunman began firing at random, killing six people and injuring 15.

The doctors raced to treat both the attacker and two officers shot while subduing the perpetrator.

On Sunday, the officers - both hailed as "true heroes" by the prime minister for preventing further deaths - emerged from comas.

Prosecutors launched a terror investigation after ministers previously said the 24-year-old Polish attacker - who remains in a critical condition - was apparently inspired by "far-right ideologies".

A British national and Austrian woman were among those killed alongside four Poles.

Professor Michal PÄdziwiatr was having lunch with a colleague, named by local media as Dr Mateusz Wierdak, and their families in central Krakow when they heard gunshots.

PÄdziwiatr told the BBC: "We saw the shooting attack. And what a surgeon can do in a situation like this?... We decided to help the victims as soon as possible.

"We rushed into the area where the shooting was and started our rescue, because there were no ambulances available, there were no professionals around."

What we know about the deadly Krakow shooting

He said his colleague used his hand to stem the bleeding from the most injured policeman, allowing them to wait for an ambulance which transferred the officer to hospital.

PÄdziwiatr said they came to learn that one of those they treated was the apparent attacker.

"As a doctor... it doesn't matter to me if it's a victim or a suspect," he said.

"It's not our task to decide what to do with this guy... for us, it was another heavily injured victim, that's all."

According to local media, Wierdak is a renowned surgeon in Krakow. The University Hospital in Krakow states PÄdziwiatr is head of General, Oncological, Metabolic and Emergency Surgery.

The police officers have been identified as senior constables Jakub Kulik and Artur MoraÅski.

The attacker has not yet been named by authorities.

When his house was searched after the attack in central Krakow, the bodies of his parents and sister were found.

The National Public Prosecutor's Office in Poland launched an investigation into the attack with a unit specialising in "terrorist offences" taking the lead.

Separately, reports emerged that six Dublin Civil Defence volunteers also assisted in the aftermath of the shooting.

Investigations continued in central Krakow on Sunday before Main Market Square reopened

James McConnell, an officer, told RTE News the group were on a city break and were close to the main square, external when the shooting happened.

The volunteers who are trained to provide medical care immediately sought to help injured people, providing "CPR and haemorrhage control".

Witnesses who were close to the attack described panic and confusion around the square as it unfolded.

Ruby Catherine Warren, from London, heard gunfire and "yelled at my friends to run and we all sprinted for our lives".

Not knowing where the gunman was - or if there was more than one - they "just kept sprinting", eventually finding a hotel to hide inside.

How the deadly shooting in Krakow unfolded

Tom Brook, from West Yorkshire, was inside a McDonald's near the square when he heard "multiple gunshots and saw people running for cover".

"I then walked into the square and saw paramedics working on someone outside [a shop] and there was a dead body covered in foil blankets 20 meters away," he said.

The suspect wore full military tactical gear during the attack and was described as having a military-grade weapon - but the government said he was not a member of the Polish armed forces.

Poland's Interior Minister Marcin KierwiÅski said "everything suggests" the suspect was "fascinated by far-right ideologies" and held ultra-nationalist views.

The rampage began shortly before 15:00 local time (14:00 GMT) on Saturday in the historic centre of Krakow.

Footage showed the attacker's vehicle speeding through the centre of the city, and people fleeing as the sound of gunfire rang out.

The glass fronts of shops were shattered by bullets and a tram was shot at during the attack, which police say lasted for around three minutes.

The mayor of Krakow said he had submitted an urgent request for the two officers injured while stopping the attacker to be awarded titles of honorary citizens of the city.

Main Market Square reopened on Sunday evening after a security cordon was lifted.

9 Movies That Were Notoriously Difficult To Make

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Getting any movie made is a minor miracle. While the finished film only takes anywhere from 90-120 minutes to enjoy, years of hard work went into making that become a reality. It begins with the script, obviously, but production can be its own special nightmare, and then various decisions have to be made during post-production to put the film together just right. That’s not to mention the fact that, depending on how the film was produced or financed, you may have 5, 7, or 10 different people "in charge," each demanding their own tweaks, adjustments, and changes. So yeah, moviemaking is hard.

But some films are particularly challenging to put together, and their “making of” stories could result in compelling movies in and of themselves. Below, we go through nine different films that were notoriously difficult to get made. From fired actors to budget overruns to PCP doses (seriously), these nine films were really tough to create, but they all reached the finish line—for better or worse.

Alien 3 was not only David Fincher’s first feature film, it’s also one of the most important films in his entire career. Fincher’s experience making Alien 3 colored how he approached his work from then onward, resulting in a confident attitude that allowed him to craft some truly groundbreaking films within the studio system while refusing to compromise his vision.

Fincher was not the first director on Alien 3. Vincent Ward, the filmmaker behind The Navigator: A Medieval Odyssey, was the original director attached. His story took place on a wood-centric planet populated by Luddite-like monks, where Ripley’s escape pod crash lands and she becomes the only woman in the monastery. Development began, sets were starting to be assembled, but when Fox executives asked Ward to make significant changes, he balked and left the movie.

So Walter Hill and David Giler were brought on to refine the script while Fincher was tasked with replacing Ward as the director. The basic outline of Ward’s story remained, but the wood planet was turned into an ore refinery and the monks were turned into prisoners. Filming began, but two weeks in cinematographer Jordan Cronenweth—Fincher’s closest ally on the production team—had to depart the project due to Parkinson’s disease, so he was replaced by Alex Thomson.

As production wore on, Fincher started butting heads with producers and studio executives more and more. Not only had he inhabited someone else’s film that had already left the station, but the script was never finished. The notoriously meticulous Fincher was trying to get his shots and craft his film while the script was simultaneously being written (and rewritten) and studio executives and producers were looking over his shoulder, trying to make the film more in line with the previous two Alien movies.

The battles continued into post-production, during which reshoots were ordered and arguments heatedly revolved around the ending. Fincher would oversee the editing, but when he left the room, the film’s editor would recall that producers and/or studio executives would come in and force him to undo or ignore Fincher’s instructions. By the end of the project Fincher had little control left and all but disavowed the movie. The film was released to negative reviews and little fanfare, and years later Fincher would say, “No one hated it more than me; to this day, no one hates it more than me.”

The Bourne Identity is proof positive that difficulties during production, and even creative differences with the director, doesn’t always equal a bad movie. The spy thriller marked Go and Swingers filmmaker Doug Liman’s biggest film to date and was Matt Damon’s first foray with an “action hero” role, but the two had no interest in making your standard actioner. Indeed, Liman’s almost documentary-like approach to The Bourne Identity adds a visceral quality to the 2002 film that your standard, clean-shaven actioners lacked at that time.

But once filming commenced, Liman and Universal Pictures started butting heads. The studio was unhappy with the film’s pacing and smaller scale action set pieces, and Tony Gilroy and Liman were rewriting the script constantly, causing the film’s release date to push from September 2001 to June 2002. Things were so hectic that Matt Damon later revealed that producer Frank Marshall ended up directing some scenes himself, because it was all hands on deck. A major point of contention was the third act, specifically the sequence set at the farmhouse. Universal wanted it cut, but Liman and Damon argued it was crucial to understanding Bourne as a character, so it went through numerous rewrites and reworks to make both Liman and the studio happy.

All involved assumed the film would be a flop, but of course it wasn’t—it was a huge hit and spawned one of the best action franchises of the 21st century. Still, Liman’s relationship with the franchise and studio was broken, and he was not asked to return to direct the sequel. But his method of figuring out the film on the fly has become standard procedure for A Doug Liman Film, making some anxious but resulting in great movies like The Bourne Identity and Edge of Tomorrow.

Steven Spielberg is notorious for working quickly, knowing exactly what he wants, and sometimes releasing two masterpieces in the same calendar year. But the filmmaker was almost broken before his career got off the ground. At 26 years old, Spielberg lobbied for the gig to direct an adaptation of Peter Benchley’s novel Jaws. Having just worked with producers Richard D. Zanuck and David Brown on The Sugarland Express, they opted to take a shot on the up-and-comer, and thus one of the most troubled productions in history began.

Spielberg actually tried to leave Jaws just after signing his contract for fear of becoming typecast after Duel, but Universal exercised its right to veto Spielberg’s planned move over to 20th Century Fox’s Lucky Lady. Unhappy with Benchley’s own adaptation of the novel, Spielberg enlisted a slew of different writers to take a crack at the script, making the characters more likeable and adding more humor. Carl Gottleib was originally contracted to just do some punch-ups, but eventually became the primary screenwriter.

Filming began on a budget of $4 million that quickly ballooned up to $9 million. The reason? One of the biggest issues facing the production was Spielberg’s decision to shoot the film on actual open water rather than in a tank. The filmmaker didn’t realize how difficult this would be, nor was he expecting the mechanical shark—the title character in the film—to be mostly unusable. Indeed the shark, which was nicknamed Bruce, was originally intended to show up much earlier in the film. But since the prop didn’t work, Spielberg had to create suspense and terror in other ways. One fix was the yellow barrels, which ended up being one of the film’s most memorable sequences.

Shooting at sea caused delay after delay, as cast and crew members got sick, cameras malfunctioned, and resetting shots took hours. Spielberg went so far as to estimate that out of a 12-hour workday on Jaws, only an average of four hours were spent actually filming something. Originally scheduled for 55 days, principal photography droned on for 159 days in total, extremely over budget and over schedule. Spielberg was convinced he’d never work again. That the studio was furious with him. That he had ruined the movie. Instead, he ended up creating one of the best films of all time and singlehandedly crafting the first “summer blockbuster.” All’s well that ends well.

It’s not often that a film undergoes reshoots to make its third act smaller, but that’s exactly what happened with World War Z. The zombie actioner was directed by Marc Forster (Quantum of Solace) and intended as a franchise vehicle for star/producer Brad Pitt. But troubles plagued production as the script was never fully locked down and the aspects that drew Pitt to the project in the first place—the geopolitical angle of the story—fell away in favor of more action. As shot, the film originally ended with a major action set piece in Russia, where Pitt’s character was leading an army of human subservients who fight off a zombie invasion in the Red Square. This, uh, didn’t work.

During post-production, Pitt invited Damon Lindelof to watch a 72-minute edit of the movie. Lindelof pitched his take on a new ending, one that raised the dramatic stakes, made it more character-centric, but ditched the big action scenes that had already been shot. To their credit, Pitt, Paramount, and Forster said okay. They ditched the action-heavy ending for a quiet, horror-driven conclusion. Their big, climactic, Russia-set finale hit the cutting room floor.

Lindelof enlisted friend Drew Goddard and in the span of about 10 days, the two delivered around 60 pages of new material for the brand new ending. Christopher McQuarrie was then hired to remain on set and revise the script as needed as the new scenes were shot, and while Forster remained onboard as director, reports indicate he remained mostly quiet and let Pitt’s production company Plan B take the lead on bringing this thing home. And it worked! World War Z is proof positive that reshoots and troubled productions don’t always equal bad movies, and indeed the new third act is the best part of the entire film.

Never, ever bet against James Cameron. During the production of Titanic, reports swirled about the film’s cost overruns, production problems, and Cameron’s heated temper. Everyone earmarked Titanic as a herculean disaster. One of the most expensive flops in history. Then it became the highest grossing movie ever made.

Cameron already had a reputation as a man who doesn’t suffer fools, and indeed that personality was on full display during the arduous Titanic shoot. The film was shot in Nova Scotia in a giant water tank, but shooting in the water left many cast and crew members ill. Kate Winslet chipped her elbow and was afraid she would drown, and other people came down with kidney infections and colds from working in the water.

One infamous incident occurred when an unknown (and angry) crew member put PCP into the soup that Cameron and other crew members were to eat. 50 people were sent to the hospital, including Bill Paxton. And for what some categorized as a split personality, Cameron earned the nickname “Mij”, which is Jim spelled backwards.

Filming was scheduled to last 138 days but instead took 160, with costs soaring up to $200 million. Already plagued with bad buzz, Fox balked when Cameron turned in a three-hour cut of the film, fearful that the long runtime would mean fewer showings and thus less return on what they already saw as a bad investment. Cameron refused to cut the movie, telling Fox they’d have to fire him first. Instead, to ease fears, Cameron gave up his share of the profits—a gesture Fox initially felt was hollow given that they didn’t think the movie would make any money anyway. Obviously, they were very, very wrong. Titanic was a smashing success, winning 11 Oscars including Best Picture and Best Director. And so much for bad blood—Winslet re-teamed with Cameron on The Avatar Sequels.

While The Lone Ranger is on this list, we could just have easily have singled out director Gore Verbinski’s Pirates of the Caribbean sequels Dead Man’s Chest and At World’s End, which he shot back-to-back in one fell swoop and which led to an exhausting, frenzied race to the finish line. But Verbinski’s live-action follow-up is even more fascinating, as The Lone Ranger nearly got the plug pulled before cameras rolled.

After venturing into the world of animation for Rango in 2011, which netted Verbinski an Academy Award for Best Animated Feature, the filmmaker opted to return to the world of epic Disney blockbusters with a wholly unique spin on The Lone Ranger. Intended to start a new franchise, Verbinski signed on to direct with Johnny Depp in the lead role as Tonto and Armie Hammer playing a sidelined Lone Ranger, and Jack White providing the original score.

But as development progressed, the budget ballooned to over $250 million, and in August 2011 the entire movie was shut down. At the time, Disney had the expensive John Carter and Oz the Great and Powerful in the can and Hollywood had just pulled the plug on At the Mountains of Madness and The Dark Tower over similar cost overruns. So Verbinski and the team came together and worked to get the budget down to $215 million, which they did by cutting action sequences and their own upfront fees, opting instead to take money on the backend.

Then filming began and, wouldn’t you know it, the budget ballooned back up to a reported $250 million. Verbinski was once again asked to trim the script, but the film was also falling behind schedule due to weather delays. And when Alan Horn replaced Rich Ross as chariman of the Walt Disney Studios, he requested the trimming of a scene in which William Fichnter’s character eats the heart of James Badge Dale. Oh but it got weirder—rumors swirled that the original script for the film included werewolves, which were cut over the initial budget concerns.

By some miracle, The Lone Ranger actually made it to the finish line, but the disappointing $260 million box office didn’t even match how much Disney had already spent on the film. Reviews were pretty scathing, but in recent years folks have come around a bit and embraced Verbinski’s delightfully strange vision. Even so, The Lone Ranger remains one of the most egregious examples of a troubled production in this century.

You know it’s a tough shoot when your lead actor literally has a heart attack in the middle of production. Indeed, principal photography for Apocalypse Now spanned a full year, beginning on in May 1976 and ending in May 1977. A typhoon wrecked the sets, ruining a month of shooting and forcing much of the cast and crew to return to the U.S. while new locations were scouted and new sets were built. As cost overruns began, director Francis Ford Coppola had to start offering up his own assets to bankroll the film, including his profits from The Godfather.

And then there was the cast. Coppola had to work hard to convince Marlon Brando to take the pivotal role of Colonel Kurtz, but Brando arrived on the set overweight, having not read the source material Heart of Darkness, and having not learned his lines. Coppola worked around Brando’s weight by shooting him in shadow and darkness. Then Martin Sheen had a heart attack in the middle of filming, took a month off, and returned to resume his duties. Sheen was also battling alcoholism, and indeed in one of the film’s early scenes of Willard alone and drunk in his room, Sheen himself was actually drunk and really cut his hand during the take.

Coppola would go on to battle further issues in post-production, including plenty of problems with the film’s audio. Upon release, Apocalypse Now received somewhat mixed reviews but nevertheless won the Palme d’Or at the Cannes Film Festival and notched eight Oscar nominations and two wins for Best Cinematography and Best Sound. Again, all’s well that ends well, but the herculean production of Apocalypse Now has taken on a myth of its own, and is captured in full in the harrowing behind-the-scenes documentary Hearts of Darkness.

A reboot of Fantastic Four was in the offing at 20th Century Fox pretty shortly after the Tim Story­-directed sequel Rise of the Silver Surfer, largely because if Fox didn’t make a Fantastic Four movie every few years, the rights would revert back to Marvel. Emboldened by the impending X-Men: Days of Future Past and a third Wolverine movie on the way (which became Logan), the studio enlisted Chronicle director Josh Trank to spearhead a new, darker take on the comics characters.

Development and casting seemingly went off without a hitch, beyond the ridiculous backlash to the race-swapped Johnny Storm with Michael B. Jordan playing the comics character. But rumors started swirling about troubles behind the scenes, with producers Simon Kinberg and Hutch Parker reportedly reworking the ending of the film. Reshoots commenced in January 2015, five months after principal photography wrapped, and reports surfaced that Fox was unhappy with Trank’s original cut with other sources claiming that Trank’s behavior was “erratic” on set.

Trank was removed as the director of an unspecified Star Wars anthology film in the wake of these reports and just before Fantastic Four’s release, Trank posted in a since-deleted Twitter message that a year ago he had “a fantastic version” of the movie that audiences would probably never see. Toby Kebbell, who played Doctor Doom, subsequently said Trank’s version was “great” and much darker than the final version of the movie.

When Fantastic Four eventually was released, the reshot scenes were made incredibly noticeable not just because of their inconsistent tone but because of Kate Mara’s super unconvincing wig. There were also plenty of shots from trailers and B-roll that revealed scenes that never made it to the finished cut, including a subplot involving the Fantasticar. Reviews for the film were unkind, but the cast seemed to get along quite well and Kinberg expressed a desire to keep this rebooted franchise alive before the First Family eventually moved to the MCU.

Recasting isn’t an altogether unique thing that happens in Hollywood, but it is rare. One of the most famous examples is surely Back to the Future. The project itself took some time getting off the ground, as Robert Zemeckis and Bob Gale’s sci-fi script was turned down by studios for four years straight, with Zemeckis and Gale continuing to hone the story the whole time. They were told the film was too tame by most studios and not family friendly enough by Disney (re: a mother falling in love with her son). Steven Spielberg eventually set the project up at his studio Amblin Entertainment, and they finally found a home with Universal Pictures.

Michael J. Fox was the first choice to play Marty McFly, but his commitment to the TV series Family Ties made the prospect of him doing the film impossible. Thus, the producers settled on Eric Stoltz based on the strength of his performance in Mask. But after an entire month of filming with Stoltz, Zemeckis finally admitted the film was not working. Stoltz would have to be fired. Although it added more to the budget, Zemeckis and Spielberg agreed to recast the lead role and reshoot most of the movie, and by this time Fox’s schedule had opened up just enough so that he could shoot both Family Ties and Back to the Future.

A deal ensued that Fox’s main priority would be to Family Ties, so if a conflict arose, the TV series would win out. Fox would shoot Family Ties on weekdays during the day, Back to the Future from 6:30pm to 2:30am, and then shot exterior scenes for Back to the Future on the weekends. It was no doubt an exhaustive experience, but one that paid off in spades. Back to the Future was a smashing success, spending 11 weeks in the number on box office slot and earning an Oscar nomination for Best Original Screenplay. It also, obviously, spawned a franchise, with Zemeckis shooting both sequels back to back—but this time he had his lead actor right from the start.

What’s Fogging Your Brain? Signs of Aging vs. ADHD

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Cognitive changes may stem from perimenopause, ADHD, or early dementia, each of which requires distinct treatment.

Q: How do I know whether hormonal changes related to perimenopause are causing my cognitive function problems, or my ADHD is getting worse, or I might be experiencing mild cognitive impairment or early dementia?

ADHD and mild cognitive impairment (MCI) exhibit clinically different cognitive deficits that help me differentiate one from the other. While ADHD symptoms involve forgetfulness, inattention, and distractibility, MCI has specific cognitive symptoms of word-finding challenges, misspellings, and the use of generic terms like “stuff” and “things” because of word-finding difficulty. Also, MCI is progressive over two to three years, whereas ADHD doesn’t generally worsen.

In perimenopause, a decline in estrogen leads to cognitive difficulties. In contrast to MCI, we know that the cognitive changes from estrogen decline are not the result of neurodegeneration.

However, the specific deficits related to declining estrogen haven’t been well differentiated from those associated with ADHD. So if you have pre-existing ADHD and you experience heightened cognitive difficulties in perimenopause and menopause, you may think, “My ADHD is getting worse.” But it’s likely not worsening ADHD. It’s declining estrogen.

Okay, so what do you do about it? There is a paucity of research for clinical guidance. Hormone replacement therapy (HRT) was not always recommended because it was alleged to increase risk for breast cancer and other illnesses. That claim has now been refuted by the most recent research, which has led to the removal of the black box warning from the FDA on HRT product labels. And so, if I have a female patient with ADHD who is 50 years old and says, “For the past two years, I’ve had cognitive decline,” the question to consider is this: “Do I increase her stimulant medication or do I put her on HRT?”

I am inclined at that point to put her on HRT for three months, keep her stimulant medication dosage stable, and monitor the qualitative changes in her cognitive functioning. If there is improvement on HRT, then the question becomes, “Does she stay on HRT for some extended period of time along with the stimulant medication?” If the HRT doesn’t work or she can’t take HRT for medical reasons, then I might increase the stimulant medication dosage to see if we can’t overcome the perimenopausal changes from the estrogen decline. This advice comes from my clinical experience, and it is unfortunate that we don’t have more research for clinical guidance here.

[Take This Self-Test: Is It Perimenopause or ADHD?]

Q: Could ADHD stimulant medication mask symptoms of mild cognitive impairment?

There is very limited research on stimulants and mild cognitive impairment (MCI). My clinical impression is that stimulants likely improve symptoms of MCI, but do not take that as an endorsement to seek treatment for MCI with stimulants. There is emerging research on MCI and stimulants that has not yet been published. The follow-up question to that is, if you have ADHD and MCI, will treating the ADHD delay the development of MCI or Alzheimer’s? There is no clinical research for me to answer that question. But if this were me, I’d be treating my ADHD, keeping my fingers crossed, and appreciating the fact that the quality of my life on medicine is better than it would be without my ADHD treated.

ADHD should not get worse; however, the experience of ADHD may be worse if life’s demands start to increase and exceed your ability to compensate. However, if you’re having cognitive changes that are beyond the scope of your ADHD, you may have two neurologic processes going on: your ADHD and something else. In this case, your ADHD may, indeed, be getting worse because you have a second neurological process that needs to be evaluated.

David W. Goodman, M.D., is an internationally recognized expert on adult ADHD.

Publishers: Agentic AI Isn’t About Pricing, But Whether You Get Any Bids At All

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The agentic skeptics have the better argument right now.

Anthony Katsur, who runs IAB Tech Lab, expects “several false starts” and years of experimentation and alignment across platforms, agencies and publishers. Razorfish called the mood around agentic AI inevitable – but incremental – at CES back in January.

And Digiday’s research in January found that 54% of 142 brand and agency professionals say their companies don’t use agentic AI in their workflows at all. That is the buy side reporting on itself.

Anyone telling publishers they are already behind on agentic AI is selling something.

However, there’s a fundamental difference between agentic AI and the “inevitable” industry shifts that came before it. Each of those shifts required somebody to build and pay for new integration, and the parties who had to pay disagreed about what to build. The variable is sunk integration cost.

Agentic is the first shift where buyers already paid that bill.

Agentic AI doesn’t face the same barriers. Fully autonomous media buying may still be years away, but AI agents don’t need new pipes or industrywide standards to start evaluating inventory, informing bidding decisions and optimizing spend. They can operate through existing programmatic infrastructure, using integrations the buy side has already built and paid for.

On April 8, 2026, Microsoft ended a relationship with Dentsu that ran for more than a decade and moved its global media planning and buying to Publicis. That mandate carried a price tag the trade press puts between $700 million and $1.2 billion. The media appointment sat inside a larger announcement about embedding agentic AI across the marketing workflow: agents on Microsoft Fabric, informed by Epsilon, deciding which segments matter and deploying media against them.

Asked why Publicis keeps winning without pitches, Arthur Sadoun said clients now understand “it’s about identity.” Publicis also agreed in May 2026 to acquire LiveRamp, a deal the FTC cleared in October subject to a 5-year neutrality order. Once it closes, one of the two largest buyers of publisher inventory will own more of the identity layer that its own agents reason over.

Those agents buy media. Media clears in auctions. Publishers get paid in those auctions.

The industry’s experience with bid shading shows how quickly new buying logic can reshape auctions.

Exchanges began moving to first-price auctions in 2017. Google announced Ad Manager’s move to first-price in March 2019 and finished four months later. Bid shading, a strategy for avoiding paying too much in first-price auctions, spread across SSPs and then DSPs within months via existing integrations, without new infrastructure or industrywide standards.

Publishers, meanwhile, were left to understand bid shading’s impact through their clearing-price reports and adjust their pricing floors accordingly.

Call bid shading machine learning rather than agentic if you prefer. But what’s different this time with agentic is what the logic decides. Shading optimized the price of a bid you were already making. But identity-informed agents decide what inventory is worth a bid at all.

The distinction matters. Publishers risk losing opportunities before their inventory even reaches an auction.

Think of agentic logic as being divided into two halves. IAB research underscores the divide between AI-powered decision-making and autonomous execution. Among 360 digital video buyers surveyed this February and March, 96% see a role for agentic AI in programmatic and RTB, and 63% expect that role to be an optimization layer that improves bidding.

But current adoption, in IAB’s own words, is centered on decision support rather than the purchase decision itself. AI-based deal execution and negotiation for video campaigns sits at 28%. Forty percent of buyers require human approval before an agent acts. That is the shape of it: Agents run freely where no money is committed and get gated where it is.

When Katsur predicts those agentic false starts, he is describing the execution half, and he is right about it.

But the decision-making half is already gaining traction. Among video buyers spending $50 million or more a year, 63% are using or planning to use agents for inventory discovery and evaluation, against 30% of smaller buyers. The largest budgets in the market are pointing agents at deciding which inventory is worth considering.

The exposure is not whether agents will pay a premium for your inventory. It is whether your inventory survives evaluation. Fail that, and you will not be outbid in some future auction. You will not be in the running at all.

Which means the work is the same work it has always been, and it pays now. Publishers don’t need to wait for agentic standards to mature to prepare. Much of the necessary groundwork can also improve performance in today’s auctions.

Here are three steps to take, none of which require knowing which standard wins:

These steps can strengthen inventory evaluation and match rates today, regardless of how quickly autonomous buying develops.

Remember: In 2019, the publishers who understood their own floors before the auction changed did fine. The ones who only learned about the change in buy-side decisioning from their revenue reports did not.

With agentic AI, waiting for fully autonomous transactions could mean missing the more consequential shift already underway.

“The Sell Sider” is a column written by the sell side of the digital media community.

Why the midterms may not spell doom for ad tech consolidation. Plus, how sports leagues are putting ad revenue before player safety.

Agentic media buying is the No. 1 topic du jour in ad tech discussion circles. But how does agentic buying even work when it comes to buying TV and streaming ads? And how does that workflow differ from programmatic? The startup Swivel aims to address those questions.

AppLovin is growing at a blistering pace, but Wall Street investors remain cautious about the business.

High-end ad blockers mimic some of the same behavior as bot traffic. So WiseGuyGaming repurposed the tools it was already using to identify bots to reveal its ad-blocking audience as well.

Criteo has weathered considerable storms in its day, including a painful moment in March 2020 when its market cap bottomed out below $500 million. Can it pull off yet another turnaround?

Disaffection and outright anger toward US sports institutions run deeper than logo-cluttered fields. Leagues and teams are sacrificing player safety and fan enjoyment for marketing dollars.

6 Movies From the Year 2020 That Are 10/10 Masterpieces

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

Back in 2020, when lockdown shrank life down to repetitive loops like scrolling through a phone half-asleep, reheating tea for the third time, and losing track of what day it was, movies became an anchor. A handful of films stood out because of the vivid moments they left behind. A scene of someone running through the woods, a quiet game of cards with a grandmother, or an entire room belting out a song brought a sense of life back into the room long after the credits rolled.

That is what great cinema does when everything else is on pause. It stays alive in the smaller moments, like a look across a dinner table, a track to hunt down on Spotify, or a decision that feels open to debate forever. Those lasting details are what set this ranking. The number one pick in this list is simply the movie worth putting on again tonight, even with the ending spoiled by memory.

In Wolfwalkers Robyn (Honor Kneafsey) begins convinced she can do her father’s (Sean Bean) work by carrying a crossbow into the woods to kill wolves. The Lord Protector (Simon McBurney) keeps the town behind its walls, and Robyn’s father has been hired to hunt the animals outside them. Then she meets Mebh (Eva Whittaker), a girl who runs barefoot through the forest and becomes a wolf while asleep. Their friendship is not instant softness. Mebh is furious, suspicious, and busy trying to find her mother, Moll MacTíre (Maria Doyle Kennedy), who is trapped in wolf form.

Robyn’s choices become harder in specific ways as she must hide Mebh, question what her father has told her, and face the fact that the hunt threatens the people she wants to protect. The animation makes the town’s straight streets and the forest’s movement feel different to watch, but the most memorable moment is plain. A child sees a frightened mother in a cage and knows the adults are wrong. The rescue gives Robyn courage. But, it does not make her suddenly fearless.

At first, Jake’s (Jesse Plemons) girlfriend (Jessie Buckley) is rehearsing a breakup in her own head while Jake drives her through snow to meet his parents. It is already an unbearable car ride because he keeps talking, changes the radio, and misses her cues while she cannot decide what she means when she imagines ending things. At the farm, his mother (Toni Collette) and father (David Thewlis) age and change between visits, and dinner becomes a test of whether she belongs there. She notices the basement door and does not go down. Later, she does.

I’m Thinking of Ending Things refuses to let anyone settle on who this woman is or what happened. At the high school, the janitor (Guy Boyd) cleans hallways and watches a student production of Oklahoma!, while the film ties him to an older version of Jake. At the barn, they find a pig covered in maggots. Anyone watching leaves wanting to rewind the opening just to hear how much their car conversation has already told them. Even the small changes in her name feel like clues missed while listening to Jake.

Small details stand out the most, such as Soon-ja teaching David Go-Stop, their walk down to the creek where she plants minari, and his resentment giving way to affection. When her stroke leaves her confused, she accidentally starts a fire that destroys Jacob’s crop storage. Nobody has the energy for a neat apology. Later, David runs after her when she tries to leave and guides her back home. Minari lets the family be tired, proud, frightened, and loving in the same scene, making every change in David’s face feel believable.

The Father keeps changing the room while Anthony (Anthony Hopkins) insists that the space is familiar. A daughter named Anne (Olivia Colman) says she is moving to Paris, but later, a man named Paul (Rufus Sewell), whom Anthony does not recognize, is living in the flat and says he is Anne’s husband. Anthony searches for his watch, questions the people caring for him, and tries to catch the lie that will explain why his life no longer adds up. That disorientation surfaces through the smallest interruptions, like a face replaced, furniture shifted, or a conversation repeated with one detail changed.

Anthony reacts with sharp irritation when people correct him, shifts to charm when he thinks he holds the upper hand, and collapses into terror when he cannot place his own daughter. Right before the credits roll, he cries for his mother. He asks to go home. Unraveling which version of the flat is real no longer matters. Someone just needs to stay with him. Someone needs to answer gently.

Before Martha (Amarah-Jae St. Aubyn) arrives, people are carrying chairs, choosing records, cooking, and doing each other’s hair. The room fills. Couples dance close, friends shout across the music, and plates get passed along. Franklyn (Micheal Ward) notices Martha, and they flirt and dance, then slip away briefly from the crowd.

The most memorable moment centers on Janet Kay’s Silly Games. The DJ lets the song play, then the crowd sings so loudly that the track almost disappears. People who have been dancing in pairs now sing together, still shoulder to shoulder, and nobody rushes them to move on. That is the pleasure of Lovers Rock, where a few hours feel important because the people in the room make them so. When Bammy (Daniel Francis-Swaby) sexually assaults Cynthia (Ellis George) in another room, the evening changes, and the others respond with anger and care. The film does not pretend the party makes the assault less serious. Standing at the edge of that room for just one song would be enough.

Nomadland follows Fern (Frances McDormand), whose home is a van, and she has learned the small maintenance tasks that keep it usable, such as finding work, parking overnight, keeping her belongings in order, and deciding what she can carry. After the gypsum plant closes in Empire, Nevada, she takes seasonal work at an Amazon warehouse and meets fellow travelers Linda May (Linda May) and Swankie (Charlene Swankie).

The film does not treat every day as freedom. Fern’s hands are tired after work, she has to find a bathroom, fix what breaks, and decide whether to accept Dave’s (David Strathairn) invitation into his family life. When she visits his son, the baby is there, the house is warm, and Fern could stay. However, she leaves. Later, she returns to Empire and walks through the empty house where she lived with Bo before going back to her van. That choice hurts because Dave is asking her into a real family, and she cares about him. Fern’s life is hers, and the film lets anyone feel what it costs her to keep choosing it.

Steve Herbert: Just when we thought it couldn't get any worse, Crawley Town hit a new low

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The most embarrassing performance and result since we became a football league club in 2011.

As a football club we have hit a new low, when we already thought sitting bottom of the entire football league couldn’t get any worse, we end up being out fought and outplayed by a team who plays at the same level as Three Bridges.

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

Let that sink in for a minute… a team who plays in a stadium not much different to Jubilee Field has just knocked us out of the FA Cup first round in front of a national television audience.

Losing last season at Boreham Wood at the same stage was bad, but this is next level stuff.

Only this club could produce a result like this one. Four hundred and seventy six Reds fans gave up a whole Remembrance Sunday to attend this horror show in Dorset. Wimborne is probably one of the trickiest places to get too, especially by public transport. It’s quicker to get to Manchester and Liverpool by train than it is to get to the South West. Yet we take the largest away following of the season, and the players let us down like that. Absolutely disgusting.

After talking up all the positivity of the last two league results against Tranmere & Oldham, it’s such a shame that the team have let Kevin Blackwell down so spectacularly. All I can say is thank god the Rotherham game has been postponed. At least it saves us another wasteful day out.

Where do we go from here? Who knows. All I know is if I was one of those players who have let the club down massively and embarrassed every fan who attended and those that watched at home, then a reaction needs to be seen at home to Fleetwood Town in the shape of a performance and a win.

French team: Zinedine Zidane reacts to the tragic death of Jesús de Miguel

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

On the sidelines of the announcement of his second list at the head of the Blues, Zinédine Zidane, who also summoned Pablo Pagis for the first time, was also questioned about the tragic death of the Spanish striker Jesús de Miguel, victim of a violent shock on a football field.

“I don’t want to get into that, I’m sad and extremely touched, it gives me goosebumps, we don’t want to see that on a football field, that’s just the message I want to give. I’m especially sad for the family, we don’t want to see that. I hope we never see that again.”he said. As a reminder, CD Tenerife announced this Tuesday the death of Jesús de Miguel, victim of cardiorespiratory arrest after a violent shock during the match against Andorra.

Pokémon Debuts New Charizard Design Perfect For Kanto Fans

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Pokémon has its fair share of fan-favorites that just about everyone seems to love. This includes Pikachu and the Eeveelutions, but it also includes Charmander's final evolution, Charizard. From Ash's Charizard in the anime to one of the most valuable Pokémon cards of all time, Charizard has made a name for itself as one of the most popular Kanto Pokémon. And over the years, we've seen Pokémon shake up Charizard's design in new and interesting ways.

It also happens to be Pokémon's 30th anniversary this year, which means Charizard and a lot of other Gen 1 Pokémon are being featured in new collabs and on different kinds of merchandise. It's not just merchandise, though, as we've seen FIreRed and LeafGreen release on Switch and Switch 2, and a number of Kanto Pokémon heavily promoted in Pokémon Champions and Pokémon GO throughout 2026.

From two Mega Evolutions to a Shiny variant and more, there are lots of Charizard designs out there. Now, fans have a chance to show off their love for Charizard just in time for the holiday season.

Available at Pokémon Center, "Charizard Red & Green Cardigan" offers up a brand-new design for the beloved Fire-type from the Gen 1 games. This is a part of Pokémon Center's exclusive new holiday collection, which is why there's a red and green scheme here. In addition to having a large Charizard on the back of the sweater, there's also a patch featuring Charizard's head on the front, so both sides of the cardigan represent the classic Pokémon.

The Charizard Red & Green Cardigan is available in adult sizes from XS to 4XL, and none of those sizes are sold out at the time of this writing. If you're looking to represent Charizard this holiday season, you'll need to shell out $59.99 (plus tax) for this item. However, Pokémon Center offers free shipping on orders of $20 or more, so you won't have to worry about paying anything extra on that front. The site has been closed down for maintence for a week, though, so it will hopefully be restored soon.

This Charizard cardigan is just one of many items released on Pokémon Center for Holiday 2026. The current collection is offering up everything from an adorable Espeon ornament to holiday-themed puzzles featuring fan-favorites like Mew and Pikachu. Some items from previous years' holiday collections are also available, and this includes past plushes, snow globes, and winter village set pieces. There's also a new Mew tree-topper being offered this year, which looks pretty awesome.

If you're looking to get this Charizard cardigan, or any of the other holiday items, you'll need to head over to Pokémon Center, since this merchandise is exclusive to that official storefront. Considering how popular Charizard is, it's great to see it getting a cool new design for the holidays.

If you're a fan of Kanto (and, really, who isn't?), then be sure to check out the other Gen 1 Pokémon getting their own sweaters this year. And if you prefer other Pokémon games, then fan-favorites from later generations, like Espeon and Munchlax, are also featured on quite a few items.

Pokémon is a Japanese multimedia franchise created by Satoshi Tajiri and Ken Sugimori, launched by Nintendo, Game Freak, and Creatures in 1996. Centered on fictional creatures called Pokémon, the series spans video games, trading cards, animation, films, merchandise, and competitive events. Players assume the role of Trainers who capture, battle, and trade Pokémon. It is one of the highest-grossing entertainment franchises in history.

Man United latest: Michael Carrick responds to uncertain future after unbelievable comeback

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Manchester United 4-4 Aston Villa: Carrick’s side twice fell apart, either side of another comeback, and will spend the international break 19th

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Michael Carrick said he still expects to be working with Manchester United over the international break and insisted he will not walk away after his players scored two stoppage-time goals to rescue an extraordinary 4-4 draw against Aston Villa in the Premier League.

The visitors appeared on course for a comfortable win after restoring their two-goal advantage in a crazy game Old Trafford, but late goals from Joshua Zirkzee and Patrick Dorgu saw United pull off their second great escape of the week after Tuesday’s incredible comeback victory against Roma.

The result still leaves United in the Premier League’s bottom three, however, and they will spend the international break 18th in the table before visiting rivals Liverpool. United have won just once in their first 10 in the Premier League, but Carrick was still applauded off the pitch by the United fans.

“We haven't won enough games, but I expect to move forward and keep working on things that we've been working on and showing a lot of the good in the game today,” Carrick said. “We need to tighten up on a few mistakes and win football matches.”

Villa boss Unai Emery was hugely frustrated to see his side twice throw away a two-goal lead, and stormed down the tunnel before full-time after Dorgu’s equaliser. Villa were unhappy that United goalkeeper Senne Lammens appeared to prevent Zion Suzuki from catching the ball before Dorgu headed in.

Follow reaction from Manchester United v Aston Villa in the Premier League, below

Belief there could be a comeback like against Roma did not last long. Diogo Dalot’s sloppy challenge on Alysson gave Villa a penalty that John McGinn coolly slotted home three minutes into the second half.

Zion Suzuki brilliantly stopped a curling Cunha effort shortly after a mooted walkout failed to materialise, with Fernandes twice trying his luck as Kobbie Mainoo and Amad Diallo threatened.

Amad continued to prove a nuisance and United’s hard work was rewarded just before half-time.

Tielemans’ low strike from just inside the box beat Suzuki, whose appeals that his view was impeded by Bryan Mbeumo were immaterial as the forward was onside.

But then Tuesday’s matchwinner Matheus Cunha to draw a foul from Boubacar Kamara in the box. Bruno Fernandes scored after the VAR ratified the on-field call, with the 16th-minute penalty sparking the best Premier League display from United since their solitary domestic win back in August.

The mood darkened further in the fourth minute. Manchester United failed to deal with a free-kick and Alysson’s strike was spilled by Senne Lammens, with Nicolas Jackson sweeping the ball into an empty net. “You’re not fit to wear the shirt” bellowed home fans as Villa teased Carrick about his job prospects.

The change Manchester United fans really want goes far beyond the man in the dugout, as highlighted by the green and gold balloons that filled the air around a banner that read ‘we don’t want a new stadium, we want new owners’.

The furious chants against the Glazers, Ratcliffe and director of football Jason Wilcox went up a notch after Unai Emery’s men capitalised on hodgepodge defending in the first minute.

The ball was played wide for Alysson and Dalot allowed him space to hit a first-time strike through Senne Lammens’ legs.

Manchester United have now gone five consecutive games where they have gone 2-0 down. This was the second time, after Roma in midweek, where they avoided defeat.

Manchester United will visit Liverpool in their first game after the international break and will do so while sitting in the Premier League’s bottom three.

They then play Brentford at home, Newcastle away, Coventry at home and Crystal Palace away before taking on Arsenal at the Emirates in their last game before Christmas.

United did manage to win at Anfield last season...

Manchester United are now guaranteed to spend the next 15 days in the Premier League relegation zone.

According to Opta, it’s the longest run of days in the bottom three since September-October 1986.

Asked whether United are in a relegation fight this season, Michael Carrick said: “We’re in November.

“I don’t like this situation where we are and where we’re at. But we can do something about it."

Manchester United have now conceded 30 goals in all competitions this season. According to Opta, no other side in Europe’s top-five league have conceded more.

On his side’s defensive record, Carrick said: “It’s just moments that we need to tighten up on. Individual moments where we’re giving teams goals that we don’t normally get. If we tighten up that, there’s a lot of good in that performance. I thought we should have won the game. But conceding the gaols we are is not helping us at all.

“No goals are good goals to concede. But there are ones you can do a bit more about. There are the ones we gave away today. We have got to tighten up, it’s pretty obvious to say that. It’s giving us too much to do in games. But I thought we did a lot and feel that we should have won the game."

Roy Keane felt Manchester United deserved credit for fighting back to claim a point against Aston Villa but lambasted their defending.

Speaking on Sky Sports, former United midfielder Keane said: “I think we’ve said previously there have been question marks over this team but they’re showing a bit of fight, a bit of desire and, as bad as the game was from a footballing point of view, they kept going to the end.

“So, on the one hand, you say dreadful to give up four goals at home, but they kept going, so we have to give them a little bit of praise.

“In terms of the bigger picture, it’s that rollercoaster of watching United. Some bits were so, so bad. Defensively the way it started, the start of the second half, but they found a way to get a point in a game where they looked dead and buried.

“Still nowhere near good enough to try and get towards the middle of the table.”

Reset your joints and build a more mobile body in 5 moves

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A mobility flow means piecing exercises together so that they form a sequence. This allows you to find more of a flow state, like in yoga or Pilates. It feels rhythmic and calming and can help you tune into your breathing, which is great for resetting your nervous system.

It's also one of my favorite ways to make flexibility and movement feel more inviting, and this five-move routine will help you feel more open, strong, and mobile in your shoulders, hips, spine, back, and hamstrings. You only have five exercises to learn, and you can do them back-to-back in the sequence below.

Grab one of the best yoga mats and get started.

Moving in one long flow helps you work with your breath and feel more like a dance, letting you switch off and enjoy how your body feels as it moves. It should also help reset your joints and spine and find better flexibility in your shoulders and hamstrings.

Perform several rounds, sticking to 6-8 reps per move per side to keep things dynamic.

During each exercise, think to yourself: "Am I using my end range of motion, or could I move more deeply into each position?" If you repeat for several rounds, pause at the top and bottom of each pose and reach deeply into your joints and muscles as you warm up.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

I want to highlight two exercises in particular.

Cat-cow is typically used as a spinal mobilizer, and it helps open the front and back body; the back body stretches during "cat" (upper back rounding), and the front body stretches during "cow" (stomach dropping toward the mat).

Downward dog stretches the arms, shoulders, chest and upper back, opening the upper body as you press through your hands and lift your hips up and back. As you draw your heels down, notice a stretch along the lower back, glutes, hamstrings and calves. Press through your hands to help activate your shoulders, then lift the hips as you move your heels.

Take your time and try to enjoy it, and of course, let us know how you get on!

During each exercise, think to yourself: 'Am I using my end range of motion, or could I move more deeply into each position?'
