WTF is the AI training opt-out policy token?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is a WTF explainer, in which we break down media and marketing’s most confusing terms. More from the series →

Some of the largest AI tech companies — including Anthropic, Google, Microsoft and OpenAI — have agreed to support a new mechanism that lets publishers opt out of having their content used to train AI models, in just a few lines of text.

The proposed standard, called a “policy token” for AI training, comes as publishers seek more control over how tech companies scrape and use their content. They’ve tried blocking AI bots, pursuing legal action and negotiating licensing deals.

At first glance, this AI training policy token appears to offer a straightforward fix. In reality, it highlights why the problem is so complicated.

The idea is that publishers can add a line to their robots.txt file — which websites use to tell web crawlers what content they are allowed to access — to express whether they want their content used for AI training.

Here’s what the policy token looks like, as written in robots.txt: User-Agent: __usage_ai-training

Below that, publishers can write “disallow” or “allow,” to show which crawlers can scrape and which can’t. In the “allow” line, publishers can also offer certain exceptions, such as letting crawlers scrape a press kit.

Typically, publishers need to list each crawler individually in robots.txt and give permission (or not) to crawl their sites. The new AI training policy token would allow publishers to do so in one step. Instead of managing separate instructions for every crawler, they can set broad preferences to participating AI and tech companies.

The proposal was written by Kevin Kelley, a technical lead for standards at Anthropic. Kelley submitted the proposal to the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF), an internet standards development organization, on Oct. 30.

Technically, the standard is not official yet. The IETF launched an effort known as AI Preferences (AIPREF) to develop new standards for website owners to express preferences about how their content gets used for AI. Anthropic, Google, Microsoft and OpenAI have all been involved in the group.

While the largest search and AI providers have agreed to support this policy token, it still needs to be deliberated on by the IETF. And the fact that these major companies have jumped to support this draft raises some concerns, said Alex Springer, technical lead at SPUR, a coalition of publishers working to develop AI standards.

“The IETF exists to allow for individuals to work in public to form consensus about the standards and protocols that make the internet work. The way that the platforms have approached this by announcing their unilateral support of a ‘standard’ that is an unreviewed draft is the antithesis of that,” he said. “They appear to have decided to unilaterally wield their collective weight in a concerted effort to force a new de facto standard in place, one that falls far short of what those content owners require.”

Anthropic says it is rolling out adoption of the signal now.

In a blog post, OpenAI said it would honor the policy token “in the coming months.” It noted that the proposed mechanism would not replace its existing crawler controls.

Google wrote in its own blog post that it aims to support this policy token in 2027. It noted that it builds on the options it already offers publishers, such as Google-Extended, a crawler that publishers can block in robots.txt to control AI training access, implemented in 2023.

Microsoft went a bit further, writing in a blog post that not only would it comply with the AI training policy token but that it would also offer publishers the ability to opt out of AI grounding, or when crawlers scrape sites to find real-time information. These preferences will replace the existing Microsoft preferences “NoArchive” and “NoCache,” for AI training and grounding respectively, starting Feb. 1, 2027.

Meta didn’t go as far as to say it would honor the policy token, but Andy Stone, a Meta company spokesperson, wrote on X: “Meta, too, strongly support publishers’ ability to express preferences about how their content is used for foundational generative AI training. We believe creating a common signal that conveys such preferences to multiple providers at once is an important step. And we look forward to working with other AI companies on the standards and their implementation across the industry.”

The proposal may appeal to publishers seeking standardized controls. But, like robots.txt, it offers no way to ensure AI and tech companies comply with publishers’ preferences.

Robots.txt preferences are infamously more of a suggestion than an enforceable doctrine.

In fact, data suggests that web crawlers rarely even check publishers’ robots.txt files when scraping their sites. Crawlers operated by the four companies backing the shared opt-out token made roughly 1 billion requests over the past 30 days — but just 0.15% of those requests were for robots.txt files, according to data shared with Digiday by AI visibility measurement firm ScalePost, which tracked activity across more than 50 publisher properties.

Paul Bannister, chief strategy officer at Raptive, called the AI training policy token “window dressing.”

“I think this is useless and is marketing, and far, far, far too late… It does nothing and doesn’t solve the problem at all,” he said.

AI tech companies have a lot of reasons to want to appear to appease publishers. They’re fighting a number of copyright infringement lawsuits, pressure from government regulators, and proposed legislation targeting crawlers.

Four major AI tech companies have said they will honor this AI training policy token, making this one of the first proposed standards with that kind of support.

While it remains to be seen if this will be the case in practice, the policy token could be another layer publishers have at their disposal to protect their content from web crawlers, in addition to mechanisms like blocking crawlers at the CDN level or using tools like TollBit.

“Even though it doesn’t keep all the thieves out, you’d still put a lock on your front door because it keeps some of the thieves out… You do lots and lots of things to minimize the chances that your house gets broken into, but you cannot prevent it completely,” Bannister said. “Is it one other arrow to put in your quiver of about 1,000 things you need to do? Sure.”

Many publishers have been working hard to improve their AI visibility, and some are selling GEO products to brands to boost their visibility in LLMs. The last thing those publishers want is to jeopardize their mentions and citations in those tools.

Of the four companies backing the policy token, Microsoft was the only one to explicitly address its implications for AI visibility. If a publisher does disallow crawlers for AI grounding, “you will see reduced brand visibility in AI experiences,” reads Microsoft’s blog post.

This is part of the reason publishers are wary of some of Google’s AI opt-out mechanisms, which let publishers exclude their content from AI-powered search features but could mean sacrificing valuable visibility and traffic they can’t afford to lose.

The AI training policy token doesn’t have anything to do with AI inference (or AI usage), which is when an AI model generates responses or performs tasks by retrieving or processing content from publishers’ websites in real time. This has become core to most of the recent AI licensing deals between publishers and tech companies.

And even if an AI crawler abides by publishers’ preferences expressed in robots.txt, it doesn’t mean the AI provider won’t be able to get that data elsewhere, such as from other crawlers, Bannister said.

“None of them have said, ‘We won’t buy data from third parties that scrape the content that don’t obey this,’ or ‘We won’t take open source crawls like Common Crawl,’ Bannister said. “They’re going to get all the data anyway, just through a different mechanism, and so it’s completely useless.”

The draft also appears to reinforce the idea that AI companies can use publishers’ content for training by default, unless publishers explicitly tell them not to, Springer said. This “opt in unless opted out” concept risks legitimizing a system where crawlers can presume they have permission for AI training, rather than needing explicit consent, he argued.

“The platforms are taking the stance that unless a preference to the contrary is expressed then content is fair game for AI training. We should not be normalizing this view and need to continue to work to maintain the concept that the usage of content should be determined by its owners and the result of their direct permission,” Springer said.

Google is moving on two fronts: working up the U.K’s Competition and Markets Authority-mandated search opt-out publishers have long sought, while enhancing AI Overviews in ways that could make opting out less appealing.

Instagram wants to pull long-form creators away from YouTube, but is it offering enough? Experts weigh in on the platform’s pivot.

How performance metrics are rewriting creator rate cards

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Any creator asking for media dollars better have the reporting to back up the request. Advertisers aren’t cutting checks just on follower count and virality anymore.

To ink a creator deal, advertisers are asking for cost per lead, cost per conversion, earned media value and other performance metrics. In some cases, some brands are asking for Instagram link clicks and engaged YouTube views up front to determine if a creator is worth the ad spend.

“They want to see if there’s actual traffic being driven. We’re seeing a stronger emphasis on accountability for sales for each creator,” said Kimberly Duman, a managing partner at Glossary Artist creator management firm.

Creator campaigns are more often being judged against traditional performance marketing math: cost per conversion, cost per lead and return on ad spend.

Consider it yet another signal of the creator economy mirroring programmatic advertising.

As the tech has gotten better, creator platforms and agencies alike have spent the last year rolling out AI-powered dashboards with years of historical performance data. These souped up control panels allow brands to see a creator’s engagement rate, follower count, conversion rate and other performance stats before a deal is ever made.

Now, follower count becomes a vanity metric as brands look for metrics that prove purchasing behavior.

“Cost per lead or cost per conversion. Simple as that,” said Joseph Perello, founder and CEO of Props, a performance-based creator marketing platform, later adding, “Every client has a cost per conversion benchmark.”

Naturally, that benchmark looks different for every client. But as the tech improves, advertisers are retooling their scorecard to determine how good of a salesperson a creator is, he said.

Or as Stefani Stamatiou, CEO of Americas at HYDP, sees it: “The way that we work with a lot of our creators is to look at the performance of their content before we contract them.”

Before deal talks start, the global creator and influencer agency HYDP evaluates a creator’s content within a 30-day window, including view counts, but excludes any viral content to set reasonable expectations, Stamatiou said.

View counts has its own set of challenges. YouTube overhauled its view count in August. Rather than a count metric starting at several seconds in, a video view is registered the moment a video starts to play.

Now, advertiseres want “engaged views” — at least Blue Hour influencer agency is asking for those stats up front, Matt Higgins, svp and head of strategy at Blue Hour told Digiday.

“The bigger point is what we should focus on when finding and negotiating with creators,” Higgins said of the YouTube changes. “We negotiate around audience fit, shares, and expected performance, with views as one input.”

The brand tracks clicks and sales, but places greater emphasis on creator-brand alignment. “Great content will always win,” Glossary’s Duman said.

The AI platform is testing an analytics dashboard that counts how often a business shows up in ChatGPT’s answers.

Despite leaning on creators for most of the year, marketers aren’t doing it for the holiday crunch. That job still belongs to celebrities.

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

As publishers await click data, Google’s AI visibility push complicates the opt-out decision

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Google’s latest publisher carrot? AI visibility.

Google is moving on two fronts: working up the U.K’s Competition and Markets Authority-mandated search opt-out publishers have long sought, while enhancing AI Overviews in ways that could make opting out less appealing.

The opt-out is a Search Console control that lets publishers exclude their content from Google’s AI features. The control follows the CMA’s first conduct requirement on Google, imposed over the summer. While the ruling was U.K.-only, Google opened it to sites worldwide on August 31. Page-level grounding controls are due by March 3, 2027.

But the controls are bundled: publishers can’t opt out of individual features. That leaves them weighing the potential visibility against the loss of control and waiting for click data to help judge the trade-off.

That click data, which Google is required to provide to U.K. publishers by Dec. 3, will give publishers a key missing piece of the puzzle. And given Google made the first requirements global, publishers hope it will provide click data globally too. Google hasn’t confirmed that.

Google has shown regulators and publishers that it is cooperating by sharing impression data: how often publisher content appears in AI features. But it has held back the click data, which would show whether those appearances actually send people to publishers’ sites. “That is the metric that will determine whether they [publishers] turn it off or not,” said Chris Dicker, CEO of Candr Media, which owns Trusted Reviews. “They’re not giving the metrics [currently] that actually mean anything, when they could have — because they’ve got those — they could have done it at the same time as impressions, but they haven’t.”

He added that “everyone is holding off” playing around with the new controls until they see the click data because they don’t want to turn off a large proportion of their activities.

Dicker expects Google to share the click data in the coming weeks, ahead of Black Friday, when publishers are unlikely to risk experimenting with opt-outs during a crucial sales period.

Meanwhile, Google has updated AI Mode and AI Overviews to show links more often and more prominently, added timely article carousels, and included links to related stories and more context around surfaced articles.

Google says these changes will help publishers better track, control and increase their content visibility. It’s also testing links from video creators’ and publishers’ names to their Search profiles, and says that its Preferred Sources — the feature that lets users pick and bookmark the specific websites and publishers they most trust — has grown sevenfold this year.

That visibility comes with a catch, though: the more valuable Google’s AI features become, the harder it may be to justify opting out.

For some publishers, that trade-off is already a live question. “We’re discussing the CMA controls and how we exercise those,” said Carly Steven, director of ecommerce and commercial editorial at the Daily Mail, at Digiday’s Media Strategies London event in October.

“We need to be so, so careful because just as we’re about to get the ability to remove articles on a URL basis, Google announces this new feature, and we have to ask ourselves: do we really not want to be there? It does still matter that we’re on the surfaces where users are. So it’s a very complicated picture.”

David Buttle, founder of publisher AI standards coalition SPUR, believes Google’s preferred approach is to provide enough referral traffic from its AI-related surfaces for publishers to agree to let Google use their content, rather than paying them each time it does so. That means it needs to continue providing some kind of value exchange. “The way they can achieve that within the context of the conduct requirement is to ensure they can deliver enough value in the form of traffic from those surfaces back to publishers, so that they can say yes voluntarily,” said Buttle.

Trying to maintain its exchange with publishers is tricky when its newest rival for information retrieval – OpenAI – isn’t constrained by the same legacy relationship. “ChatGPT doesn’t pay for content generally, and it doesn’t send any traffic out,” he added. “So Google’s approach is to try and intermediate as many clicks from Discover, which is a really important traffic source for publishers, and they’re serving summaries really aggressively in Discover, and bundling the control for that with the control for summaries everywhere else.”

For all the noise around opting out, publishers are unlikely to do so unless they can opt out in a more granular way, stressed Buttle. “What it [Google] can do, though, is play around with the interface and the product for Discover to juice the AI-intermediated referrals that come out of that to get as much traffic out of that, so if you’re a publisher, it makes that decision to opt out far harder,” he added.

For some publishers, the question is still how to assess the controls before deciding whether to use them. At The Economist Group, that process is only beginning.

“On the CMA point, we’re looking at it,” said Josh Muncke, vp of generative AI at The Economist Group on stage at the Digiday Media Strategies event earlier this month. “I don’t believe we’ve tested it yet, but we’re certainly looking at it.”

Whatever that decision is, it will ultimately hinge on two things: one is how much traffic Google sends The Economist. The other is how often its name appears in the AI answers at the top of the results, even when nobody clicks through. He added: There are second-order effects to turning these things off that aren’t just about the traffic. They’re about broader visibility in the world.”

Some of those effects are already on the table. Some publishers expect excluded sites to drop out of the Top Stories carousel Google now places inside AI Overviews and Google hasn’t said how its control treats that unit.

Reuters Digital Phil Andraos told Digiday in June that the lack of click data meant any decision could be slow and probably involve testing.

Muncke’s instinct runs the same way. He said he wouldn’t frame it as a single in-or-out call at a fixed point. “It will be a case more of thinking about where and how we can test, measure, and see the impacts, and make kind of like more structured, data-driven calls as we go,” he said.

The Professional Publishers Association (PPA) is appealing to the CMA to press Google to unbundle the controls so they apply to individual features, rather than solely site- and page-level, so that publishers can have a deeper level of control.

For example, a specific query answered by an AI Overview may replace a click a publisher would previously have received, while the same content could still be valuable in a broader, conversational AI search.

“You’re either opting out of search AI Mode, AI Overviews – basically all these different features, or none of them,” said PPA’s head of policy and Public Affairs Eilidh Wilson. “And this is something that we continue to hear is what most concerns publishers. We’ve been really wanting to land this with impact with the CMA, so we’ve agreed to do a further follow-up briefing with them on it to look at the different parts,” she added.

Major AI companies are backing a new AI training opt-out standard proposal for publishers, but it exposes deeper tensions over AI scraping.

Instagram wants to pull long-form creators away from YouTube, but is it offering enough? Experts weigh in on the platform’s pivot.

Ad tech’s winners and losers emerge as CTV growth masks open-web struggles.

From supermarket fridges to passenger flights, a wave of threats is testing Europe's defenses

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Cyberattacks, bomb threats against passenger flights, civil unrest and suspected acts of sabotage across Europe have raised questions about the continent's resilience — and how prepared it is to defend itself against modern security threats.

Hackers remotely manipulated refrigeration systems at supermarkets across Europe last week, forcing retailers to discard spoiled food and exposing vulnerabilities in everyday infrastructure.

French blood-bank refrigeration systems were subsequently targeted in what appeared to be a similar incident, while a wave of suspected hoax bomb threats disrupted dozens of flights across the continent, forcing diversions and, in some cases, fighter jet escorts.

The week culminated with bomb threats targeting European news outlets, including the U.K.'s Sky News and Irish public broadcaster RTÉ.

The incidents come against a backdrop of heightened geopolitical tensions and domestic unrest across parts of Europe, with civilian infrastructure potentially becoming an increasingly attractive target for hostile actors.

While authorities have not established who was behind the attacks or whether they were connected, defense and security experts warn that disruption to everyday services can have consequences far beyond the immediate economic damage.

If Russia were responsible, such attacks could be intended to demonstrate the costs of Europe's continued support for Ukraine, said Sidharth Kaushal, a research fellow at the Royal United Services Institute, or RUSI.

"I think it's more about cost imposition," Kaushal told CNBC. "Convincing the European political leadership that the costs of a war can come home to them."

Russia didn't immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

On Monday, supermarkets in Belgium, Luxembourg, Sweden and the Netherlands found that refrigeration systems had been set to as much as 50 degrees Celsius, or 122 degrees Fahrenheit, affecting retailers including Ahold Delhaize, IKEA, Colruyt Group and Carrefour.

It followed an October cyberattack that targeted hundreds of French supermarkets, causing fires, fridges to overheat and food to spoil. The incidents highlighted vulnerabilities in food-supply infrastructure.

They also highlight the role of private companies in national resilience, as internet-connected equipment can be exploited to cause physical disruption.

Fredrik Blix, cybersecurity expert at Visente and associate professor at Stockholm University, told CNBC that the supermarket attacks required very little technical sophistication since vulnerable refrigeration control systems were connected directly to the public internet, allowing potential attackers to find and exploit them.

"These attacks, they don't really require any specific advanced knowledge to carry out," he said, adding that some systems were protected only by default passwords or easily guessed credentials.

"This is why this is so cheap to do," Blix said, warning that the publicity surrounding the incidents could encourage copycat attacks. "Anyone can do it, and it does not require any resources from a country or anything like that."

Cyberattacks have become more common over the past decade as societies have become more digital and vulnerable systems have become easier to access, Blix said. However, he noted that mounting geopolitical tensions have also made European countries more alert to potential hybrid warfare threats, leading to scrutiny by both governments and media.

While Blix acknowledged the possibility of a state or state-backed actor being behind the attacks, he said the more likely scenario is someone testing the boundaries and "having some fun."

Kaushal said the incidents should be viewed within the larger context of suspected sabotage and hybrid threats across Europe, including damage to undersea cables in the Baltic Sea.

"If this was an event in isolation, one might say 'okay, we should view it on its own merits,' but when you view it in the backdrop of all of that, it becomes less clear," he said, also citing death threats against European defense executives and a bomb placed on a DHL aircraft in 2024.

European governments are contending with greater uncertainty over U.S. security guarantees after decades of underinvestment in defense, while facing a rise in unconventional but potentially devastating threats.

Hybrid warfare combines tactics such as cyberattacks, sabotage and disinformation to destabilize adversaries without triggering open military conflict.

Countries' abilities to withstand hybrid threats vary substantially across the continent, according to Kaushal. While Finland and Sweden have well-developed civilian defense programs reflecting their Cold War experience, much of Europe's infrastructure was built around economic efficiency rather than defense needs.

"As one goes further west in Europe, the level of preparation, both against hybrid attacks, but also just simple missile attacks of the kind that we've seen in Ukraine, is far less great," he said.

"Every attack generates a bit of resilience," he said, citing NATO's increased focus on threats to underwater cables and gas pipelines after the 2022 Nord Stream sabotage.

Oana Popescu-Zamfir, a senior research fellow specializing in European security at RUSI, said the recent incidents showed Europe had lagged in "preparing for something that we have known would be coming."

"We've been slow in adjusting to a new reality," she said. "We still tend to conceptually divide between peace and wartime, and that is no longer the case because we're not at war, but we're also not in peace time."

She said Europe was likely to face sustained hybrid attacks designed to achieve military objectives without conventional military force.

Popescu-Zamfir said Russia could draw on networks of proxies in Europe, some directly controlled and others more loosely coordinated, making responsibility difficult to trace directly to the Kremlin.

She told CNBC that "the damage that can be done is huge" in targeted attacks on internet-connected systems, citing the potential disruption of blood reserves.

The attacks are not a "piece of cake for Russia to carry out, but they're also not rocket science," she said, adding that they "can do tremendous damage in concrete terms and in terms of the psychological impact on our societies and the feeling that we are unprotected."

Blix said companies can take relatively straightforward steps to reduce their exposure.

"These are basic cyber hygiene things... You should not be allowed to connect industrial control systems and this kind of operational technology directly online without any proper security around it," he said.

Responsibility is split among product manufacturers, maintenance providers and grocery chains, making accountability unclear as each points to the others.

Blix said maintenance companies sometimes prioritize convenience by enabling remote access despite manufacturers' security guidance, while retailers may fail to enforce adequate cybersecurity standards among suppliers.

"What worries me is that we don't really do anything," Blix said, adding that "we are shuffling a lot of papers" and "doing a lot of risk assessments" but failing to take meaningful action.

Europe has also seen widespread unrest in recent months, adding political pressure as households grapple with high living costs.

Domestic political and economic tensions can also give foreign adversaries opportunities to deepen divisions, said Alexander Anderson, president and director of policy at the Centre for International Security and Economic Strategy.

He told CNBC that Russian and potentially Chinese influence operations may work to amplify those divisions.

"But we should be careful not to dismiss legitimate domestic grievances as the product of foreign interference," he said. "Doing so risks misunderstanding both the causes of political instability and the methods through which hostile actors seek to exploit it."

Weeks of nationwide protests, riots and strikes have increased pressure on the French government, complicating efforts to rein in public finances and deepening existing political divisions.

In Spain, demonstrations and a parliamentary impasse over the housing crisis led Prime Minister Pedro Sanchez to call a snap election. The vote is scheduled for Nov. 29. The far-right Vox party is expected to make gains, putting Sanchez's left-leaning government at risk of losing power.

Germany, Italy, Portugal and Albania have also seen unrest over rising living costs in recent weeks.

On Friday, protesters in Amsterdam tried to enter a Microsoft data center, Reuters reported.

Meanwhile, elevated energy prices, persistent inflation, sluggish growth and large budget deficits are pushing up borrowing costs and leaving governments with less room to support struggling households.

AMD vs. Intel: Which Is the Better AI Chip Stock to Own for the Next 3 Years Following the Semiconductor Crash?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

AMD and Intel stocks have both seen major pullbacks in the past month.

Chip stocks have been absolutely pummeled recently, with Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +11.04%) and Intel (INTC +7.71%) two of the biggest losers over the past month. Meanwhile, the strong Democratic showing in the midterm elections put the artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure build-out on notice.

Despite the political shift, I wouldn't expect there to be a major impact on overall AI infrastructure growth in the coming years, with hyperscalers being able to navigate the current environment and shifting some projects overseas. However, between AMD and Intel, I think the clear winner is likely to be AMD. Here's why.

Advanced Micro Devices has been one of the hardest-hit stocks in the semiconductor pullback. However, the company is riding two powerful waves in inference and agentic AI that are just starting to kick in, and its new Helios rack solution could be a game changer.

Helios is AMD's turnkey AI infrastructure solution that incorporates its graphics processing units (GPUs), central processing units (CPUs), and networking components into a single system. The system contains 72 GPUs and 18 CPUs per rack and is designed to provide the "lowest cost per token" to help reduce overall computing costs. The system has already started being rolled out to key customers, including OpenAI, Meta Platforms, Microsoft, Anthropic, and Oracle, and OEM (original equipment manufacturer) and ODM (original design manufacturer) partners will also soon begin to ship Helios.

AMD has two large deals in place with OpenAI and Meta Platforms for 6 gigawatts of compute capacity each. With each Helios system costing between $5 million and $5.5 million and consuming between 225 kilowatts and 245 kilowatts of power, each deal would be worth approximately $120 billion. Meanwhile, Anthropic's 2-gigawatt deal would be worth around $40 billion, with Microsoft and Oracle as smaller customers.

At the same time, the company is seeing huge demand for its server CPUs, given that these chips are needed to help manage AI agents. Its CPUs are helping run Meta's new Muse AI agents, and the company sees server CPUs becoming a $220 billion market by 2030.

As a result, AMD is projecting growth to soar next year, with it recently forecasting revenue to surge 80% in 2027. The pullback has dropped the stock to a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of around 9 times its 2030 analyst estimates, giving it plenty of room to run the next three years.

While AMD has been the victim of a broader semiconductor stock sell-off, much of Intel's wounds are self-inflicted. This is due to the company's still-outsized exposure to the PC market, which is seeing weak demand as high memory costs have caused computer makers to raise prices, slowing demand.

Intel's client computing and physical AI group (CCPG) remains its largest segment, and revenue for the unit fell 5% last quarter. However, the company is expecting units to drop 10% in 2027 after a 12% decline this year, which will also lead to a large inventory correction that will significantly impact the next two quarters.

Meanwhile, Intel's foundry business continues to produce large losses, including a $1.9 billion operating loss in Q3. The company is struggling to find external customers, in part due to technology and in part due to conflicts of interest, and if the data center build-out shifts overseas, this will likely sting it even more, given its strong domestic footprint.

Intel is the market leader in server CPUs, and like AMD, it's been seeing strong growth in this area. However, the company has been consistently losing shares to AMD and custom CPUs. This is only expected to continue in the years ahead, especially as the company has no turnkey solution like Nvidia and AMD.

As such, I think the stock could continue to underperform over the next three years.

Geoffrey Seiler has positions in Advanced Micro Devices and Meta Platforms. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Advanced Micro Devices, Intel, Meta Platforms, Microsoft, Nvidia, and Oracle. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

HP DesignJet T250 printer places professional printing in users’ hands

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

From printing menus to educational posters, technology company HP’s DesignJet T250 printer gives businesses the ability to design and print high-quality materials in house.

Described by the brand as the “world’s smallest plotters”, with HP DesignJet T250 users can customise thousands of professionally created templates and print content on-site when required.

The printer can be used for everything from designing large-format posters to advertising a new product or an event.

It can also print branded communications or create retail displays, among other materials. According to HP, this benefits multiple industries, from food and culture to education and design.

HP explained that this flexibility redefines the professional printing workflow by giving businesses control over the creative and operational process, reducing their reliance on external printing providers.

This also results in increasingly flexible timelines, with in-house designers able to tweak their design, then quickly print another copy.

According to HP, the printer also gives brands and companies greater control over costs.

This is particularly useful to smaller businesses, entrepreneurs and creative studios, it explained, where developing professional marketing materials can be expensive and time-consuming.

Instead, HP DesignJet T250 places control into user’s hands by giving them a fast and flexible way to bring creative ideas to life.

According to HP, click software incorporated into the device further streamlines workflow by providing an easy print experience and helping ensure prints are produced as intended.

“By bringing design and large-format printing together in one accessible solution, the HP DesignJet T250 helps small businesses and creative professionals create impactful visual communications without the complexity traditionally associated with professional printing,” the company said.

The HP DesignJet T250 is compact enough to be placed in small workspaces, offices or studios. It was made from at least 40-per-cent recycled plastic and is the first HP large-format plotter to contain recycled metal.

Users can also save 65 per cent energy when using the printer’s on/off auto-scheduler, according to the brand.

For a limited time, users can purchase an HP DesignJet T250 with a year’s worth of Canva Pro included. According to HP, this gives designers everything they need to create and print professional-quality posters from a single workflow.

“The combination of Canva’s intuitive design platform and the HP DesignJet T250 creates a seamless workflow that allows users to move from concept to final print with ease,” said HP.

To learn more about HP DesignJet T250, visit HP’s website.

BVNK Opens OUSD Minting and Payments Across Four Blockchains

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: BVNK says eligible business customers can now mint, redeem, hold and transfer Open USD (OUSD) on Ethereum, Solana, Base and Tempo. The November 6 rollout is a practical payments integration, but the company has not provided transaction volumes proving how extensively the new service is being used.

A stablecoin’s biggest hurdle may not be issuing tokens but making them useful inside ordinary financial operations. Payments infrastructure company BVNK has taken a step in that direction by making Open USD accessible through its existing platform.

In a November 6 announcement, BVNK said eligible businesses can mint and redeem OUSD against dollar balances, hold it and transfer it on Ethereum, Solana, Base and Tempo. The product is aimed at payment providers, treasury teams and companies that need to move between bank money and blockchain settlement without assembling separate integrations for every network.

BVNK outlines three routes. A direct integration allows a company to use OUSD in its own treasury and operations. An embedded option lets developers offer wallets, pay-ins or payouts within customer-facing applications. Wholesale access is aimed at exchanges, trading desks and market makers that need larger-scale minting and redemption.

These categories are more important than a promotional claim of being multichain. A business needs clarity about the currency held, when redemption is available, which network is supported and whether the underlying legal and compliance arrangements suit the countries involved. A token arriving quickly on a blockchain is only one step in an end-to-end payment.

BVNK says the launch offers 1:1 conversion against dollar balances and positions OUSD as part of an open-infrastructure model. The firm identifies a consortium of financial and technology companies behind the wider initiative, but membership of a consortium should not be mistaken for a guarantee of liquidity, issuer credit quality or operational support from every named institution.

A company with customers on different chains can otherwise face bridge complexity, additional custody arrangements and fragmented liquidity. Supporting several networks in one interface potentially reduces that operational burden, especially for businesses already using BVNK’s services.

However, crosschain availability does not mean every route is interchangeable or that every address and wallet supports every version of the token. Businesses still have to prevent wrong-network transfers and reconcile balances across chains. The value of the integration will be visible in reliability and error handling, not just a list of supported networks.

For context, NewsBTC has covered how Solana’s settlement tools are being built for institutions. OUSD addresses a related but separate problem: supplying a payment asset and an operating layer through which companies can use it.

BVNK says OUSD is available to eligible business customers outside the European Economic Area. That geographic qualification matters because stablecoin access and licensing rules differ between jurisdictions. Users should check eligibility before assuming they can open a new account or send tokens through a particular network.

The company has not published figures establishing how much OUSD has been minted or transferred specifically through this new integration. For now, the news is a live product capability, not a demonstrated leap in adoption. The next useful evidence would be sustained payment volume, repeat business usage and disclosures about redemption performance during busy markets.

Moon phase today: What the Moon will look like on November 9, 2026

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

There's barely any of the Moon visible tonight as it approaches the New Moon. With less than a sliver illuminated, there is nothing for us to see in the way of lunar features.

As of Monday, Nov. 9, the NASA's Daily Moon Guide tracker tells us the Moon is in the New Moon phase, with 0% of its surface visible.

The next Full Moon will take place on November 24.

It takes the Moon around 29.5 days to pass through a complete lunar cycle. As it travels around Earth, the angle at which sunlight reaches its surface changes what we can see from here, producing the different phases, from the New Moon through to the Full Moon and back again. The cycle then starts over, as explained by NASA.

New Moon - The Moon is between Earth and the sun, so the side we see is dark (in other words, it's invisible to the eye).

Waxing Crescent - A small sliver of light appears on the right side (Northern Hemisphere).

First Quarter - Half of the Moon is lit on the right side. It looks like a half-Moon.

Waxing Gibbous - More than half is lit up, but it's not quite full yet.

Full Moon - The whole face of the Moon is illuminated and fully visible.

Waning Gibbous - The Moon starts losing light on the right side. (Northern Hemisphere)

Third Quarter (or Last Quarter) - Another half-Moon, but now the left side is lit.

Waning Crescent - A thin sliver of light remains on the left side before going dark again.

You can find bylines in publications including Fit&Well, Metro, and Coach magazine, usually covering deals on everything from earbuds to TVs, or guides on how to beat your half marathon time.

Lois also holds a Master's degree in Digital Journalism from Strathclyde University and obtained a Master of Arts in English Literature at the University of Aberdeen.

34 Years Ago, Marvel's Best Creators Rebelled Like Never Before

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Back in 1992, a crew of Marvel Comics' best artists dramatically left to start their own venture; that decision would end up having a colossal impact on the comics industry of today. Image Comics is nowadays known as a powerhouse unto itself, historically publishing major works like Spawn, The Walking Dead, and Savage Dragon. Meanwhile, Robert Kirkman's Invincible has been adapted into what's arguably the 21st Century's best animated superhero series so far.

Despite a slew of top-tier material, what makes Image Comics a particular standout in the history of comics is the publisher's fraught origins. Nestled within its founding and the early years when Image defined '90s comics is a story of independent comics, creators' rights, and more that connects back to creators' disillusionment with Marvel for decades. Image Comics just happened to have the right combination of things to make a true creators' rebellion work.

Image Comics was born from what was effectively an exodus of Marvel's hottest talents of the time: Todd McFarlane (Spider-Man), Rob Liefeld (X-Force), and Jim Lee (X-Men), all of whom had broken sales records—Lee's X-Men #1 remains the best-selling comic of all time. This wasn't something that Marvel rewarded. For Marvel, brand identity was the most important thing—over and above the personalities working on the comics, themselves. Comics were effectively written freelance as work-for-hire, meaning that creators themselves were—and often still are—disposable.

This had two effective consequences for breakout stars working under the Big Two. The first, for some creators like Liefeld, was the feeling that their stardom made them disposable; creators with star power presented a risk to the brand-centric paradigm. The second, for other creators, was a feeling of being undervalued: because they worked as freelancers, they had no actual rights to things like royalties for merchandise based on their work.

While Invincible enjoys its well-deserved place in the spotlight today, it’s worth remembering the groundbreaking work done 24 years earlier.

All of this should also be understood in the context of a tide-shift in the industry, generally. More and more independent and creator-owned publishers were cropping up, with varying levels of success. Creators' rights had always been a major concern in the industry: milestones included the 1978 formation of the Comics Creators Guild (featuring huge names like Neal Adams, Frank Miller, Chris Claremont, and more). Todd McFarlane had made efforts to unionize creators at Marvel prior to Image Comics being established.

For the Image Comics exodus in particular, the minutiae were well documented in Michael Dean's four-part series "The Image Story" for The Comics Journal, charting the history of Image Comics up to its publication in 2000. What the deep-dive shows isn't an organized group of rebels but disgruntled artists with variable motivations and wavering commitments simply seeking to own the works they create if they were going to stay in the comics industry.

Image Comics was the end result of a long process through which Liefeld found other options—it was Liefeld who would bring Marvel's top artists together, and it was Liefeld who went to Malibu Comics and probed into whether they would publish black-and-white lines from Marvel's biggest names. Liefeld would go on to recruit McFarlane, Lee, Erik Larsen, Jim Valentino, Marc Silvestri, and Whilce Portacio as a united front to bargain with Marvel for creative control.

The triumphant Image Comics rebellion doesn't start with an exodus, but with an ultimatum given to Marvel Comics' president by Liefeld, Lee, and McFarlane in December 1991: that they would continue working at Marvel on their own creator-owned lines, or they would leave to publish elsewhere. The outcome was perhaps predictable, and the artists left with intentions to defect completely.

In 1992, Image Comics was officially established. The underlying principle of creators owning their own works wasn't new, but Image's managerial structure was relatively novel; Image would act as infrastructure for the studios and partners working under its umbrella, and no partner could interfere with another partner's work. Malibu Comics played a critical role in realizing those principles, willing to take on the Marvel talent at face value and provide essential early support with marketing, publication, and distribution.

Through a combination of luck, timing, and star power, Image would become a massive hit, even briefly overtaking DC's market share with record-breaking titles like Liefeld's Youngblood, McFarlane's Spawn, and Larsen's The Savage Dragon. To this day, many of Image Comics' best series can be traced to the company's first year. By 1993, Image was successful enough to leave Malibu entirely and become independent.

The Youngblood franchise returns and expands this summer and ScreenRant is proud to offer an exclusive first look at two exciting variant covers.

The following year, more non-partners would start publishing under Image with titles like Alan Moore, Steve Bissette, and Rick Veitch's 1963 and Larry Stroman's Tribe (which would become the best-selling African American comic from an African American creator of all time).

To an extent, Image's structure would also become its undoing for a while. The surrounding hype combined with its creators' failures to deliver books on time would arguably play a considerable role in comics' speculative bubble bursting in 1993. The same varied motivations that birthed Image Comics would pave the way for intense disagreements, tension, and poaching between founders—Liefeld would officially resign in 1996, right before a meeting where he would have been ousted. (He would later return as a creator, not a partner, in 2007.)

Despite everything, though, Image Comics eventually righted the ship, steadily expanding their line of comics and eventually bringing on a new partner, Robert Kirkman. Today, Image stands as an emblem of independent comics publishing in general: independent studios can not only survive and thrive in an ecosystem dominated by the Big Two, but they can become enough of a force to compete with them.

This is despite unglamorous origins in an often understated history of creators' rights in comics that, ironically, found a major watershed at Marvel itself when the legendary Jack Kirby, co-creator of many of the brand's biggest characters, started to publicly criticize Stan Lee and the company over rights, royalties, authorial credit, and the work-for-hire model in the '70s and '80s.

The MCU Slump is still at the front of fans' minds, and 1 Marvel creator says the dip is happening on comics for the same reason.

In other words, the stardom that would propel Image to success was latent in Marvel's own machinery. Image stands as a spiritual win for creators' rights in comics. But its huge success also meant it could provide a truly commercially viable platform for independent creator-owned works that could stand up against the Big Two—the lack of which creators had been circling for years before.

The history of Image Comics is fascinating precisely because it's rocky. There was no golden origin, and the reality is that the bedrock was there for something like Image Comics to emerge eventually—the industry had been setting up for a creator-focused behemoth that could actually contest the Big Two for decades. But Image Comics is what emerged, and through the rebellion of seven disgruntled Marvel artists, the company now stands as a beacon for the stories mainstream publishers might not want to tell.

10 2026 Movies That Are Officially Oscar Contenders

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

This year, the record-breaking MCU movie Spider-Man: Brand New Day and Christopher Nolan's historical epic The Odyssey headlined the biggest summer of cinema since the COVID-19 pandemic. But the late summer nights are turning to cold winter evenings, and the attention of cinephiles has turned to the biggest event in the film calendar: the Academy Awards.

The glitz and glamor of Oscars night is coming into view, as the 99th ceremony readies to celebrate the best of the past twelve months of film. Scheduled to take place March 14, 2027, at the Dolby Theatre in Los Angeles, next year's Academy Awards will feature all the brightest stars in Hollywood today, with ambitious blockbusters, indie darlings, and even a documentary hoping to earn a coveted Best Picture nomination. But who are the current favorites? Here's a look at 10 2026 movies that are officially Oscar contenders.

The film on everyone's lips, A24's You Can See Everything, might finally make history as the first documentary to earn a Best Picture nomination in 100 years of Academy history. The documentary, co-directed by Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim, explores the days leading up to the incarceration of the infamous Theranos founder, Elizabeth Holmes.

"The year's most original movie will make you question reality," wrote Collider's Samuel R. Murrian in one of countless glowing reviews for this mind-bending documentary. With its viral success helping rocket the film up the box office charts, and with the world stunned by the life-altering contents of this three-hour masterpiece, it's possible that You Can See Everything ​​​​​​won't just earn a Best Picture nomination; it could win the trophy.

One of the defining films of 2026, it would be difficult to find someone who hasn't yet seen Project Hail Mary. This epic Phil Lord and Christopher Miller-directed space story follows Ryan Gosling's Ryland Grace as he leads a one-man mission into the stars to try and save the universe. However, he isn't alone in the dense emptiness of space, as he is joined on his mission by an alien he names Rocky.

A favorite to earn plenty of nominations in technical categories, with lead puppeteer and voice actor James Ortiz potentially in the race for Best Supporting Actor, Project Hail Mary will most definitely make its mark on next year's Oscars. As one of the most critically and commercially successful films of 2026, come March 14, 2027, the world could watch Grace Rocky save Oscars.

Another of the most popular films of the year, Focus Features' Obsession will hope to follow in the footsteps of The Substance and Weapons by becoming this year's horror darling at the Academy Awards. Star Inde Navarrette seems most likely to take home Oscars gold, as she currently ranks as one of the favorites in the Best Actress category for a performance that Collider's Hannah Hunt called "one of the year's best" in her review.

A box office record-breaker, becoming the highest-grossing original horror movie of this century among many more titles, Obsession shocked the world as it overcame the latest Star Wars movie through sheer viral appeal. Curry Barker's breakout is nothing short of a horror masterpiece, as one of the most important visionaries in Hollywood's next era gets off to a flying start.

Although some titles lead the charge during the Oscars race, others perform better under the radar, lurking beneath the competition before surprising cinephiles with several nominations. Right now, it seems like one of the best outside contenders for Academy recognition is Olivia Wilde's The Invite, a remake of the Spanish-language comedy The People Upstairs.

Unfolding like a great play, The Invite takes a simple premise, a single location, and four actors at the top of their game and transforms them into an utterly unmissable comedy. But this comedy isn't without its dramatic flair, with every twist and turn playing out like a hilariously realized thriller. Starring Seth Rogen, Edward Norton, and Penélope Cruz alongside Wilde, The Invite feels destined to earn recognition in writing and acting categories.

If, for the aforementioned Penélope Cruz, Oscar night doesn't equal gold for Wilde's The Invite, rest assured she stands a second chance as part of the ensemble of one of the bookies' favorites. Javier Calvo and Javier Ambrossi's La Bola Negra is a 155-minute drama inspired by the unfinished work of the same name that explores the connections between three gay men and the pain they endure.

Themes of love and loss in the queer community, as well as the erasure of queer identity, become ever more relevant each passing year, with these topics better represented at the Academy Awards in the process. La Bola Negra is the latest LGBTQIA+ story to receive the Oscars spotlight, as it is deemed a favorite to not just earn a Best Picture nomination, but to win the prize.

When forecasting the Oscars nominees for 2027, it's wise to look back at the historical voting of the often predictable Academy. Both 2021's Dune and 2022's Dune: Part Two received nominations in the Best Picture category, having combined to win eight trophies so far. So, with that in mind, the final installment in Denis Villeneuve's acclaimed trilogy seems destined to pick up a handful of nominations once again.

The ambitious attempt to turn the once-thought "unfilmable" Frank Herbert story into cinematic magic has already been the subject of endless praise from critics. But in his last installment, perhaps it's time for Villeneuve to win in the biggest categories? That's what many sci-fi fans will think, and as December 18 and the consequences of Paul Atreides' (Timothée Chalamet) power inch closer, whispers of a Dune: Part Three Best Picture bid grow.

Andrew Scott has quietly proven to be one of the most consistent actors working today, submitting a fine selection of truly terrific performances over the past few years. Come Oscars night next year, it seems the Sherlock star will finally receive recognition as one of the favorites to take home the Best Actor prize as Ian Charleson in the stunning drama, Elsinore.

Directed by Simon Stone and written by Stephen Beresford, Elsinore looks likely to be the second Hamlet-related film in two years to take home an acting prize at the Oscars, following Jessie Buckley's deserved win for Hamnet. Also like Hamnet, Elsinore looks to be this year's biggest tearjerker, with Collider's Therese Lacson writing, "For god's sake, bring more than just one pack of tissues to the theater," in her review.

In 2023, The Banshees of Inisherin was one of the year's biggest nominees, having earned nods in eight categories. Sadly, it won zero prizes, a fate that Irish filmmaker Martin McDonagh will be hoping to avoid with his latest Academy Award play, Wild Horse Nine. The film follows two CIA agents on a mission from Santiago to Easter Island in 1973, and is packed with all of McDonagh's typical dark, comedic wit.

As usual, a McDonagh movie looks set to take home nominations in writing categories, with star John Malkovich heavily tipped to take on the aforementioned Scott for Best Actor. Sam Rockwell, Steve Buscemi, Parker Posey, and the rest of the supporting cast deserve just as much credit for this uniquely encapsulating effort from an unstoppable filmmaker.

It's rare that any given year's Palme d'Or winner doesn't make an impact at the Academy Awards. In recent years, the likes of Bong Joon Ho's Parasite and Sean Baker's Anora have won both Cannes' biggest prize and the Best Picture trophy, so it's fair to assume that Cristian Mungiu's Fjord will also make its presence known come Oscar nominations day.

Mungiu's second Palme d'Or winner is arguably his most controversial film, touching on deeply nuanced topics and never offering an easy moral way out. But it is in this nuance that Fjord thrives, with Sebastian Stan and Renate Reinsve delivering perfectly detailed performances that capture both the best and worst of these troubled characters and their terrifying situation.

The front-runner to take home the Academy's biggest prize is Christopher Nolan's historical epic The Odyssey, dubbed an "unrivaled cinematic spectacle" in Joe Schmidt's review for Collider. This famous tale's star-studded retelling hasn't been without its detractors, but those with a say have made it clear their love for The Odyssey, placing it firmly at the #1 spot in many people's predictions.

In fact, The Odyssey could very well overtake Ryan Coogler's Sinners as the most nominated film of all time, considering its sure impact in both the major and craft categories. Nolan delivers his typically ambitious scale and scope to one of history's grandest stories, culminating in a final thirty minutes that are, without doubt, some of the most encapsulating cinema of the decade so far. Could The Odyssey sail to a record-breaking Oscars night? All will be revealed in a few months.

Sunday Night Football: Bears overcome five giveaways, beat Bucs 27-24

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

It was a wild one in Chicago, but the Bears were able to pull out a 27-24 victory over the Buccaneers and move to 7-2 on the season.

Caleb Williams and the Bears’ offense started out white hot, but an interception midway through the second quarter threw off the unit for nearly the rest of the night. Williams ended up tossing four interceptions — the most in a single game he’s had in his college or pro career.

But even with five giveaways — Luther Burden also had a fumble in the fourth quarter — the Buccaneers scored just 10 points off of them. Tampa Bay couldn’t turn a blocked punt into points either, despite getting the ball at midfield.

Chicago began the game with a 14-0 lead, as Williams tossed touchdowns to Rome Odunze and Burden. The Buccaneers got on the board with Chase McLaughlin’s 27-yard field goal in the second quarter before Cairo Santos hit from 25 yards to make the score 17-3 with 3:02 left in the half. McLaughlin drilled a 21-yard field goal to end the half, with Bucs head coach Todd Bowles explaining in his halftime interview on the NBC broadcast that he felt his team could score to start the third quarter.

That turned into reality, as Bucky Irving scored a 2-yard touchdown to cap Tampa Bay’s opening drive in the second half, narrowing Chicago’s lead to 17-13.

While Williams threw his fourth interception, Baker Mayfield then threw a pick of his own to Jaylon Johnson, who returned it 67 yards for a touchdown.

McLaughlin again made it a one-possession game with a 35-yard field goal with 11:24 in the fourth quarter. Then Burden’s fumble gave the Buccaneers the opportunity to tie it deep in Bears territory, with Irving scoring on a 6-yard run. Mayfield made the two-point conversion successful by scrambling up the middle to the end zone.

But even with the offensive challenges, the Bears got the play they needed to win it. Williams connected with Colton Loveland for a 38-yard pass all the way down to the Tampa Bay 16.

After running time off the clock, Santos sent the ball through the uprights as the fourth quarter expired for a 38-yard, game-winning field goal.

Williams finished the contest 22-of-37 for 279 yards with two touchdowns and four picks. Burden led with seven catches for 97 yards with a TD. He had to be examined in the sideline medical tent after his fumble in the fourth quarter and was subsequently taken back to the locker room for further evaluation.

Mayfield finished 18-of-32 for 243 yards with two picks. Irving had 97 yards on 20 carries with two TDs, plus four catches for 28 yards.

With the win, the Bears are 7-2 in the league’s best division — as all teams in the NFC North have at least six wins. Chicago will be on its bye in Week 10 before hosting the Saints in Week 11.

At 3-6, the Buccaneers will also be on their bye in Week 10. They’ll look to get back on track on the road against the Lions in Week 11.

Linus Torvalds sees signs AI-prompted patch flood has peaked

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Fixes are arriving faster than new bug reports for one major Linux project

The worst of the patchpocalypse may be behind us, according to Linus Torvalds.

Writing in his weekly kernel update post, the Linux kernel dev leader delivered Linux 7.4 rc2 – the second rough cut of the next kernel – which he rated as a big release for two reasons.

One is that he left some code out of rc1, because he was busy and because it was already the largest such release in the history of the kernel.

The other is what Torvalds described as “various AI fixes.”

Such fixes are necessary because ever since Anthropic launched its Mythos bug-finding model in April, developers have been able to find more software flaws than was previously possible.

Microsoft, for example, addressed a record 974 flaws in September – its third record fixing spree this year after it whacked 622 flaws in July and 206 in June. Oracle addressed 1,449 flaws in a single month.

That’s many more patches than IT teams are set up to handle, yet they must find ways to deal with bugs more quickly because attackers are increasingly targeting flaws found by AI.

Torvalds’ post suggests that, perhaps, the worst of this is behind us.

“This time around Alexei claims that on the bpf side at least the fixes are now coming in faster than the new reports, and that sounds lovely if true,” he wrote.

That’s a reference to Alexei Starovoitov, co-creator and lead dev on the Extended Berkeley Packet Filter (eBPF), the project that makes it possible to run sandboxed programs within the Linux kernel.

“I think most other areas haven't reached that point yet, but it's at least a sign that we _may_ be starting to see the tide starting to turn,” he added. “Don't hold your breath, but maybe cross your fingers we'll be getting there in the not entirely distant future.”

If Torvalds is correct, that’s welcome news. The Linux kernel’s 40-million-plus lines of code makes it one of the world’s bigger software projects. If AI soon stops finding flaws in other parts of the kernel beyond eBPF, perhaps the deluge of fixes developers and users alike will need to handle has peaked. ®

10 Most Perfect Thriller Movies of All Time

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

One simply can't get enough of thriller movies. Fast-paced, heart-pounding, and packed with twists and surprises, the genre feeds off the reactions of audiences as they are pulled to the edge of their seats. A good thriller delivers suspense, but an excellent one reinvents classic thriller elements, all while staying relevant to the anxieties of the era it was released in.

Whether it's the effects of corporate culture, a rookie police officer taking on 30 levels of criminals, or a chef who's sick and tired of serving uppity patrons, these movies show that there's more than one way to deliver the adrenaline. That said, here are 10 intense thriller movies that are perfect from start to finish.

What happens when a revolutionary loses his spark? One Battle After Another introduces us to Bob Ferguson (Leonardo DiCaprio), a former demolition expert for a militant group who, 16 years later, has become a jaded single father to the teenage Willa (Chase Infiniti). But when a secret is revealed about Willa’s true origins, she goes off the grid, prompting the washed-up Bob to reignite the fire he once had.

One Battle After Another has the makings of a classic thriller. However, there is a refreshing comedic edge to him. Considering that he’s not as energetic and physically limber as he used to be, he falls and stumbles as he evades the cops. Bob can barely run without losing his breath, but with his daughter kidnapped, he’s not catching a break anytime soon.

Sinners refuses to be boxed into conventional thriller norms. When twin brothers Smoke and Stack (Michael B. Jordan) open a massive juke joint on the outskirts of town, the last thing they expect is for a group of vampires to crash the party. As the night wears on, these undead intruders begin turning the brothers’ human guests one by one.

Historical, breathtaking, and full of movement, Ryan Coogler has a knack for using horror to bring up broader themes like racial identity and systemic oppression. In Sinners, blues music serves as the voice of the marginalized, giving power to those who play it. Coogler goes one step further by portraying blues as a near-magical force that can literally open portals to the supernatural.

No other thriller has tested an audience’s perception of reality more than Oldboy. For reasons unknown, a drunken Oh Dae-su (Choi Min-sik) is abducted and imprisoned in a sealed room with no communication to the outside world. 15 years have passed, and the now disoriented Dae-su is suddenly returned to society — this time framed for the murder of his wife.

It’s mystery after mystery in Oldboy, but it all boils down to a hyper-specific revenge ploy. Sometimes, death is too swift and merciful a solution — although the movie does boast graphic scenes like the violent hallway fight. Instead, the most effective kind of revenge is one that twists a person’s cognition, leading them to make immoral decisions by their own doing.

Corporate gets cold-blooded in American Psycho. Although modern movies like The Wolf of Wall Street show the high-adrenaline rush from working in investment, Christian Bale’s performance as banker Patrick Bateman is much more polished and restrained — but even more deadly. A swanky apartment and a ridiculous salary aren’t enough to fill his empty void, prompting him to find alternative sources of pleasure.

Thus begins the true thesis of American Psycho — the rich aren’t devoid of feeling because they lack material wealth; it’s because they can’t appreciate what they have. Capitalism emphasizes the idea of having more. Unfortunately, the more Bateman tries to gain — whether status, money, or women — the less satisfaction he finds, until he finds something that cannot be bought: the ability to control whether someone lives or dies.

The only thing that’s separating a 20-man SWAT team and one crime lord in The Raid is a 30-story rundown apartment. But like Dante’s nine circles of hell, each level in the building is infested with all sorts of criminals. From drug dealers to machete-wielding killers, rookie officer Rama must conquer the threats on each floor before getting his hands on the feared underworld kingpin, Tama Riyadi.

There is a non-stop, relentless pace that drives The Raid from start to finish. The not-so-fun part about raiding a high-rise building is that there is practically no way of escaping. The hallways are tight and narrow, meaning that any criminal could just jump on Rama from whichever direction, amplifying the anxiety from being only literally one step away from being stabbed by a knife.

There's no knowing where a Quentin Tarantino thriller might go, and the in-your-face Inglourious Basterds is as unpredictable as they come. Lt. Aldo Raine (Brad Pitt) is responsible for his "Basterds," a crew of Jewish-American soldiers whose job is to infiltrate Nazi spaces and kill them. Somewhere in the French countryside, Shosanna Dreyfus (Mélanie Laurent) has a personal vendetta against the infamous "Jew Hunter," Hans Landa (Christoph Waltz).

Inglourious Basterds doesn’t skimp on the anxiety of being hunted like helpless prey, and much of that tension comes from Walt's performance as Landa. Widely regarded as one of cinema’s greatest villains, Waltz plays him as chillingly polite one moment and completely ruthless the next, especially when he suspects he has found a Jew in his presence.

In Parasite, the resourceful Ki-taek (Song Kang-ho) does everything he can to pull his Kim family out of their scrappy, cramped basement apartment. His luck changes when his son, Ki-woo (Choi Woo-shik), secures a tutoring position with the affluent Park family. With no proper qualifications, he sweet-talks his way into the job, gradually bringing the rest of his family into the Parks’ lives.

Parasite challenges the audience’s pre-existing notions about the rich versus the poor. While it may seem unethical for the Kim family to manipulate the Parks, they are simply doing what they can to achieve upward mobility. The Parks, on the other hand, appear kind because they have the resources to afford generosity. The film suggests that privilege itself enables the ability to be “nice.”

A weekend out of the city goes sideways in Get Out. Black photographer Chris Washington (Daniel Kaluuya) accepts an invitation from his white girlfriend, Rose Armitage (Allison Williams), to a family gathering at her home in the countryside. Unbeknownst to him, her parents have a horrifying pastime of hypnotizing and lobotomizing Black individuals as a vessel for immortality.

Get Out reimagines how modern-day slavery would take place. The scariest part is that, unlike in the past, with recorded accounts of white masters being viciously cruel, Rose’s family initially welcomes Chris with open arms. This sense of niceness — almost too nice — strikes a nerve, considering that Chris is the only Black person in the countryside with no outside communication.

Unlike Marco Pierre White and Gordon Ramsay, Chef Julian Slowik (Ralph Fiennes) is cool as a cucumber — the perfect facade to hide his taste for revenge in The Menu. Obnoxious food snob Tyler (Nicholas Hoult) takes his less-than-enthusiastic date Margot (Anya Taylor-Joy) to a group fine dining experience at Chef Julian's secluded island restaurant, only for the night to get bloody.

Despite the terror they face from the disillusioned Chef Julian, there’s something darkly funny about paying $1,250 per person only to end up getting killed. Then again, The Menu is about a service industry man’s attempt to get revenge on the entitled guests who have caused him to lose his passion for the simple joy of cooking. The wealthy will pay anything for an “experience,” and that is exactly what they’re going to get.

Nobody can trust anyone in The Handmaiden. Set in Japanese-occupied Korea in the 1930s, Sook-hee (Kim Tae-ri) is appointed as the loyal handmaiden to the Japanese heiress, Lady Hideko. Unlike the tales of cruel masters, Lady Hideko (Kim Min-hee) shows nothing but compassion to Sook-hee. Unbeknownst to them, the male powers shadowing over them plan to use them as pawns for material and colonial gains.

The Handmaiden is as much of a period piece and a forbidden romance as it is a thriller. Considering the context of the time, the two leading women, despite their class differences, are no less put into precarious positions. For all its sickening plot twists, the bond between Sook-he and Lady Hideko gives the thriller a strong sense of heart. After all, the two are only trying to escape from their oppression, even if that route requires manipulation.

Thank you for subscribing to our newsletter,

Stephen King’s Supernatural Sci-Fi Series Is Officially Back, And It’s The Perfect Carrie Follow-Up

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Mike Flanagan's Carrie became the biggest Stephen King hit of the year, and it is all set to be followed up with season 2. However, it will be a while before Carrie season 2 sees the light of day. While waiting for its release, horror fans can now watch another ongoing TV adaptation of a Stephen King book, which has officially returned in 2026.

The show in question, The Institute, has a lot in common with Carrie, and these parallels between the two Stephen King stories will now be even more evident.

Carrie marked Stephen King's literary debut. The Institute, in contrast, ranks among the King of Horror's more recent books as it was published in 2019. Its first TV adaptation of the same name landed on MGM+ and garnered a more mixed reception compared to recent shows like It: Welcome to Derry and Carrie. Still, MGM+'s The Institute performed well enough to be renewed for another season.

Interestingly, The Institute season 1 exhausted the story from the original Stephen King novel. Owing to this, its second season is now expanding the source material's lore. After season 1's developments and season 2's opening arcs, The Institute is already starting to look like the perfect follow-up to Stephen King's Carrie. The two Stephen King stories also share some hidden connections.

Carrie was the first breakout horror novel in which Stephen King used telekinesis as a narrative device to highlight the impact of abuse on a young adult. The Institute not only riffs on the same themes but also significantly raises the stakes. Instead of featuring only one character with the "Shine," it shows how multiple children are gifted with telekinesis (TK), telepathy (TP), and precognition.

The characters in The Institute, however, are subjected to explicit institutional abuse after being abducted from their homes and subjected to immoral experimentation. It almost feels like a sequel to Carrie where the White Commission has realized that more children like Carrie White exist. Now, they have started an "institute" to exploit their powers.

The Institute season 2's episode 1 further leans into these overlaps. It shows how entire systems have been built to exploit children with superpowers. The episode introduces older test subjects who use their abilities to help an organization serve a perceived greater good. This introduction of older subjects suggests that humans with TK and TP have been around for a very long time.

Its origins can probably be traced back to Carrie White.

Season 2's episode 1 also reveals how the overarching corporate-backed project is trying to develop an AI that could replace humans with precognition, TK, and TP. However, AI is just not as effective as humans. Mike Flanagan's Carrie also ends with the titular character surviving, paving the way for season 2.

It is hard not to see how her story will be similar to the young characters from The Institute who are struggling to escape the clutches of a powerful shadow organization.

Stephen King never draws any explicit connections between The Dark Tower, Carrie, and The Institute. However, it is hard not to notice many clear overlaps between all three. For instance, in The Dark Tower universe, all psychic abilities are manifestations of a single cosmic force called the "Shine."

In The Dark Tower book series, specifically in Wolves of the Calla and The Dark Tower, characters with psychic powers are called are held captive by the Crimson King and referred to as Breakers. The Breakers are collectively expected to use their powers and chip away at the Beams of reality. Even the characters in The Institute are expected to do the same through quantum manipulation.

Put simply, in the grand scheme of Stephen King's larger horror universe, Carrie tells the foundational story of young adults with superpowers. The Institute and Firestarter step things up by featuring organizations that exploit these powers. Finally, The Dark Tower walks through the cosmic purpose of these powers, revealing why those with the "Shine" are so sought after in every Stephen King story.

Carrie serves as the first step for Mike Flanagan to establish a larger Stephen King universe on the small screen. He will double down on these connections and overlaps in Carrie season 2 and his highly-anticipated Dark Tower adaptation.

The Institute, released in 2025, follows 14-year-old genius Luke Ellis, who is kidnapped and wakes up at a mysterious facility filled with children possessing unusual abilities. Meanwhile, Tim Jamieson, a former police officer, seeks a fresh start in a nearby town, inevitably intertwining his fate with Luke's.
