At Last, The MCU Is Officially Setting Up The Best Doctor Doom/Scarlet Witch Story

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Marvel Studios is preparing for the debut of Robert Downey Jr.s Doctor Doom in Avengers: Doomsday, which is set to release at the end of this year. However, an exciting new theory suggests Doom's arrival in the MCU may also involve the return of The Scarlet Witch.

Played by Elizabeth Olsen, Wanda Maximoff's Scarlet Witch has been absent from the main MCU continuity since 2022's Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness, which saw Wanda controversially being corrupted by the Darkhold in a bid to reunite with her sons from a different reality (no matter the cost). Eventually, Wanda was shown how far she'd fallen, prompting her to sacrifice herself by bringing down Mount Wundagore, destroying every copy of the Darkhold in the process.

That said, many fans have never stopped believing in Wanda's eventual return to the MCU, especially with the rest of her family still having such prominent, ongoing roles. Between the new VisionQuest series on Disney+ and the reveal of mysterious mystical characters beyond Victor von Doom in Avengers: Doomsday, there's plenty reason to suspect that Marvel Studios has secret plans for the Scarlet Witch (which may involve Doctor Doom himself).

Beginning with 2021's WandaVision, Wanda's grief losing Vision (Paul Bettany) in Avengers: Infinity War led to her inadvertent creation of a massive hex over the town of Westview, transforming it into her TV-perfect happy ending, while also manifesting a new Vision and their twin boys from the comics, Billy and Tommy. By the end of WandaVision, Wanda undid the hex despite the fact it meant losing Vision and here boys. This is what ultimately led to her corruption by the Darkhold trying to get her boys back via another reality and subsequent death at Mount Wundagore in 2022's Multiverse of Madness.

However, the story of Wanda Maximoff's family has very much continued even without Wanda herself. In 2024's Agatha All Along, it was revealed that Billy used his magic to transfer his soul into the body of teenager William Kaplan (Joe Locke) before the Hex was being undone. He then did the same for Tommy's soul with Thomas Shepherd (Ruaridh Mollica), a key parallel to their origins from the comics.

While Agatha All Along ended with Billy/William and the ghost of Agatha Harkness searching for Tommy/Thomas, we now have 2026's VisionQuest starring the original Vision (rebuilt by SWORD at the end of WandaVision), as well as Tommy himself who's gradually been learning from his synthezoid "father" about Wanda, the events of Westview, and their family.

With VisionQuest just days away from its finale, Vision and the Maximoff twins finding each other again feels increasingly likely. Combined with Vision starting to regain the humanity and emotion he temporarily lost after being reactivated, nearly all of the pieces are in place for a major Maximoff family reunion in the MCU's future except one: Wanda.

While she seemed to have died under the collapse of Mount Wundagore, The Scarlet Witch is one of the most powerful beings in the entire Marvel Universe. As such, it's not much of a leap in logic to suggest that she could have survived (or was perhaps even rescued by someone seeking her power...)

Beyond what's been happening on the small-screen for the surviving Maximoffs, the ambiguity and hopes about Wanda's return have never been higher thanks to Avengers: Doomsday and its masked "Latverian Witches" who will apparently be serving Doctor Doom.

Their exact identities and purpose of these witches in the upcoming MCU crossover have yet to be confirmed, and they may very well just be generic servants of Doom (a master sorcerer in his own right). However, they've notably been shown wearing different colored robes including green, purple, orange, and red, leading many to wonder if established magical characters like Sylvie, Agatha, Clea, or Wanda herself could be under Doom's influence.

Choose the movie with the higher Rotten Tomatoes score.

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Thinking about Wanda in particular, Doom would have certainly had the power to secretly intervene to save Wanda at Mount Wundagore, perhaps in a bid to bring her into his service and plans for the multiverse at large. There's also some key comics precedent for this idea as well, as seen in Marvel's classic "Children's Crusade" storyline.

In "Children's Crusade", Doctor Doom secretly takes in an amnesiac Wanda from Mount Wundagore after her major breakdown that resulted in her magic triggering the near-genocide of mutantkind (House of M). Hoping to marry Wanda and use her power for his own ends, the intervention of Wanda's boys, their fellow Young Avengers, and several Avengers and X-Men alike prevented Doom from taking full control of the Scarlet Witch's power and her memories being restored.

While none of this confirms that the MCU's Wanda Maximoff is indeed one of these Latverian Witches in Doomsday, being onee of the masked magic wielders would provide a very natural way for the MCU to kick-off a new storyline inspired by "Children's Crusade", perhaps even one that continues beyond the Multiverse Saga itself.

The basic premise of "Children's Crusade" feels remarkably perfect to the MCU's future. While VisionQuest can bring Billy and Tommy together with Vision, Avengers: Doomsday can establish Doom's true purpose for these Latverian Witches, and whether one of them is indeed Wanda Maximoff. Perhaps the reveal will even saved for the end of Doomsday or even in 2027's Avengers: Secret Wars to then kick off a substantial project in the MCU's next major saga.

Most importantly, this major MCU thoery would go a long way to redeem the events of Multiverse of Madness and Wanda's controversial (and underveloped) turn to the darkness. Likewise, throwing Doctor Doom into the mix even just to start would not only keep the Maximoff Family's story tied to the greater events happening in the MCU, but would also provide the perfect narrative launchpad by adapting the basic elements from "Children's Crusade".

It may be some time before we get it, but the twins' reunion more than likely approaching and Doctor Doom's mysterious magical followers definitely makes it feel like a solid foundation is being formed for Wanda's long hoped-for return in the MCU.

The finale of VisionQuest begins streaming November 10th on Disney+. Avengers: Doomsday releases in theaters on December 18th from Marvel Studios.

Lead designer Bruce Nesmith says Skyrim was a hit because it was built on institutional knowledge from Oblivion

Maybe laying off people who know what they're doing is bad, actually.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I don't know about you, but I'm not a huge fan of the way YouTube encourages everything becoming a video that's over an hour long and doesn't have a text version available. There's a recent video putting the original Pool of Radiance into the greater context of the history of CRPGs that's almost 10 hours long, and the odds of me watching more than 10% of it are perishingly low.

Fortunately for you, we're here to convert video to text for your edification, as with The Examined Game's comprehensive interview with Skyrim lead designer Bruce Nesmith. Asked how a game as successful as Skyrim came to be, Nesmith emphasized the importance of keeping the team together.

If I had an actual blueprint on how to do it, I would be one rich son of a bitch. A lot of it comes down to we had the same team that had worked on Oblivion. The studio had not really changed dramatically. So we were able to deal with institutional knowledge at a high level that had been retained, which allows us to not make mistakes and build on successes. That was definitely key to it.

I think also we gave a high priority to making sure that the world was full. There was nowhere that you went where there wasn't at least something fun to see if not something fun to do. And it and it can be just little things, it can be a little vignette on the side of the road. It doesn't have to be a huge big adventure, but the visual storytelling was fantastic.

I go back to that sense of freedom once again, but we accelerated that in Skyrim compared to Oblivion. It makes the world feel rich when there's a variety of experiences. The same experience over and over and over again doesn't cut it. So you have a wealth of enemy types, you've got a lot of different treasure types and loot types, you're allowed to pick everything up in the world.

I have to disagree with Nesmith on the characters, because memorable NPCs are one of Skyrim's weak spots—all those people with recycled voices and limited dialogue. But he's right that Skyrim feels full despite the absence of Ubisoft-style repeatable activities. At no point do you pick up a butterfly and get a notification that says "1/100 butterflies collected". The butterflies are just there, and you can grab them and turn them into potion ingredients if you want. Then while you're out harvesting ingredients you meet a thief or a dying orc or an NPC who points you to a sidequest, and you're off on a new journey. That's what Skyrim does well.

Nesmith goes on to say depth like that was made possible, "by having people who knew what they were doing and could hit the ground running. You didn't have to train people what it meant to make an Elder Scrolls game." Instead of laying off half the Oblivion team the moment it shipped, Bethesda kept them together. "If you look at the history of AAA studios that do sequels, you'll see that repeated," Nesmith continues. "You'll see the best game in a sequence frequently was able to maintain a particular percentage of the staff—was able to maintain a vision."

Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, is a Chinese citizen and former senior executive at Singapore-based data centre firm Megaspeed, which bought more than $4.8 billion worth of Nvidia chips over two years, according to the New York Times. She is not related to Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. A Chinese private fund database lists her as a former TV reporter at Hunan TV and state broadcaster CCTV, an assistant at HSBC’s private banking arm and a vice president at CITIC Securities. The New York Post, however, could not find a CCTV profile for her, and a Hunan TV executive said he had never heard of her. She was first photographed beside Jensen Huang at a Taipei bar in June 2024. A classified memo alleges that China’s Ministry of State Security recruited her to gather information on Nvidia. These claims have not been publicly proven. President Trump called the matter very complex and said officials would look into it closely.

Amy Duggar Claims Cousin Joy-Anna Duggar ‘Will Not Talk’ to Her Amid Scandals

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Amy Duggar is claiming that her cousin Joy-Anna Duggar refuses to speak with her amid the Duggar family’s scandals.

“I wish her well, she will not talk to me, but the door is always open, and I’ve made that perfectly clear,” Amy, 40, wrote in response to a social media user who commented on her Saturday, November 7, TikTok video that made claims about her family. The commenter had noted how much Amy looked and sounded like Joy-Anna, 29, to which Amy agreed.

Amy’s video claimed that despite “a lot of people” not liking her opinion, she believes the Duggar family “loves to smooth things over, [like] when there is a tragic event taking place,” such as her cousin Joseph Duggar being accused of child molestation during a 2020 family vacation in Florida. (Amy has been enthusiastically vocal in her support of Joseph’s alleged victim via social media, and has advocated for her family to be allegedly more truthful as a whole.)

Amy did not offer any further insight into her alleged estrangement from Joy-Anna, who has labeled the allegations against Joseph, her brother, “disturbing” in the past. (Joseph was arrested in March. He pleaded not guilty and requested a jury trial before he was released on a $600,000 bond that same month. He is currently awaiting court proceedings.)

Joy-Anna has not made any direct public statements about Amy, even when news of the first major Duggar scandal, which resulted in her oldest brother, Josh Duggar, being jailed in 2021 for receiving and possessing child sexual abuse material (CSAM), broke.

Amy’s post detailed her perspective on the Duggar family’s culture. “My family, I believe, knows how to say, ‘Everything is rosy, everything is great, we’re wonderful, we’re such a great loving family,’” she said, adding that “they always find somewhere to speak.”

She concluded, “My opinion is, if they’re so great, how come they didn’t protect girls in the first place?”

On the October 26 episode of the “Fire Chats: The Mindfire Pod” podcast, Amy spoke out in support of Joseph’s alleged 2020 victim. “Just as an advocate for children, clearly, you have to believe the person speaking out against him, right? You have to believe the victim,” she said at the time. “I will always stand up for the victim. I will always speak up for the victim.”

Amy added during the episode that she had “reached out” to the girl earlier this year, adding that they have communicated ahead of Joseph’s trial, which has not currently been scheduled. A pre-trial hearing is, however, slated for Tuesday, November 10.

If you or someone you know is experiencing child abuse, call or text Child Help Hotline at 1-800-422-4453.

This Tuesday is the sixth assessment of the ranking of European top scorers for the 2026/2027 season. We thus find the scorers who shook the nets in Germany, England, Belgium, Spain, France, Italy, Netherlands, Portugal and Türkiye. And it’s still Raphinha in the lead. Scorer despite the 2-1 defeat against Real Madrid in the Clasico, the Brazilian has 13 goals in 9 matches this season.

Regular, the Greek Vangelis Pavlidis maintains the rhythm. Against Santa Clara (2-1), he offered himself a new achievement bringing his record to 9 goals in 9 matches. It’s a little better than Victor Osimhen who comes fifth. The Nigerian scored a double yesterday with Galatasaray against Fenerbahçe and has 8 goals in 7 matches. Similar statistics for Niclas Füllkrug who remains on a double with Werder Bremen against Mainz (3-2).

Seventh, Luis Suárez keeps the pace with 8 goals in 9 matches. The Colombian scored on Sunday despite a 2-1 defeat against Viseu. Gift Orban comes eighth and offered himself a new achievement with Amedspor against Samsunspor (2-1). Ninth, Mohamed Salah maintains the tempo with a goal last Monday against Beşiktaş (2-0). He did not score yesterday against Rizespor (4-3). Finally, Yassir Zabiri comes tenth with 8 goals in 10 matches. The Moroccan once again made a splash with Racing Santander against Espanyol Barcelona (2-1).

Lamine Yamal (FC Barcelona), Søren Tengstedt (Go Ahead Eagles) and Brynjolfur Willumsson (Groningen) failed to reach the top 10 with also 8 goals. John Oliver Dunks on Midterms Losers Elon Musk and Haley Stevens

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

After issuing an urgent warning prior to the midterms about the GOP’s increasingly troubling ties to neo-Nazis (with a little dig at Elizabeth Holmes and Billy Evans, of “You Can See Everything” infamy, thrown in for good measure), John Oliver returned to the “Last Week Tonight” desk on Sunday to bask in the Democrats’ blue tsunami that saw them wrest back control of Congress in imposing fashion.

“It has been a busy week and, given the midterm results, a pretty good week, too!” Oliver exclaimed at the top of his HBO show. “Democrats won so big on Tuesday it was sometimes genuinely hard to take all the positive results in.”

Oliver opined how it was “genuinely hard to believe Texas had just turned blue,” adding it was “hard to process” all the wins, including 22 state trifectas, Chris Pappas becoming the first openly-gay man elected to U.S. Senate, Deb Haaland of New Mexico being the first Native American woman elected governor of a U.S. state, and Georgia’s Keisha Lance Bottoms elected the first Black woman governor of a U.S. state.

The comic also applauded how, “Abdul El-Sayed won in Michigan, meaning all the hateful shit thrown his way did not work, and also that this woman [Haley Stevens] imploded her own political career for nothing. Then, you realize Susan Collins lost too! A decade of having to listen to her express reservations about something before going ahead and doing it anyway are finally fucking over.”

But wait, there’s more: “Then it hits you that Elon Musk wasted so much money! The half a billion dollars he spent to try and buy the election just went up in flames faster than a parked Cybertruck. Speaking of which: we also learned this week he’s ending production of Cybertrucks, which isn’t an election result but kind of feels like one.”

Yes, Musk spent nearly $500 million during the midterm election cycle, including $200 million to (unsuccessfully) defeat Senator-elect James Talarico in Texas; while Stevens, a supposed Democrat, backed GOP candidate Mike Rogers over her primary rival (and eventual winner) El-Sayed.

Loud Wealth vs. Stealth Wealth: Why Materialism Fades Over Time

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

I’m a proponent of stealth wealth. It makes for a more peaceful life with lower expectations and less scrutiny. The last thing you want to do is show off and put you and your family in danger.

Stealth wealth is also the natural endgame of following FIRE principles for decades. If you keep living well below your means, which is required to retire early, your investments will eventually compound faster than you can spend. At some point, your passive investment income and returns will surpass your living expenses.

So by default, every long-term FIRE practitioner eventually becomes a stealth wealth practitioner. Whether they mean to or not.

In private, you can do whatever you want. But in public, staying low-key is better.

1) Dress simply. No designer brands or fancy outfits. Clean, comfortable clothes are all you need. Think jeans, t-shirt, warm jacket, sweater, sneakers, baseball hat.

2) Drive an old car.  Driving a new car is stressful and can attract unwanted attention. If you insist on a luxury brand, try to keep it for at least 10 years.

3) Stay quiet about your spending on social media. Don't post vacation photos or the nice things you buy. If a tree falls in Hawaii and nobody sees it on Instagram, did the vacation still happen? Yes, and it was more relaxing.

Do these three things and you’ll join the Stealth Wealth club, a group of operators who move in and out of the shadows unnoticed.

Once you’re in, you’ll realize there are far more members than you think. That realization is oddly bullish. A lot more people are doing better than the media or politicians would have you believe. They’re just not wearing it.

In 8th grade in Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia, I wanted two things: to be cool and to have girlfriends.

My parents drove a 1976 Datsun with missing hubcaps. Getting dropped off in that car was social agony. So I had to find status elsewhere.

At the time, there were two ways to increase your coolness as a teenager: be a good skateboarder or dress in designer clothes. I chose both.

I still remember forking over 160 ringgit, about $64 back then (roughly $150 today), for a used pair of Armani Exchange jeans. 30-inch waist, 30-inch cut length, bronze eagle triangle on the back pocket. That was an absurd amount of money for a 13-year-old to spend on pants. But all the cool rich kids had them, so I had to have them too.

Then came the equally expensive Doc Martens. Then a Guess shirt. Looking back, nearly all my work and allowance money went to clothes and shoes.

And you know what? I enjoyed it. It made me feel good, and I was part of the cool crowd with plenty of friends.

Today, I couldn’t give two shitake mushrooms about designer labels. I want comfortable, practical clothes with zipper pockets so I don’t lose my wallet, phone, and keys. I cut my own hair to save time and money, and I usually cover it with a frayed baseball cap anyway.

So what happened between Armani jeans and black running pants?

I stopped needing respect from strangers.

I’m writing this post because of a clip from author Min Jin Lee explaining why some people with little money spend lavishly on material things. As personal finance enthusiasts, we shake our heads at such lavish spending. Don’t they want to eventually be financially free? But we’re missing something important.

Her point: when you grow up in a poor community, respect matters enormously. It becomes a kind of currency. And when you don’t have much else, you tend to overvalue it. So you buy the nicer clothes, shoes, watches, and cars, hoping they command respect, even if it means carrying credit card debt.

This resonated with me. Respect, especially for elders, is a big part of my culture. When people disrespect me online or in real life, it can be infuriating. In my younger years, I’d go after them to defend my honor.

When you think about it this way, materialism isn’t irrational. It’s a logical strategy for buying respect when you don’t have other ways to earn it. With this understanding, I'm going to try and be extra respectful to everybody I interact with from now on.

At 13, I spent a fortune on Armani jeans to earn respect from my skater friends and the girls I liked. When I started working in finance, I dressed to earn respect from my peers and bosses.

Then I left finance in 2012. Overnight, the nice clothes went untouched. I no longer needed respect from anyone outside my home.

Funny enough, they're all still hanging in my closet 14 years later in pristine condition.

Today, the only people I want respect from are my wife and children. Having respect from my parents and sister would be nice too, but we don’t see each other day-to-day so it’s no big deal if not. As any parent and provider knows, taking care of a family is hard work.

As a result, on our family trip to Las Vegas, I confess that a part of me felt disrespected when my wife gave me a bothersome look when I asked her to take a few more profile photos of me at Redrocks.

In that moment, my brain immediately pulled up the receipts. I agreed to this $7,000 trip despite being in a liquidity crunch. I went to bed at 2 am and still woke up by 6 am to get everyone breakfast. What’s the big deal?

Yes, I realize feeling disrespected in that moment might sound ridiculous. But that’s the point. A man wants to feel respected by his wife and children he provides for.

Reaching financial independence doesn’t eliminate the desire for respect. It just shrinks the circle of people whose respect you care about. The flip side is that when you don’t get respect from the few people who matter most, it stings far more than any stranger’s opinion ever could.

It’s worth doing an honest self-assessment of your spending on material things.

Maybe you genuinely love watches, cars, clothes, shoes, jewelry, or handbags. That’s fine, as long as you can comfortably pay cash and never carry a revolving credit card balance.

But if you’re buying expensive things you don’t even use or love, you may still be chasing status to earn respect. Maybe the midlife sports car is compensating for something. Maybe the designer wardrobe is a response to someone who once made an unkind comment about your body. Or maybe the nonstop social media highlight reel is an attempt to prove the middle school bullies wrong.

Whatever the reason, it’s worth exploring. Because the people you’re trying to impress are probably too busy trying to impress someone else.

For many of us in the FIRE community, the desire for stuff fades over time because the need for outside respect fades too. Once you’re living the life you want and own the things you need, strangers have nothing left to grant you. Nobody can hand you a better life than the one you’ve already built.

That’s the real luxury of stealth wealth. Not hiding your money, but no longer needing an audience for it.

Readers, did you go through a loud wealth phase when you were younger? What was your version of the Armani jeans? Do you think materialism is really about respect, or is something else going on? And now that you’re older, whose respect do you still care about, and how do you feel when you don’t get it?

Stealth wealth means nobody else needs to know your net worth. But you should.

Nvidia’s board has set up a special committee of independent directors, supported by outside lawyers, to review allegations involving CEO Jensen Huang. The move follows reports that Huang had a relationship with Alice Huang, a former senior executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed International, whom US intelligence agencies reportedly suspected of having ties to Beijing. Here Are 5 Of Today's Best Gas Sport Compacts, Ranked By Horsepower

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Enthusiasts like to moan about how performance cars aren't as common or affordable as they used to be, but if you actually look at the market and account for inflation, we're sort of living through a golden age of sport compact cars right now. Toyota, Honda, Volkswagen, Subaru, and even Hyundai all make go-fast versions of their entry-level compacts. They're all scrappy, front- or all-wheel-drive machines that have hatches, trunks, and back seats, and most are still available with manual transmissions.

Some are easier to get ahold of than others, but for now, they all sticker at or under $50,000. They all make around 300 horsepower, an amount that we at Jalopnik have determined is the correct amount of horsepower. And we've lined up the best gas-powered sport compacts on the market right now (sorry, Ioniq 5 N Stans), ranking them on how many ponies their little engines put out.

To the continued disappointment of its fans, Subaru hasn't made a bona fide WRX STI since 2022, but the less powerful, current WRX still arguably lands in, or at least close enough to, the 300-horsepower ballpark. Subie's street-legal rally machine makes 271 hp out of its turbocharged 2.4-liter flat-four engine. You can get it with a six-speed manual, but also a continuously variable transmission (CVT) that the automaker euphemistically calls the Subaru Performance Transmission if you so choose.

This being a Subaru, symmetrical all-wheel drive is standard, and my brief time behind the wheel confirms that it is quite good on a loose surface. On-road, its inputs feel looser than those of its rivals, the powertrain can feel a tad agricultural, and as you'd probably assume from the numbers, it isn't quite as potent. On this list, the WRX may come last in terms of power, but if it's any consolation, it costs a good amount less than the top performers. A new base trim introduced for 2026 starts at just $33,690 (including a $1,195 destination charge).

Also keeping the affordable sport compact flame alive is the $36,595 Hyundai Elantra N. This car's turbocharged 2.0-liter four-cylinder makes 276 hp if you opt for the six-speed manual, and the power is the same most of the time with the available seven-speed dual-clutch automatic. The latter, however, comes with an "N Grin Shift" push-to-pass-style boost system that amps things up to 286 hp temporarily. That's still not enough to shift its ranking on this list, but an extra 10 hp is an extra 10 hp.

In practice, raw speed is one of the less notable aspects of the Elantra N experience. It sounds rude and burbly, its limited-slip differential, sticky tires, and huge brakes make it an absolute treat to carve up a backroad in, and despite being on the cheaper end of the spectrum, it has one of the nicer interiors in this class. Hyundai has already unveiled the new-gen Elantra, which means an N version of that is very likely around the corner, but this one is still a good deal while it lasts.

Speaking of corners, the Toyota GR Corolla is a car that absolutely loves 'em. Can't live without them, really. It's still a firecracker down the straights, though, pumping out 300 horsepower on the dot out of its 1.6-liter turbocharged three-cylinder — yes, three-cylinder. It's the only car on here that doesn't have four pistons. A six-speed manual is the standard gearbox, naturally, but Toyota added an available eight-speed auto starting from the 2025 model year.

Out of all the cars on this list, the GR Corolla feels the rowdiest to drive. The least refined, the least luxurious, and the least concerned about comfort, tech amenities, or cargo space. But that's arguably where its charm lies. That three-cylinder engine always sounds like it's about to explode, while GR-Four AWD and Torsen limited-slip diffs front and rear make it feel like the rally machine that it is, constantly. But if it's pure horsepower you're after, there are a couple of hot hatchbacks that trounce it. A 2026 Toyota GR Corolla with the manual transmission starts at $41,815.

One of the cars that trounces the GR Corolla (relatively speaking, of course) is the mighty Honda Civic Type R, which makes 315 hp out of its turbocharged 2.0-liter four-cylinder engine. Like the Elantra, power goes to the front wheels only, and the Type R has the distinction of being the only vehicle here to be offered exclusively with a manual transmission. Once you've driven it, though, that decision makes a whole lot more sense, because not only does this car have the best shifter in this class hands-down, it's one of the best shifters ever made, regardless of segment or age. Add in a weapon of a front axle, steering to die for, and a chassis that makes some of the other cars on this list feel floppy, and you have one of the best performance cars available today, period.

This car gets a mild update for 2027 with improved engine cooling, tweaked damping in +R mode, better rev-matching, and a brake pedal that's said to feel more direct. It starts at $49,990, although finding a dealer who will actually sell it to you for that number may take some effort.

As great as that Civic is, it isn't the most powerful gas-powered hot hatch available today. That honor goes to the Volkswagen Golf R, which makes 328 hp out of its turbocharged 2.0-liter, sending power to all four wheels. VW used to sell this with either a six-speed manual or a seven-speed dual-clutch auto, but as of the 2025 refresh, the Golf R is auto-only. Perhaps the same lean toward outright pace that gives it the most horsepower in its class was also responsible for it ditching the manual.

The Golf R is an undoubtedly speedy vehicle. Behind the wheel, it feels relatively composed and like a buttoned-down brand of hot hatchery, which pairs well with its very practical, luxury car-adjacent interior. On the flip side, that also means it likely won't be your first choice if you're looking for an unapologetic sense of theater or fun. But if you must have the one car in this class that'll win the most spec sheet comparisons, the VW Golf R is your guy. The 2027 Volkswagen Golf R starts at $51,320 (assuming the $1,275 destination charge carries over from 2026).

How Smart Glasses Detector Apps Work, and How Much You Can Trust Them

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Detector apps can identify Meta’s glasses and similar devices by their Bluetooth signals, as long as they’re switched on, nearby and broadcasting.

Bluetooth devices announce themselves to nearby phones with short broadcast signals, which is how your earbuds show up when you open your phone’s Bluetooth menu. Smart glasses do the same thing, and apps such as Zuckoff listen for signals that match known models, then send an alert when one turns up nearby.

It’s a clever workaround for glasses designed to blend in. How useful it is depends on what you want it to tell you: The app can detect that a pair is close by, but not whether it’s recording. Still, that’s more notice than most people get today, especially if they haven’t spotted the glasses’ built-in LED indicator

Here’s how those apps work, what they reliably catch and what they miss.

Broad concerns over privacy have led to a technical battle. On one side, hardware modifiers are tweaking wearable cameras to make them even harder to spot, often by disabling LED indicators. On the other, developers and privacy-focused coders are launching counter tech to give you a heads-up when a hidden camera walks into the room.

Zuckoff, whose name takes a direct jab at Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg, has joined a small ecosystem of apps such as Antizuck Smart Glasses Scanner and Nearby Glasses that offer similar functionality — letting you know when someone has smart glasses activated and how close the device is, although they can’t tell you specifically whether someone is actively recording or using AI to pull up information. The apps detect approximate device proximity by sweeping local Bluetooth frequencies to locate vendor IDs, system identifiers and hardware signatures associated with smart glasses.

As Zuckoff’s website notes: “Glasses advertise loudest while powering on, pairing or leaving the case. Once paired to their owner’s phone, they can go quiet, so silence is not proof that nobody is recording, and a detection is not proof that anyone is. Signal strength gives a rough distance and no direction at all.”

Zuckoff’s developer, Pawel Szydlowski, said he was already working on projects that used Bluetooth to detect nearby devices and discovered the tech could be applied to smart glasses. “It’s just the same technology wrapped in a different paper,” he said in a video chat with CNET.

Recent reports have shown that smart glasses are being used for everything from bullying in schools to viral videos with unwitting subjects who don’t know they’re being recorded.

What drove Szydlowski to actively develop a software detector was seeing social media videos of people wearing smart glasses who recorded pranks or harassed women with pickup lines. None of the targets seemed aware that their content would appear online, which made Szydlowski angry. “There was not even a blur involved to try to censor them, to give them some sort of feeling of privacy,” he said. “These days, everybody feels entitled to post everything.”

Since it launched in early August, the Zuckoff app has gotten 2,000 sign-ups, and Szydlowski is beta-testing an Android version he hopes to make available on the Google Play Store. The app also has a premium version for $10 a year that can run continuous detection checks in the background when the app isn’t in use.

But a booming ecosystem of DIY modifications and third-party hardware hacks is also giving smart glasses wearers unprecedented stealth by allowing them to disable the activation lights, either by bypassing the capture LEDs or by patching software to suppress system notifications.

Tech journalist Joanna Stern hosted a video showing the process in which a local hacker removed the LED light from her Ray-Ban Meta glasses for $100, allowing the wearables to secretly record without notice.

Detecting active smart glasses in public or private spaces adds a new layer of security awareness, but it raises an obvious dilemma: What do you actually do with that information? Once a detector app alerts us that smart glasses are nearby, it seems our only choices are to either leave the space or ask the wearer to remove the glasses.

Uttara Ananthakrishnan, an associate professor of information systems at the University of Washington who studies the societal impacts of technology, says we’re still figuring out the social norms around wearables like smart glasses. It’s impossible to know whether the person filming will behave responsibly, and the question of consent gets even thornier if children might be captured on video or in photos.

Unlike with smartphones, where it’s easier to tell when someone is recording or taking photos, “smart glasses shift the burden of privacy entirely onto the person being photographed,” Ananthakrishnan said.

“Until we develop clear mechanisms to balance seamless innovation with consent, these devices will continue to turn everyday public spaces into zones of surveillance anxiety,” she said.

That surveillance anxiety may prompt some hardware makers to release alternative versions of smart glasses that entirely avoid potential privacy violations.

The Even G2 smart glasses don’t have a camera for recording photos or videos. They can pair with a separately sold R1 control ring that works as a fitness tracker.

For instance, Even Realities’ G2 smart glasses don’t include a front-facing camera at all. Instead, the Even G2 devices, released in late 2025, focus on providing the wearer with information and acting more like a personal assistant.

“We made the decision from the beginning that smart glasses don’t need to have cameras on them in order to be useful,” Even Realities CEO Will Wang told CNET in an email. “We believe something worn on your face should respect not only the person wearing it, but everyone around them.”

The G2 devices don’t record externally, a design choice Wang said gave the company a unique challenge. “Leaving the camera out also pushed us to think differently about what actually makes smart glasses useful day to day, rather than assuming more sensors automatically make a better product,” he said.

Wang said there’s room in the smart glasses market for different approaches to features and functionality, and that buyers may want to make trade-offs for devices they’ll wear on their faces. But Wang noted that the rise of smart glasses detectors indicates that social acceptance is an issue the industry needs to consider.

Over the last several months, smart glasses have been getting cheaper. And with more models and companies jumping into the fray, the demand for surveillance detectors is likely to grow — that is, unless Big Tech and wearable companies radically change their devices to make them invisible to detector apps.

Zuckoff’s Szydlowski said it’s possible a company could shrink the kind of 5G technology to fit in smart glasses, making them less dependent on phones and Bluetooth, but that’s unlikely to happen anytime soon. If Meta or Snap deliberately updated their hardware to hide from detection apps and disable smart glasses’ digital footprint, it would further undercut any claim of privacy accountability.

Szydlowski said his hope is that, in time, Zuckoff won’t be needed at all. He pointed out that the ability to detect nearby Apple AirTag devices eventually became part of the operating systems on iOS and Android devices. If consumers pressure manufacturers and their governments to make smart glasses easier to detect, it would be “the perfect solution” to make his app obsolete, he said.

tBTC bridge partially resumes as $120M Aave reserve remains frozen

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Threshold halted its tBTC bridge on Oct. 30 as operators addressed a critical Linux kernel vulnerability that could allow attackers to execute code on unpatched servers.

Threshold Network’s tBTC bridge has resumed onchain operations after a nine-day security pause, but an Aave reserve holding about $120 million in tBTC remains frozen to new deposits and borrowing against the token.

On Sunday, Threshold said the bridge’s underlying network was restarting as security-patched node operators reconnected. These independent operators collectively authorize Bitcoin movements and process deposits and redemptions.

Requests submitted before the shutdown are expected to be processed as more operators return, but the app will not accept new deposits, minting or redemptions until enough nodes are online. Threshold did not announce a restart date.

Threshold initially halted the bridge on Oct. 30 while operators addressed a critical Linux kernel vulnerability that could allow remote attackers to execute code on unpatched servers. The project said it found no evidence the flaw was exploited against tBTC nodes.

Aave froze its tBTC reserve on Ethereum Core on Oct. 31 to prevent additional exposure while redemptions were unavailable. The restriction stops new tBTC deposits and the token from adding borrowing power, while existing users can still withdraw and repay loans.

Threshold said roughly 4,320 BTC remains in bridge wallets against about 4,185 tBTC in circulation, with no unauthorized Bitcoin transfers or token minting detected. Aave risk provider LlamaRisk said it would propose restoring the reserve after Threshold’s operations return to normal.

Series A In 2026: Capital Is Flowing Faster, But Fewer Startups Are Making The Cut

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

More money is pouring into Series A rounds, but it isn’t opening the door to more startups. U.S. companies have raised $42 billion at that stage this year through October, up roughly 60% year over year, while deal counts remain on pace with the past few years, Crunchbase data shows.

The numbers point to an increasingly divided early-stage market. AI is fueling demand for capital and helping some startups grow at unprecedented speeds, while others struggle to turn their first customers into repeatable sales.

Investors are responding by moving in opposite directions: Some are writing much larger checks at seed to get ahead of the competition, while others are waiting until Series B or beyond for clearer evidence that a business is taking off. (At Series B, too, we’re seeing a similar trend of fewer but larger deals.)

That leaves companies still proving their growth potential navigating a Series A funding crunch even as capital floods into the fastest-growing businesses.

The low point for Series A funding in the past five years was 2023, with $23 billion invested in U.S.-based startups. Series A investment totals each year since have grown, doubling this year compared to 2023.

At the same time, round counts have settled, averaging 1,200 companies funded at Series A at $1 million or more in 2023 through 2025. This year, the trend is holding, with round counts in that range, give or take 100 deals.

“On the application side, it’s a bit harder, and that’s where I think there’s more of a Series A crunch,” according to Siddharth Ramakrishnan, a partner at Scale Venture Partners, which primarily leads Series A and B rounds.

This dynamic — steady deal count despite much more money flowing — is also impacting how early-stage VCs invest.

Among investors, there’s more competition, especially at the early stage. In turn, VCs are increasingly pursuing one of two opposing strategies: either writing much larger checks much earlier, or holding back until later rounds, Ramakrishnan said.

With the first strategy, check sizes are getting much bigger, he noted, with $10 million to $20 million seed deals not uncommon. “Instead of waiting till the Series A, where they have $4 million in revenue and growing, you’re going to do it when they have $500,000 in revenue, they have a pilot, the team is smart,” he said.

Other investors are waiting to invest later. “ ‘I’m not getting any new information by the time they’re at $2 million in revenue from $500,000, so I’m just going to wait until they actually take off,’ and then you’re seeing a gap,” Ramakrishnan said, explaining the thinking behind this investment approach. “People are putting in a lot of money at the Series B, Series C onwards, because that’s when you get the signal that things are really working.”

Scale expects there to be a bit more variance in this current fund. Its preference is to stay at Series A and B risk profile, by “potentially pulling a little bit earlier and a little bit later and recreating it in the aggregate,” he said.

The firm’s most recent fund 8 is its largest to date at $900 million, up from its prior $600 million fund.

“We still think that Series A is a great place to be if you can make it happen, because it’s at that point where you ideally have enough information to make an informed bet, but not enough that there’s still some of the growth investors that are scared to come in,” Ramakrishnan said, “and so you get great companies at great prices.”

At each quartile, U.S. Series A rounds have trended up in size this year, Crunchbase data shows. So far in 2026, 10% of Series A rounds are $75 million and over, up from $50 million in 2025.

Those much-larger checks are translating into hypergrowth for some companies. “Instead of going zero to $7 million, which in the old world would have been amazing numbers, now there’s people going zero to $20 million, or zero to $30 million,” Ramakrishnan said.

For founders, securing those larger checks often hinges on turning a handful of early customers into a repeatable sales process. Many startups get stuck in that transition, even as investors compete to back those that break through.

“If you have the ambition to build a world-class company, then you also need to raise a big Series A to expand and go abroad, take on different types of markets,” according to European seed investor Anke Huiskes of NP-Hard Ventures, whom I interviewed on-stage at the HumanX Amsterdam conference on AI earlier this year.

“I think the whole messy phase is in this go-to-market phase where you don’t really know who your ideal customer profile is,” said Huiskes, who invests at the seed stage in product-focused founders.

She’s seen startups get stuck when they have a few customers but aren’t growing fast enough. “A lot of the companies hang out here,” she said.

But for the companies who build that repetitive sales cycle, VCs are eagerly reaching out, she said: “And then I get a text, ‘Can you please give me an intro?’ And all of a sudden, I have 10 term sheets. So it’s really black and white.”

Paper Unicorns Are Cheap. Building A $1B Revenue AI Company Takes A Multimarket Cap Table

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A $1 billion valuation still makes a startup a unicorn on paper. Yet when 250 companies joined The Crunchbase Unicorn Board in the first half of 2026, the speed of wealth creation raised another question: How many of these businesses can keep their customers once the initial excitement fades?

I would set a higher benchmark for unicorns with $1 billion in revenue sustained by customers who keep paying.

I spent five years at Harbin Institute of Technology and Shanghai Jiao Tong University, where “fail fast, improve faster” shaped my approach to building technology. Having studied in China before building an AI company in Abu Dhabi, I have also seen how the same growth story can raise different questions among investors, eventually boiling down to: “What will customers keep paying for?”

Fast revenue deserves attention. Founders who turn pilots into paying deployments are solving a real problem, and strong sales can make the next round easier. An annualized run rate built from discounts, experimental budgets or generous compute subsidies, however, offers little assurance that customers will stay.

Since 2023, the price of hitting a fixed level of AI performance has dropped thirteenfold every year. Startups that just resell model access now face brutal price competition. Look at OpenRouter and its $113 million Series B: developers can already swap between models through one shared interface.

Businesses built around proprietary workflows face a different test. Software embedded in a company’s approvals, records and daily decisions can be far harder to replace than the model doing the underlying work. I would check whether customers renew at full price and expand their contracts. Then I would see whether margins stay healthy after inference costs.

Once founders know who keeps buying, they should raise across regions, because a local backer can double as market entry. Borders barely slow an AI product down. Enterprise sales still depend on local budgets, procurement procedures and relationships. A foreign investor becomes valuable when those connections turn a product demonstration into a paid contract.

The $30 million Series A for London- and Gulf-based 1001 AI offers a useful example. U.S. venture firm Lux Capital led the financing, alongside Saudi PIF-backed Sanabil Investments. The deal cannot establish future retention by itself, but it shows how capital, talent and customers can sit on different maps, with engineers in London and customers in the Gulf.

Ask every investor what they bring besides money. One warm introduction to the right buyer can beat a higher valuation if it turns into a contract you can actually deliver and renew.

The choice between traditional venture funding and sovereign-backed money is often framed as a choice between fast and patient capital. I find the more practical distinction lies in the obligations attached to each check.

In conversations with U.S. and European venture investors, I have often been asked how quickly we could turn product adoption into repeatable sales before competitors caught up.

Venture firms need returns within their fund’s life. That puts commercial momentum and a sizable exit at the center of their investment decisions. The pressure can sharpen execution. It can also backfire when founders who chase fast expansion without counting delivery costs may fill their pipeline with discounted experiments instead of enduring enterprise relationships.

Sovereign-backed investors usually ask me a very different set of questions. They want to know where user data gets processed and which technical capabilities stay in the country. Will local customers still be able to rely on the service years from now?

Some funds carry an explicit domestic development mandate. For them, those concerns can count as investment criteria alongside financial returns. The upside is a partner who cares about local infrastructure and enterprise adoption. The price can be longer diligence and localization spending. Contract terms that make it harder to expand elsewhere can also cause a problem.

Neither mandate is universal. Many sovereign-backed funds pursue conventional financial returns, while traditional VCs can support long enterprise sales cycles. Government-affiliated capital doesn’t guarantee procurement wins, either.

Founders should read the fine print: governance rights, exclusivity requests, promised commercial introductions. Then judge whether the investor’s regional reach is worth the constraints.

The best capital strategy follows the customer’s path, from a working product to a renewal. Venture money speeds up development and sales once the business model works. Regional strategic investors earn their place when a new market’s infrastructure and procurement rules call for local expertise.

I would rather show an investor a small cohort of customers renewing without discounts than a glossy revenue forecast that depends on the next funding round. The numbers to watch are net revenue retention, production deployments and margins after delivery costs.

Market sentiment will keep minting and erasing paper unicorns every quarter. The founding teams that build $1 billion in lasting annual revenue will likely balance venture momentum with regional obligations. Their global cap tables will mirror the enterprise markets they serve.

Ilman Shazhaev is the founder and CEO of Dizzaract, an AI and gaming infrastructure company headquartered in Abu Dhabi and the largest game development studio in the Middle East. His work focuses on distributed AI infrastructure, human behavioral data and the interactive environments in which AI systems are trained and tested. Shazhaev is a United Nations expert under the UNODC program, advising on how AI can help solve social problems in developing economies. He is also the author of 46 scientific articles, including published research on AI that reads a player’s psychological state from ordinary gaming hardware, and holds 10 registered invention patents.

Why Boeing 777 Freighter Conversions Are A Stopgap Built On A Shrinking Supply Of Used Jets

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The Boeing 777 is helping the global air cargo industry address a growing shortage of large freighters, but the aircraft providing that additional capacity are becoming very difficult to find. As passenger airlines retain their widebody jets for longer and new-build freighters face production and delivery constraints, three conversion programs are competing to turn used 777s into dedicated cargo aircraft. The irony is that the industry's immediate solution depends on an aging aircraft supply that cannot be replenished indefinitely.

For cargo operators, converted 777s offer an attractive way to obtain substantial payload capacity without waiting for a new-generation freighter. However, their fuel consumption, acquisition costs, and remaining service lives raise questions about their long-term commercial viability. With the Airbus A350F approaching entry into service and the 777-8F still in development, the converted 777 looks like a temporary answer to a problem that will require a more permanent solution.

Growing interest in Boeing 777 passenger-to-freighter (P2F) conversions is closely tied to a shortage of available large widebody freighters. Cargo airlines need more capacity, but the transition from existing production programs to next-generation aircraft has created a gap they can't fill immediately. Production constraints, certification delays, and changing emissions requirements have made used passenger aircraft an important source of additional capacity.

The 777-300ER variant is particularly attractive because of its large fuselage, substantial structural capabilities, and established operational history. Originally designed for long-haul passenger services, the aircraft offers the dimensions and performance needed for demanding cargo operations. Converting an existing passenger aircraft also lets operators add capacity without waiting for a completely new aircraft to be manufactured.

However, the supply of suitable aircraft is becoming a major limitation. Passenger airlines are keeping their 777-300ERs in service longer, partly because delays affecting replacement 777X aircraft have disrupted fleet-renewal plans. This means conversion specialists must compete for a relatively small number of aircraft that are young enough, structurally sound, and economically viable to modify. The question is whether the remaining supply can support the ambitions of the companies developing these conversion programs.

Three companies have emerged as the principal players in the 777 conversion market: Israel Aerospace Industries (IAI), Mammoth Freighters, and Kansas Modification Center (KMC). Although all three target the same broad market, their programs involve different 777 variants, engineering approaches, and development schedules. Their progress also illustrates how quickly the industry is trying to expand conversion capacity before suitable passenger aircraft become even harder to source.

IAI was the first to establish a commercial operation with its 777-300ERSF, also known as the Big Twin. The aircraft entered commercial service in September 2025, with operators including Kalitta Air, Fly Meta and Challenge Group. Mammoth Freighters subsequently secured Federal Aviation Aviation (FAA) certification for its 777-200LRMF in April and delivered the first converted aircraft to Jetran on August 21 for DHL Air UK operations. The company is also developing the larger 777-300ERMF. Meanwhile, KMC is pursuing its 777-300ERCF, which uses a distinctive forward cargo-door design intended to reduce structural weight.

Commercial operations since September 2025

Developing a lightweight forward cargo-door solution

These programs are also expanding geographically. In October, IAI, Ascent Aviation Services and World Star Aviation announced the commencement of 777-300ERSF conversions in North America, with the first aircraft arriving at Ascent's Marana facility in Arizona. The site is expected to operate two parallel conversion lines. Mammoth has also expanded into China through a partnership with STAECO, where the first 777-200LR entered conversion last month. A second line is planned for 2027.

These investments show confidence in the converted 777 market, but adding conversion lines does not automatically increase the number of available aircraft. Companies still need to acquire passenger jets, and customers must be willing to pay substantial sums to secure them.

Boeing is pushing hard to get its answer to the new generation of cargo aircraft out of the factory. However, the 777X delays have trapped its promise

The financial appeal of a passenger-to-freighter conversion is straightforward: an operator buys an existing aircraft, modifies its interior and structure, and puts it into cargo service without paying for a brand-new production freighter. However, the purchase price represents only one part of the investment. The aircraft must undergo extensive structural modifications, maintenance inspections, and potentially expensive engine work before it can begin earning revenue.

According to aircraft consultancy IBA, a half-life 777-300ER converted into a freighter can cost approximately $75 million to $80 million, including the aircraft and conversion work. The investment can approach $100 million if the GE90-115 engines require shop visits. These costs are high, particularly when the resulting aircraft retains the operating characteristics of an older passenger jet rather than incorporating the latest generation of engines and aerodynamic improvements. The aircraft must also have sufficient remaining structural life to justify the expenditure.

Fuel consumption becomes particularly important when operators deploy converted freighters on long-distance routes with high utilization. At the TIACA Air Cargo Forum, Airbus freighter-marketing executive Bernard De L'Estoile described conversions as only part of the solution, arguing that fuel burn matters most when converted aircraft operate frequently over long distances,Air Cargo News reported. His comments should be read in the context of Airbus selling a competing new-build freighter, but the underlying economic issue matters regardless of the manufacturer's commercial interests.

The difference becomes clearer when comparing the Mammoth 777-200LRMF with the next-generation alternatives:

A converted 777 can remain attractive on routes where operators need substantial capacity but cannot justify the investment in a new aircraft. Its economics become less convincing when it flies long-haul services almost continuously, where small differences in fuel consumption accumulate over hundreds of operating hours. The commercial sweet spot is consequently likely to be routes with moderate utilization, not the most demanding intercontinental cargo networks. But if converted aircraft aren't the best long-term option for those networks, what will replace them?

The long-term answer lies in purpose-built freighters designed around modern engines, improved aerodynamics, and dedicated cargo operations. Airbus and Boeing are both developing aircraft to replace older widebody freighters, but their delivery schedules mean converted 777s will remain relevant during the transition. The A350F is currently further along in its development program, while the 777-8F is expected to arrive later in the decade.

Airbus achieved a major milestone on September 29, when the A350F completed its maiden flight. The aircraft flew for four hours and 10 minutes, beginning a flight-test campaign expected to last more than nine months. Airbus is targeting initial deliveries in the second half of 2027, although some industry observers believe the program could slip into 2028. The manufacturer had secured 115 firm orders from 14 disclosed customers by the end of August. The A350F offers a maximum structural payload of 111 tonnes and a range of 4,700 nautical miles, with Rolls-Royce Trent XWB-97 engines.

Boeing's competing 777-8F is designed to carry even more cargo, with a maximum structural payload of approximately 118.2 tonnes and a range of 5,000 nautical miles. Based on the 777X family, it incorporates a composite wing and GE9X engines, offering improved efficiency compared with the current-generation 777F. Boeing also advertises 17% more cargo volume than the existing 777F. However, the program has experienced delays, and the manufacturer is targeting deliveries around 2029, approximately two years after the expected first delivery of the 777-9. Boeing had 81 firm 777-8F orders according to the October industry comparison.

The transition will not happen overnight. Even after the first A350Fs and 777-8Fs enter service, production will need time to ramp up, and operators will need time to integrate the aircraft into their networks. That is why converted 777s have an important window to generate revenue, but the length of that window depends on whether the new-build programs meet their delivery commitments. Another complication is that the existing 777F production line has received additional time, potentially changing conversion demand.

Cargo flats are getting older, and the end is getting further out of sight. With delays more common than ever, when will cargo operators modernize?

Passenger-aircraft availability is becoming the biggest constraint on the conversion market. Strong demand for the Boeing 777-300ER has kept residual values relatively high, while 777X delays have encouraged passenger airlines to retain their existing widebody fleets. Some carriers have even invested in additional cabin refurbishments rather than retiring aircraft they originally expected to replace.

This situation creates competition between passenger airlines and freighter conversion companies. A 777-300ER that remains commercially useful in passenger service may generate more value for its existing operator than selling it to a lessor or conversion specialist. The aircraft's age is not necessarily a reason for retirement, particularly when replacement capacity is unavailable. As a result, conversion companies cannot simply purchase as many aircraft as their production facilities can accommodate. They must wait for suitable jets to become available and compete with other buyers.

Boeing is also developing its own freighter production strategy. The FAA granted Boeing an exemption allowing up to 35 additional 777Fs to receive their first airworthiness certificates between January 1, 2028, and January 1, 2031. This provides a temporary extension to the existing production program while operators await the 777-8F. According to Cirium's October 2026 analysis, the extension could influence conversion demand because some customers were waiting for clarity on continued factory-built 777F availability before making purchasing decisions.

However, the waiver does not eliminate the feedstock problem. Cirium reported 86 firm orders across the IAI, Mammoth and KMC conversion programs, while noting that securing individual aircraft for all those commitments remains challenging. The additional 777F production also represents a limited extension, not an unlimited source of new capacity.

The implications are significant for lessors and operators. If used 777 prices continue to rise, conversion costs could exceed what customers are willing to pay, especially when engine maintenance is included. Meanwhile, the A350F and 777-8F will eventually offer more attractive operating economics for airlines flying intensive long-haul networks. The remaining question is whether conversion specialists can deliver enough aircraft before feedstock scarcity and new-build competition make further expansion increasingly difficult.

The 777 conversion market is entering a period in which demand alone will not determine its success. Cargo operators still need large widebody aircraft, and converting existing passenger jets can remain economically attractive in specific markets. However, the availability of suitable airframes, the cost of engine maintenance and the operating efficiency of older aircraft will increasingly determine which conversion programs can remain competitive. The industry is facing a rather unusual situation: it needs more converted 777s, but the aircraft required to produce them are becoming more valuable to their existing owners.

Upcoming milestones include the A350F's anticipated first deliveries in the second half of 2027 and the 777-8F's expected arrival around 2029. In the meantime, IAI, Mammoth Freighters and KMC will need to secure additional passenger aircraft while managing conversion costs and production schedules. The FAA's allowance for 35 additional 777Fs also gives cargo operators another option during the transition, potentially moderating demand for converted aircraft.

Converted 777s will remain an important part of the global freighter market, but they are unlikely to be the industry's permanent answer to the shortage of large cargo aircraft. Their greatest value may lie in connecting two generations of freighters, providing capacity while manufacturers work through development and production challenges. Whether this bridge can be extended will depend less on how many conversion facilities open and more on how many suitable passenger 777s are available for conversion.

27 Years Later, Science-Fiction's Groundbreaking 4-Part Saga Is Free to Watch on Streaming

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The machines are winning, but at least the movies are free

Some science fiction franchises are so ambitious they become their own cinematic languages. Star Wars built an entire galaxy with a mythology that’s still growing almost 50 years later, while Blade Runner helped define the vision of a far-future Los Angeles through rampant tech advancement and unchecked capitalism. They’re the kinds of franchises that can change the way we imagine the future, influencing everything from fashion and technology to the stories other filmmakers want to tell. Plenty of franchises have tried to capture that same vibe, but few have managed to pull it off quite like The Matrix.

Released in 1999, Lana and Lilly Wachowski’s cyberpunk action thriller introduced one of the most original sci-fi concepts in modern cinema (by shamelessly ripping off cyberpunk literature and golden age anime). The story follows Thomas Anderson (Keanu Reeves), a computer programmer and net surfer living under the hacker alias "Neo," who discovers that the everyday world of the late 1990s is just a complex simulation called the Matrix. Built by sentient machines, this fake reality keeps humanity docile and unconscious while harvesting their bioelectric energy in a real-world wasteland. Neo violently wakes up to the truth, joining forces with a mysterious rebel leader in Morpheus (Laurence Fishburne) and a skilled operative named Trinity (Carrie-Anne Moss). With the entire four-movie saga currently streaming free on Pluto TV, what better way to spend a weekend than by delving back into the digital rabbit hole that changed cinema forever?

Landing on the doorstep of the new millennium, The Matrix hit upon the exact cultural fears and anxieties surrounding the internet at the time. On the cusp of the impending Y2K bug, a widely feared computer glitch that threatened to disrupt everything from banking systems to power grids when the calendar rolled into 2000, the Wachowskis captured a palpable dread about future technologies, transforming fears of rogue code and automated systems into a leather-clad action movie that completely rewired 2000s sci-fi and action cinema. It’s no surprise that The Matrix became the blueprint for a whole decade of internet-obsessed action-thrillers, from Kurt Wimmer’s Equilibrium to Michael Bay’s The Island.

Despite striking gold, the Wachowskis weren't working from a blank slate. Mamoru Oshii's 1995 masterpiece Ghost in the Shell shaped the film's aesthetic and ideas, with the Wachowskis even reportedly showing the film to producer Joel Silver while pitching their vision. The Matrix inevitably combined those influences with Western cyberpunk, religious imagery, and martial arts cinema to create a singular blend of philosophical science fiction and blockbuster spectacle unlike any other. Its famous bullet-time sequences became a sensation, with the iconic rooftop showdown between Agent Smith and Neo still one of its slickest. It was visual storytelling at its finest, physically demonstrating that Neo was finally beginning to understand the rules of the simulation as he surpassed the boundaries of his human limitations.

The original film's breakout success led to The Matrix Reloaded and The Matrix Revolutions, which expanded the mythology into a multi-layered epic exploring the political machinery of Zion. The sequels might not have captured the same cultural lightning as their predecessor, but they deserve credit for refusing to simply repeat the first film's formula. Reloaded introduced more complicated questions about the cyclical nature of the Matrix, while Revolutions brought Neo's conflict with the machines to an appropriately operatic conclusion. The franchise also expanded into the digital realm with The Animatrix, a nine-part anime anthology featuring legendary Japanese directors, as well as assorted video games, and other multimedia projects, creating a sprawling sci-fi universe that showed little signs of dying.

Eighteen years after Revolutions brought Neo’s journey to a definitive close, The Matrix seemingly still had stories left to tell with 2021’s The Matrix Resurrections. Directed solely by Lana Wachowski, the fourth installment reunited Reeves and Carrie-Anne Moss, but its greatest ambition also proved to be its biggest stumbling block. Rather than delivering another nostalgic action spectacle, Resurrections tried to tackle Hollywood’s relentless obsession with reboots and legacy sequels through a self-referential lens. It was a hard sell for fans hoping for another revolutionary sci-fi leap, with the film struggling to strike a satisfying balance between its cerebral commentary and the blockbuster storytelling that made the 1999 film a masterpiece.

Still, an uneven fourth installment can't erase what The Matrix accomplished. Its vision of artificial intelligence and simulated reality feels particularly relevant today, even if we're still waiting for someone to invent a pair of sunglasses that look quite as cool. For anyone who missed the phenomenon the first time around, or simply wants to revisit one of Hollywood's most influential sci-fi universes, there's never been a better excuse to follow the white rabbit again.

Hoshigaki: A Persimmon Delicacy

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

All Gardenista stories—from garden tours and expert advice to hand tools and furniture roundups.

Starting from scratch or upgrading an outdoor space? Look here for advice on plants and hardscape materials. We have 200 guides on everything from fences to foxgloves.

Heading somewhere? Plan your trip with our destination guides to our favorite public gardens, hotels, restaurants, and shops.

Gardenista’s members-only directory of landscape architects and garden designers.

See our obsessively curated catalog of favorite products sourced by the Gardenista editors.

Cool nights, falling leaves, and thoughts of Thanksgiving signal the arrival of persimmons. Bright ornaments in fruit form, they are perfectly timed to brighten days that seem to end abruptly, when Daylight Saving Time ends in November. One of my seasonal rituals is to seek out the fattest persimmons I can find, and transform them into hoshigaki. This East Asian treat is created by peeling and hanging the fruit to dry slowly, aided by an occasional massage that encourages a white bloom of sugar to appear on their surface like a dusting of frost. Their weeks-long transformation yields a dense, richly flavored dried fruit whose flavor and texture are a luxurious and unique delicacy.

Hoshigaki are beautiful to behold, and simple to make.

Any persimmon can be used to make hoshigaki.

Acorn-shaped Hachiya-types are the Asian persimmon that, when firm and unripe, leaves your tongue feeling insulted by its tannins. But this doesn’t matter, for hoshigaki. The drying process turns even unripe Hachiyas magically sweet. In fact, the firmer the persimmon the easier it is to peel; peeling is essential, because it yields the characteristic texture, and the hoped-for coat of sugar. A ripely squishy fruit—while luscious to eat raw—is close to impossible to strip neatly of its skin and will also drip relentlessly until it dries, encouraging mold.

The other well-known Asian persimmon, the Fuyu-type, with a flat bottom, can also be used. The only difference is the finished shape: Hachiyas tend to dry long, while Fuyu look squatter.

Even the (much) smaller native American persimmons make good hoshigaki.

Traditionally, hoshigaki are strung to dry outdoors, under cover. If your house has eaves, now is the time to festoon them with bright orange, edible decorations (although your local squirrels may thank you). But many of us must make peace with indoor drying, and it seems to work very well.

The first hoshigaki I made were hung in a wide, sunny window that I would crack open on nice days. The sugar-bloom on those first fruits seemed magical.

More recently, my hoshigaki have been suspended from the ceiling in a bright nook near a skylight. Sometimes I place a little fan beneath them to boost air-circulation if their surface seems to be remaining sticky for too long.

There is one distinct disadvantage to making hoshigaki Stateside. With very few exceptions, persimmons in the US are sold without a piece of stalk attached. That firm little handle is where, traditionally, the twine to hang them would be tied. We improvise. I use two methods: One is to insert a stainless steel (never galvanized) screw into the calyx at the top of the fruit. It provides an anchor for the string, and does not pull out when the persimmons are suspended (a substantial persimmon can be weighty).

When the hoshigaki are dry I remove the screws, wash them, and save them for the following season.

The other method is to pierce a wooden skewer through the top of the fruit, just under the calyx.

Use as many fruit as you like—you just need the space to suspend them without touching one another.

Soak the skewers or screws in alcohol for 5 minutes (you could also boil them); this is to minimize future mold issues.

For screws: Twist a screw into the top of each fruit. Tie twine to the screw heads. You can tie the fruit on one long piece of twine, like holiday-lights, or individually.

For skewers: Pierce the persimmon horizontally across the top, just under its calyx—the skewer should poke out at each end. Tie string to each side of the skewer.

To clean before hanging: Either dunk the whole stringful into a pot of boiling water and remove at once, or use alcohol: Place the persimmons in a shallow bowl and pour the alcohol over them. You can re-use the same hooch for a whole batch.

Hanging the fruit: I use screws secured in the ceiling as anchors for vertical strings of hoshigaki, or as anchors for light bamboo rods from which to suspend them in horizontal rows (easier for the skewered fruit). Ideally, hang the hoshigaki in a sunny spot with decent airflow. If the space is neither sunny nor breezy, a small fan is close to essential.

When the exterior of the persimmons is dry to the touch, usually after 24 to 36 hours, give each fruit a gentle squeeze all over. As days and weeks pass you’ll notice the persimmons darkening and the interior yielding more and more, like taffy, until you can manipulate the whole fruit without damaging the exterior.

Drying times vary, depending on humidity and the size of the persimmons. Six to 12 weeks is average. The degree of dryness is also a personal preference. Less time, and the hoshigaki will be slightly jammy in the middle, more time and they will turn very firm.

Finished hoshigaki keep indefinitely. I store mine in large mason jars, at room temperature.

Trouble-shooting: The most common issue is mold, which seems to appear on fruit that was very ripe or soft to begin with, and whose exterior stays moist longer. My fix is to brush it off with a clean paper towel or cotton swab and then to paint that spot with a pastry brush dipped in alcohol. Inspect your fruit daily for the first couple of weeks to nip this potential problem in the bud.

N.B.: This post was first published December 2022.

A Revived Catalan-Modernist Apartment: Quintana Partners in Barcelona

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

From house tours and expert advice to favorite finds and DIYs.

Design-forward shops, hotels, and restaurants for your itinerary.

Renovating? Start here for expert advice, deep dives, and helpful tips.

Our catalog of more than 10,000 products featured on Remodelista.

Remodelista’s members-only directory of architects, designers, and landscape professionals.

Several summers ago, our friend Megan Wilson returned from a vacation on the Spanish island of Menorca and introduced us to the work of Quintana Partners, a local interior design firm run by Pol Castells and Benito Escat. Tasked with revitalizing historic houses, the duo do more than respect the existing bones, they inject new life into them by assembling rooms that are all about joie de vivre: see, for instance, Casa Telmo and the QP Way with Kitchens.

The results are the opposite of most “decorated” projects. But make no mistake, plenty of thought and labor goes into every square foot: painted walls are scraped down to their atmospheric under layer, crumbling and non-existent period details are re-created rather than updated, and the duo supply furnishings found all over—but rarely in contemporary showrooms. “The most important aspect of what we do is the reutilization of existing elements in the renovation,” they write. “And we extend that mission to furniture: we believe in giving a second chance to waste.” Translation: they celebrate the used and the vintage.

Demand for their work of late has grown far beyond Menorca, and so the duo recently took over an apartment in a Catalan-modernist building in Barcelona as a live/work second base. Its kitchen and bathrooms, they tell us, are tiny and impossible to photograph, but there’s still plenty to see.

Photography courtesy of Quintana Partners.

“We were looking for a big, beautiful, well-located space,” says Pol, “Catalan modernist is a building style inspired by visionaries like Gaudí that has attracted us since ever.” Located in Barcelona’s Eixample district, the apartment is in a late-19th century family residence with soaring ceilings and moldings typical of fin-de-siècle Spanish design. The space had been  well-maintained and mainly required repair work and paint.
