Magnite vs. Pinterest: Which Media Stock Is a Better Buy in 2026?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

One operates the ad-tech backbone for streaming; the other has built a massive online platform. Their financial profiles and risk exposures also tell very different stories.

As digital advertising shifts toward streaming and social commerce, choosing between Magnite (MGNI -0.47%) and Pinterest (PINS -4.19%) requires a close look at where marketing dollars are moving today.

Magnite serves as the technical backbone for publishers looking to sell ad space, while Pinterest provides a visual search environment where users actively look for inspiration and products. Comparing these two companies helps investors understand the different ways to play the ongoing evolution of the digital ad market.

Magnite provides a sell-side advertising platform for publishers and media owners in the media stocks landscape. As the largest independent player in this space, it helps content creators monetize their inventory across connected television, mobile, and desktop. The company maintains critical partnerships with providers like LG Ad Solutions and Telly, allowing programmatic buyers, including The Trade Desk, to access premium video inventory.

In FY 2025, Magnite reported revenue of nearly $714 million, representing a growth rate of approximately 6.9% compared to the previous year. The company achieved a net income of roughly $144.6 million during this period. This is an increase from the net income of approximately $22.8 million reported in FY 2024, reflecting an expansion in its net margin to roughly 20.3%.

Looking further into the financials, Magnite has total cash of $392.8 million and total debt of $411.9 million. Its operating cash flow for the 12-month trailing period is nearly $300 million.

Pinterest operates a visual discovery and shopping platform that now serves approximately 661 million monthly active users. Unlike traditional social media, the platform focuses on curation, making it highly attractive to advertisers in the retail and consumer packaged goods sectors. By integrating shopping features directly into its pins, the company has transformed from a simple image board into a performance-driven marketing channel.

In FY 2025, revenue reached approximately $4.2 billion, which was an increase of nearly 15.8% over the previous year. The company reported a net income of roughly $416.9 million for the year. This resulted in a net margin of approximately 9.9%, which followed a net margin of roughly 51% in FY 2024 when the company benefited from significant tax adjustments and valuation changes.

For a quick glimpse at the financials, Pinterest has $1.4 billion in total cash and $1.2 billion in total debt. Its operating cash flow for the 12-month trailing period is $1.3 billion.

Magnite faces intense competition from larger, better-funded tech giants such as Alphabet, Amazon (AMZN -0.35%), and Comcast (CMCSA -2.24%). The company is also navigating ongoing litigation with Alphabet regarding ad-tech practices, which could cause operational distractions or financial pressure. Technological shifts, including the removal of third-party identifiers and the rise of new privacy regulations, continue to challenge the way Magnite identifies and targets audiences for its publishing partners.

Pinterest faces significant competition for user attention and advertising budgets from Amazon, Meta Platforms, Alphabet, and smaller rivals like Snap (SNAP -2.55%) and Reddit (RDDT +1.38%). The company is currently involved in a securities class action lawsuit related to previous advertising disclosures. Furthermore, Pinterest remains vulnerable to changes in mobile operating systems that limit tracking capabilities, which can make it harder for advertisers to measure the effectiveness of their campaigns.

Pinterest appears to be more of a value, according to traditional metrics.

Pinterest has built up an impressive monthly user base. And there is something to be said for building your own network and controlling that user base. But Pinterest is caught between being a social media site and an advertiser, whereas Magnite's value is more straightforward. Also, between the two, Magnite is more of a growth stock, whereas Pinterest is seeking more stability, with its stock price down 30% over the last 12 months. In comparison, the Magnite stock price is up 81.3% over the same period.

Analysts also have a more favorable outlook on Magnite: of the 18 analysts who rate Magnite, 89% rate it a buy, with 11% rating it a hold. In comparison, the view is more split on Pinterest. Of the 42 analysts who rate PINS, 50% rate it a buy and 50% a hold.

As a growth stock, Magnite could be hit particularly hard if there were a slowdown in the economy. And heading into 2027, we're seeing concerns about an artificial intelligence bubble and mixed signals about the economy's strength. Still, between the two, I would rather own Magnite as a long-term position because of its sales growth opportunities.

Jack Delaney has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms, Pinterest, Reddit, and The Trade Desk. The Motley Fool recommends Comcast and Magnite. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Palantir vs. Snowflake: The AI Sell-Off Is Exposing a Critical Difference for 2027

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Palantir and Snowflake continue to deliver impressive growth, but soaring AI expectations and renewed market volatility are putting their valuations under scrutiny.

The artificial intelligence (AI) boom has fueled remarkable growth across the technology sector, but the recent sell-off has Wall Street reconsidering how much that growth is worth. For investors looking toward 2027, the pullback presents an opportunity to reassess two leading enterprise AI players: Palantir Technologies (PLTR +3.48%) and Snowflake (SNOW +2.35%).

Both of these companies are benefiting from growing AI adoption, but their distinct business models, financial performance, and valuations present nuanced investment opportunities.

Palantir develops software platforms that help government agencies and businesses integrate complex datasets, improve decision-making, and deploy artificial intelligence. Its Gotham, Foundry, and Artificial Intelligence Platform (AIP) products serve customers ranging from the U.S. military to commercial enterprises.

In fiscal 2025, Palantir generated $4.5 billion in revenue, an increase of 56% year over year. Net income reached approximately $1.6 billion, representing a profit margin of 36.5%. More recent results demonstrate accelerating growth. In the third quarter of 2026, revenue surged 96% to $2.3 billion, while U.S. commercial revenue climbed 148%. Notably, U.S. commercial revenue surpassed U.S. government revenue for the first time, highlighting the company's expanding presence in the private sector.

At the end of 2025, the company carried virtually no debt, with a current ratio of approximately 7.1x. Free cash flow reached $2.1 billion during the year, providing substantial flexibility to fund continued expansion. However, stock-based compensation totaled approximately $684 million, representing 32% of operating cash flow, which could introduce potential shareholder dilution despite the company's strong cash generation.

Snowflake operates a cloud-based data platform that enables businesses to store, manage, and analyze information across multiple cloud environments. Its consumption-based pricing model allows customers to pay for the computing resources they use, while partnerships with Amazon (AMZN -0.35%), Microsoft (MSFT -0.99%), and Alphabet (GOOGL +0.78%) (GOOG +0.78%) support its global reach. As businesses increasingly incorporate artificial intelligence into their operations, Snowflake's platform plays an important role in making enterprise data accessible for AI applications.In fiscal 2026, Snowflake generated approximately $4.7 billion in revenue, an increase of 29% year over year. Still, it was unable to turn a profit and reported a net loss of $1.3 billion, representing a negative profit margin of roughly 28%. However, more recent results demonstrate continued growth. In the second quarter of fiscal 2027, revenue increased 35% to $1.55 billion, while product revenue climbed 37%.

At the end of fiscal 2026, its debt-to-equity ratio stood near 1.2x, while its current ratio was approximately 1.3x. The company generated $1.1 billion in free cash flow, but stock-based compensation totaled $1.6 billion, exceeding operating cash flow.

One of the key risks for Palantir comes from its reliance on large government and enterprise contracts, where lengthy sales cycles and customer concentration can create revenue uncertainty. In addition, its involvement in sensitive defense and surveillance operations also introduces regulatory and reputational risks.

Top risks for Snowflake include intense competition and a consumption-based revenue model that makes growth sensitive to customer spending. Not to mention, ever-growing cybersecurity threats introduce operational and regulatory risks.

Snowflake offers a lower P/S ratio, measuring price against sales over the past twelve months, while Palantir is cheaper relative to future earnings estimates via its Forward P/E.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

From a valuation standpoint, neither stock is cheap. Palantir trades at 103 times forward earnings, while Snowflake commands an even higher multiple of 131 times. Snowflake is considerably cheaper on a price-to-sales basis, at 18.9 times sales compared with Palantir's 54.5 times, but its continued losses and path toward sustained GAAP profitability are less certain.The key difference is that Palantir has demonstrated a stronger ability to turn growing AI adoption into profits. Its latest quarterly revenue surged 96%, driven by accelerating commercial demand, while its strong balance sheet and minimal debt provide considerable financial flexibility.Looking ahead to 2027, Palantir is my preferred buy, although its valuation gives me pause. Investors are paying a substantial premium for future growth, leaving little room for disappointment if the company falls short of expectations. Ultimately, both stocks carry lofty valuations, and the recent sell-off doesn't automatically make either a bargain.

RJ Fulton has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Palantir Technologies, and Snowflake. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

A Recipe for Disaster in Taiwan

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

REBECCA LISSNER is Senior Fellow for U.S. Foreign Policy and Director of the Future of American Strategy Initiative at the Council on Foreign Relations. She served as Deputy Assistant to the President and Principal Deputy National Security Adviser to the Vice President during the Biden administration.

MIRA RAPP-HOOPER is a Visiting Senior Fellow at the Brookings Institution. She was Special Assistant to the President and Senior Director for East Asia and Oceania and Director for Indo-Pacific Strategy at the U.S. National Security Council during the Biden administration.

Just a few years ago, U.S. President Joe Biden caused a stir when he confirmed publicly that his administration would defend Taiwan if the island were attacked by China—a divergence from the traditional U.S. policy of strategic ambiguity, in which Washington does not commit to taking any particular action. In May, U.S. President Donald Trump made waves for taking a different position. In a television interview, he described Taiwan as “a very good negotiating chip” in dealings with Beijing and mused that the island was close to China but far from the United States, implying that U.S. interests in Taiwan were negligible. In doing so, Trump seemed to confirm something many Asia watchers had feared: that this U.S. president would be willing to loosen the United States’ commitment to Taiwan to bolster his relationship with Chinese leader Xi Jinping.

A few days later, Secretary of State Marco Rubio told the Senate Foreign Relations Committee that there had been no change in U.S. policy toward Taiwan. But Trump’s words were alarming nonetheless—in part because they reflect a shift in Washington that goes beyond the Oval Office. Since 2025, politicians from both parties have been shying away from affirming the United States’ long-standing support for Taiwan. Meanwhile, China is developing new strategies to coerce Taiwan without going to war, and Beijing will be increasingly tempted to use them if it judges that the U.S. commitment to Taiwan is waning among both politicians and the public.

Yet that would be a grave miscalculation on China’s part. In fact, the United States’ interests in Taiwan are growing, not diminishing. Whatever the nature of Washington’s rhetoric right now, the United States would have to respond to any action by Beijing that threatened those interests, and both countries would then face a great-power crisis. To defuse this dangerous situation, American leaders must update the United States’ Taiwan strategy to reflect a new reality—and they should begin immediately.

Taiwan’s unique international status has long made U.S. policy complex. The Republic of China, now known as Taiwan, was a World War II ally of the United States. When the People’s Republic of China was founded on the mainland, in 1949, the Republic of China’s president, Chiang Kai-shek, decamped to Taiwan, and the United States maintained formal diplomatic ties with Chiang’s government, not the People’s Republic of China. In 1954, as the Cold War crested in the Pacific, the United States concluded a formal mutual defense treaty with Taiwan, in part to forestall increasingly aggressive artillery barrages from the mainland. Nearly 20 years later, however, when U.S. President Richard Nixon and his national security adviser, Henry Kissinger, began to engineer a diplomatic opening to the government in Beijing, they knew it would cost Taiwan.

In part because they understood that building diplomatic ties with Beijing would be painful for Taiwan and cause controversy in Congress, where Taipei had many boosters, Nixon and Kissinger slow-walked the process. So did Nixon’s successor, President Gerald Ford. It finally fell to President Jimmy Carter to make the change. On January 1, 1979, the United States ended its alliance with Taiwan and recognized the mainland government as “the sole legal Government of China.” Congress was irate. Lawmakers from both parties quickly drafted the 1979 Taiwan Relations Act, which remains in force. The law requires Washington to provide Taipei with defensive arms, creating the trappings of an alliance with Taiwan despite the relationship’s unofficial status. In 1982, the Reagan administration made an additional set of commitments to Taiwan, known as the Six Assurances. These include an affirmation that Washington does not endorse Beijing’s sovereignty claims over Taiwan and a promise that the United States would not use arms sales to Taiwan as a bargaining chip.

In the 50 years since, U.S. Taiwan policy has been remarkably successful. Through its partnership and arms sales, the United States has helped underwrite peace and stability in the Taiwan Strait, and Taiwan has thrived as a democracy and an economy, even as Washington has maintained a relationship with Beijing. But U.S. commitments to Taiwan have lately come under increasing strain. China has proceeded with a massive military buildup designed to give Beijing the option to take Taiwan by force; used coercive economic tactics, launched cyberattacks, and interfered in elections to put pressure on Taipei; and run frequent army and coast guard exercises to demonstrate its ability to strangle the island. All of this has stoked concerns across the Pacific that the United States’ long-standing partnership might not be enough to keep the peace in the Taiwan Strait.

The fear that China could attempt to retake Taiwan by force, possibly during Xi’s tenure, has grown in recent years. Analysts have frequently cited 2027 as the beginning of the window of maximum danger, based on a 2021 statement made by Philip Davidson, the former commander of U.S. Indo-Pacific Command, about when the People’s Liberation Army might be prepared for an invasion. But this timeline was never hard and fast, and Xi’s recent purge of more than 100 officers has likely delayed the military’s readiness. U.S. intelligence assessments do not anticipate an invasion of Taiwan next year, and most scholars believe the Chinese government would consider such a move as a last resort only if unification could not be accomplished by other means.

The primary danger now is that Xi will escalate a pressure campaign designed to coerce Taiwan into negotiating a change in its status. Since 2025, China’s leaders have used their diplomatic channels to the Trump administration, including Xi’s direct line to Trump, to insist on American concessions on Taiwan in exchange for progress in trade talks. In May, China secured a pause on a planned $14 billion U.S. arms sale and other defense funding for Taiwan. Beijing may hope that additional incremental moves will further weaken Taiwanese confidence in the United States and that such actions will be difficult for Trump or a future U.S. president to reverse.

China is also clearly planning for more dramatic forms of coercion. It has a few options: since 2022, China’s military has practiced blockades of Taiwan, and its coast guard has simulated a quarantine of the island’s major ports. Beijing could also use customs enforcement or other administrative measures to attempt to assert its jurisdiction over Taiwan while minimizing the likelihood that the United States would intervene to stop it. If Taiwanese President Lai Ching-te, who favors Taiwanese independence but has not pursued it in office, is reelected in 2028 and Chinese leaders believe that the path to a peaceful unification is closing, Xi may consider using these tools against the island in late 2028 or early 2029, before a new U.S. administration takes office. Given Trump’s alienation of U.S. allies in Asia and Europe and Washington’s limited means to respond to a maritime quarantine, Beijing might hope to get away with such a large-scale coercion campaign without inviting serious pushback.

Taiwan’s growing strategic importance makes Beijing’s development of new coercive options more concerning. Washington is no longer maintaining a partnership with Taipei because of a historical commitment or, as Beijing would have the world believe, to needle China. The artificial intelligence boom has put Taiwan at the center of the global economy. Taiwan is a critical supplier for the U.S. tech industry, and the United States recognizes that it cannot be a leader in AI without Taiwan.

The scale of the United States’ economic dependence on Taiwan is revealing. Taiwan is the country’s fifth-largest trading partner; a Chinese blockade of the island would imperil more than $250 billion in trade and could shave more than three percent off U.S. GDP in a year, according to a Bloomberg report. As much as 97 percent of the global supply of the high-end logic chips used in smartphones and other advanced electronics are produced or packaged in Taiwan. Losing access to Taiwanese chips could reduce advanced electronics production globally by 60 percent, with factories forced to rely on their chip inventories. The United States’ auto and medical device industries could deplete their inventories in a matter of weeks. The U.S. firms Amazon, Apple, and Nvidia are among the Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company’s ten largest customers, which together represent more than $14 trillion in market capitalization. With AI growth buoying the U.S. stock market and the broader economy, a crisis in Taiwan would trigger a crash—potentially one similar in scale to the stock market crash at the start of the COVID-19 pandemic.

The economic effects of a Chinese effort to squeeze Taiwan would not be limited to the United States. The research firm Rhodium Group has conservatively estimated that a yearlong blockade of the island could cost the global economy between $2 trillion and $3 trillion. A full-scale war would be far worse; Bloomberg has estimated that it would cause $10 trillion in economic losses and a global recession. It is therefore increasingly likely that the United States would be forced to act if Taiwan’s supply chains were put in jeopardy. The cost of standing aside would simply be too high.

Yet just when the United States needs to be doing all it can to prevent a crisis, Washington’s support for Taiwan appears to be wobbling. Trump’s musings about Taiwan’s geographical distance from the United States and his use of arms sales as a bargaining chip are the headline examples, but they are not the only indicators of a shaky U.S. commitment. Policy analysts have argued that the United States should change its position to definitively oppose Taiwanese independence or even withdraw U.S. forces from the military bases in Japan and the Philippines that are closest to Taiwan.

Public opinion in the United States is shifting, too. Americans’ feelings toward China have warmed considerably over the past two years, according to a July 2026 Chicago Council on Global Affairs survey. A majority, 57 percent, support friendly cooperation and engagement with China, whereas 40 percent support an active effort to limit the growth of China’s power. The share of Americans who see China’s territorial ambitions as “a critical threat” has decreased by more than ten percentage points since 2022, and those who favor the use of U.S. troops if China invaded Taiwan has fallen from 52 percent to just 38 percent.

In part, the declining support for defending Taiwan reflects Americans’ growing wariness of instability and conflict. A sense that the world was descending into chaos helped fuel Trump’s 2024 campaign; as president, Trump has heightened that sense by taking assertive military action in places such as Iran and Venezuela. Americans are now suffering the economic consequences of nine months of war in the Middle East, further deepening antiwar sentiment, as the midterm election results vividly demonstrate. Candidates for office used to clamor to sound tough on China, but many now blanch at the idea of suggesting that the U.S. military should defend Taiwan against a Chinese invasion, calculating that this is not the time to be seen as a hawk.

Of course, public attitudes can change quickly during a crisis. If China actually committed aggression against Taiwan, Americans could become more supportive of a strong U.S. response. And if the early stages of a conflict saw American casualties or a Chinese attack on U.S. critical infrastructure, the U.S. public could very well rally around the flag and demand a muscular intervention.

For now, however, China may believe that the United States’ will and ability to fight are both diminishing. The war with Iran has substantially reduced U.S. military readiness for a Taiwan contingency. The United States has used up much of its missile defense stock, including roughly two-thirds of its Patriot interceptor inventory, which was already depleted by U.S. aid to Ukraine, and as much as 80 percent of THAAD interceptors against Iranian drones and missiles. Even before the Iran war, analysts had warned that the U.S. military could run out of long-range, precision-guided munitions in a fight over Taiwan; now, the munitions shortfalls have become perilously acute. The war has also drawn military assets away from the Indo-Pacific. In August, Washington moved its last aircraft carrier in Asia, the USS George Washington, out of the region. It has also relocated missile defense systems to the Middle East, including Patriot and THAAD systems that had been deployed in South Korea.

The result is an increased risk of dangerous miscalculation. Should Chinese leaders assess that the United States’ commitment to Taiwan is wavering in both political parties and that the country is ill prepared for a fight, they might conclude that they have an opportunity to pressure Taiwan without inviting an American response. Yet because the United States has a greater interest in Taiwan today than at any point in history, Beijing’s bet could turn out to be wrong. China could announce a maritime quarantine of Taiwan or another coercive measure only to have the United States intervene, potentially prompting a great-power standoff.

Avoiding such a showdown will require American leaders to recommit to deterrence. First, Washington needs to update the way it communicates its positions. In their public remarks, policymakers should clearly state that the U.S. economy’s dependence on Taiwanese exports makes peace and stability across the Taiwan Strait a vital interest of the United States. American political leaders should emphasize to Beijing that the United States has an obligation to prevent a crisis that would tank the U.S. stock market, cut critical supply chains, and fell many American industries.

Clarifying U.S. interests would not impugn 50 years of careful diplomacy with China, nor would it require any change to U.S. policy as established by the Taiwan Relations Act, the Six Assurances, or the series of joint U.S.-Chinese statements, made between 1972 and 1982, known as the Three Communiqués. The updated statements, rather, would convey to Chinese leaders that certain changes to the cross-strait status quo would be unacceptable to the United States, even if Taiwan were to acquiesce to those changes under pressure. Washington would support a peaceful unification, for example, only if the process guaranteed the security of critical supply chains.

Once the United States makes its interests clear, it will also have to back up its statements with a new playbook to deter Chinese actions that fall below the military threshold. Beijing may already be in the early stages of imposing a quarantine on the island. Washington should not tolerate additional measures that interfere with commerce and maritime traffic in and out of Taiwan. To deter China from taking such steps, the next U.S. Congress should use legislation to clarify its wholesale, bipartisan opposition to Chinese blockades, quarantines, and other restrictions. Even if the White House continued to take a softer position, this would send a message to Beijing that other political actors might force the president’s hand. Congress should also press the administration to prepare punitive diplomatic and financial measures for use in the event of an escalating Chinese quarantine of Taiwan. By planning these reprisals well ahead of any coercive action, Washington would signal that a maritime pressure campaign would entail clear costs for Beijing and carry dangerous escalation risks.

The United States should strengthen military deterrence, too. The next Congress must pressure the Trump administration to release the stalled $14 billion package of arms sales to Taiwan, as well as other security assistance the administration is slow-rolling. Congress has a legal obligation to do so under the Taiwan Relations Act. Pausing security aid violates U.S. policy, and allowing the Trump team to make this concession in its likely quixotic pursuit of rapprochement with Xi only weakens Washington’s hand.

Washington does not have to—and shouldn’t—take these steps alone. U.S. allies such as Germany, Japan, and South Korea would suffer nearly as much as the United States if China were to take control of Taiwan’s semiconductor industry. These countries have an interest in working quietly together to deter and develop joint responses to Chinese coercion while also collaborating to diversify their chip supply chains and make them more resilient. In the United States, this work does not have to wait for a new presidential administration. Congress can send delegations and use other diplomatic opportunities to start developing plans with parliamentary counterparts abroad.

As the 2028 U.S. presidential election approaches, moreover, candidates from both parties should speak clearly about the country’s strong economic interests in Taiwan and about their commitment to deterrence. This does not require embracing bellicose positions, but presidential candidates do need to state firmly that any move by Beijing to choke off vital supply chains would harm U.S. interests and demand an American response. Consistent messaging would also signal to China’s leaders that they should not expect a future administration to be bound by Trump’s policy concessions—and signal to allies that come 2029, the United States will again be a reliable partner in standing up to China’s economic aggression.

The United States needs to update its Taiwan policy, but ceding ground as Trump has done only makes the cross-strait balance less stable. Unlike other Cold War–era policies, the importance of preventing Chinese aggression against Taiwan has become more—not less—vital with the passage of time. U.S. interests in Taiwan are plainer and easier to explain than they have ever been. Only by stating those interests clearly and backing up the words with actions can the United States continue to avert a crisis or conflict that would be devastating for all.

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

What the Godfather of Realism Can Teach Trump

Beijing’s Theory of Success in the Face of Failure

The New Epicenter of Global Terror

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

EGHOSA E. OSAGHAE is Director-General of the Nigerian Institute of International Affairs.

How Africa’s Weak States and Rare Earths Drive Transnational Extremism

Twenty-five years after 9/11, much of the world has moved on from the war on terrorism. Following the defeat of the Islamic State in Iraq and Syria and the U.S. withdrawal from Afghanistan in 2021, Washington has turned to other major concerns; along with many of its European allies, the United States has been more preoccupied with the growing confrontation with China, and with the wars in Ukraine and the Middle East, than with the threat of transnational extremism. Buttressing this shift has been a general decline in terrorist attacks in many parts of the world since ISIS’s peak, in the mid-2010s.

Yet there is one region of the world where terrorism not only persists but also appears to be growing. According to reports by the United Nations and the Global Terrorism Index, Africa is the overwhelming center of terrorist activity today. The African continent accounts for more than 50 percent of all global terrorism-related deaths over the past five years. It also hosts three of the four deadliest jihadi groups in the world: ISIS, which is now primarily based in Africa, Jamaat Nusrat al-Islam wal-Muslimin (JNIM), and al-Shabab. (The fourth is Tehrik-e-Taliban Pakistan, also known as the Pakistani Taliban.) Six of the ten countries that are most affected by terrorism are also in Africa: Burkina Faso, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Mali, Niger, Nigeria, and Somalia. And whereas the Middle East has had a 93 percent decline in terrorist attacks between 2017 and 2025, the Sahel has experienced a 17 percent growth over the same period, with a record 4,043 terrorism deaths in 2023 alone.

To the extent that African terrorism has attracted global concern, much of the attention has focused on the Sahel region. The broad swath of land separating the Sahara and the Maghreb in the north from sub-Saharan Africa is a hotbed of extremism, hosting JNIM and ISIS affiliates such as Boko Haram in Nigeria, Cameroon, and Chad, as well as bandit groups such as Lakurawa in Niger and Nigeria and al-Shabab in Somalia. But terrorist activity in Africa now extends to a host of other countries in western, central, and southern Africa as well, including Benin, the Central African Republic, Mozambique, and Togo. Many of these countries have little previous history of jihadi ideology or extremist violence.

World leaders overlook this continent-wide terrorist threat at their own peril. Not only have terrorist groups become a major challenge to African stability and development; they also pose a growing danger to global security. For now, these groups may appear to have little capacity to project their power beyond Africa’s borders. Yet amid the new scramble for Africa’s mineral resources—and for influence on the continent—this may change. Global powers, especially China and Russia, have been increasing their efforts to gain economic, political, and military leverage in Africa, which has in turn undermined collective international initiatives to fight terrorism. And many African jihadi groups themselves have strong anti-Western sentiments and have been using the resource wars to help build transnational networks.

Thus far, the global response to the African threat has largely focused on military operations, especially in the Sahel. But to truly address the phenomenon, outside powers and world leaders will need to understand the underlying forces that have led to this proliferation of extremist groups. Above all, they will need to find new ways to help African countries address the erosion of state institutions and governance that has made terrorism endemic across the continent.

Africa’s entanglement with terrorist and jihadi groups is hardly new. Al-Qaeda’s 1998 attacks on U.S. embassies in Dar-es-Salaam and Nairobi were planned from Sudan, where Osama bin Laden had been based between 1991 and 1996. After 9/11, security experts viewed those attacks as a crucial precursor that should have set off alarm bells. Then, in the early years of the twenty-first century, local African groups coalesced into al-Shabab, based in Somalia, which also began to pursue transnational terrorism as an affiliate of al-Qaeda. Later, al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb formed a major foothold in Mali. These developments prompted a number of Western-led antiterrorism campaigns, often supported by African forces. Most notable were the military interventions in the Sahel led by the French and other Western special forces and the initial U.S.-supported intervention force in Somalia, the so-called Somali Warlord Alliance.

But these counterterrorism operations did little to stop extremist activity in Africa. In the 2010s, countries in and close to the Sahel began to transition from being breeding grounds of terrorism to becoming the focus of terrorist attacks. Over the past few years, these countries have not only continued to be the places where terrorist groups can organize, radicalize their adherents, and spread violent extremist ideology; they also now bear the brunt of terrorist attacks and killings and the instability and breakdown in security that go with them. Worse, this activity has spread to new areas of the continent, such as Benin, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, and Mozambique, where there had not been jihadis previously. In these countries, terrorist groups have capitalized on long-standing popular grievances spurred by government neglect, exclusion, and poverty. The jihadi expansion has been led by new local groups with connections to al-Qaeda and its branch JNIM in the Sahel region of West Africa and to ISIS affiliates in central Africa, whose major motivations include generating wealth from illegal mining, smuggling, and transnational organized crime.

What the outside world has often failed to recognize—both in the earlier and more recent phases of extremism—are the underlying factors that have made so many African countries prone to this activity. In nearly every case, fragile or collapsing state institutions have been crucial enablers: states’ inability to ensure the welfare and security of their citizens, to exercise sovereign authority over the length and breadth of their territory, and to alleviate the widespread poverty, unemployment, and disease that have created openings for extremism. In many countries, these factors have been exacerbated by large numbers of internally displaced persons and refugees, a lack of social infrastructure, and perennial violent conflict.

The unique characteristics of African demography have added to the challenge. In contrast to the aging populations of most other regions of the world, people under the age of 30 make up nearly 70 percent of Africa’s population and are also most affected by state failure. Large numbers of young people are not in school—more than 270 million African children in 2026, accounting for more than 40 percent of the global total. Nigeria alone has nearly 20 million youth out of school, the highest of any country in the world. At the same time, youth unemployment has remained persistently high: the continental average is 18 percent, but in some countries, such as Djibouti, where mass employment opportunities are limited and young people generally lack the requisite education for skilled employment in the logistics and transport sector that dominates the economy, youth unemployment may reach more than 75 percent. Disaffected young Africans constitute a huge reservoir of potential recruits for extremist groups, which have grown commensurately: according to recent estimates, al-Shabab has more than 15,000 fighters and JNIM has around 6,000.

But state complicity also plays a role. It is now well documented that political leaders have been building ties to bandit and terrorism groups to secure and consolidate their power. For example, the emergence of Boko Haram and various other militant outfits in Nigeria has been abetted by state governors and influential politicians employing them to intimidate opponents during elections. It is worth noting, as well, that elites in Muslim-dominated northern Nigeria initially opposed the U.S. designation of Boko Haram as a terrorist group—based on the assumption that the group was defending the interests of mainstream Muslims and northerners. This kind of support helped legitimize the group and enabled it to extend its control beyond the northeast of the country. The adoption of sharia law in the early years of the twenty-first century by 12 predominantly Muslim states in northern Nigeria also helped incubate Boko Haram. In this sense, Nigeria followed the path of Sudan, whose long history of Islamic fundamentalism and practice of sharia law made it a safe haven for bin Laden as he began to build his ideology of global terrorism.

Yet another contributing factor has been climate change and natural resource wars. Many of these countries have difficult desert geographies and have been beset with growing food insecurity and conflicts over diminishing resources. The result has been the emergence of so-called ungoverned territories within a number of African countries—areas characterized by power vacuums, porous borders, and illicit mining of critical minerals and other natural resources by terrorist groups, bandits, and criminal gangs. Often in these places, parallel state structures have been established by nonstate actors—not only to provide public goods, including safety, protection, and social services but also to challenge state legitimacy through sectarian uprisings and war. As these extremist nonstate groups seek to root their ideologies in ethnic, religious, and communal identities, territorial control has become central to their goals. This is especially true of jihadi groups such as al-Shabab, Boko Haram, JNIM, and ISIS affiliates, which aim to create caliphates, although none has thus far demonstrated the capacity to take over an entire state.

Although the domestic sources of African terrorism are important, external factors and global interconnections have been even more crucial to its spread. Take the continent’s jihadi and radical Islamist movements, which have for decades been inspired and supported from outside. For example, the 1979 Iranian Revolution spurred the rise of Shiite movements among Muslim youths across Africa. The Iranian government actively supported the movements by sponsoring the establishment of schools and mosques, offering scholarships for students to study in Iran, and creating training and indoctrination programs. Today, Ibrahim al-Zakzaky’s radical Islamic Movement of Nigeria, whose goal is to overthrow the secular government and establish Islamic law, remains one of the more resilient Iranian affiliates in Africa and has training camps in northwestern Nigeria that are linked to Iran and Lebanon’s Hezbollah.

In the 1990s, the exploits of the Taliban in Afghanistan inspired young Muslim radicals in northern Nigeria, where the forerunners of Boko Haram initially called themselves the Taliban. In turn, terrorism researchers have identified Qatar, Saudi Arabia, Turkey, and other Middle Eastern powers as funders of Salafi extremism, which espouses the violent imposition of Islamic law on everyone, including fellow Muslims. Such is the success of these efforts that many African terrorist groups today consider themselves part of the global network of Salafist jihadism.

Among the most important external forces driving African terrorism are porous borders and the free flows of weapons, drugs, money, and human trafficking into and out of the continent. These flows have facilitated the integration of African groups into transnational terror networks. Al-Shabab was one of the earliest examples of this phenomenon: the group was founded by Somali supporters of the Afghan mujahideen in the late 1980s. Later, it became an affiliate of al-Qaeda, pledging allegiance to the group in 2012.

Other African groups followed a similar trajectory. Al-Qaeda’s African affiliates have grown to include Ansaru, a splinter faction of Boko Haram operating in northern Nigeria, as well as al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb, which is based in Algeria. ISIS has added a panoply of African groups to its own network, such as Islamic State Sahel Province in Burkina Faso, Mali, and Niger, and Islamic State West Africa Province, which is active in the Lake Chad Basin. And it has developed ties with Islamic State Central Africa Province, which also operates in parts of Mozambique and the Democratic Republic of the Congo and Uganda; and Islamic State in Somalia, which is a rival of al-Shabab. Meanwhile, al-Shabab has cultivated links to the Houthis in Yemen, a development that has heightened the potential for transnational terrorism to become enmeshed in the struggle for control of the Red Sea and the Gulf of Aden between Egypt, Ethiopia, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey following conflicts and closures in the Strait of Hormuz.

These transnational affiliations have offered African groups many advantages, bringing new sources of funding, new weapons (including drones), and training in suicide bombing and IEDs, as well as indoctrination, foreign fighters, intelligence, and logistics. In return, African terrorist groups have helped their international affiliates by engaging in sea piracy and smuggling, human trafficking and migrant smuggling, money laundering, kidnapping for ransom, robbery, livestock rustling, drug trafficking, and illegal mining. African groups use many of these activities, as well as illegally taxing the areas under their control, to raise illicit funds for their international affiliates.

In addition, African jihadis provide personnel for international operations—al-Shabab members have been involved in terrorist training operations in the Philippines—and safe havens for fugitive terrorists. During the decades of the post-9/11 war on terrorism, there was a steady influx to Africa of foreign terrorists, fighters, and instructors who were fleeing Iran, Iraq, Syria and other parts of the Middle East. Others came from North Africa, especially following the civil war in Libya and unrest in Algeria. These foreign extremists brought with them massive amounts of weapons and helped turn Africa into a center of terrorist activity. As the epicenter of global terrorism today, Africa now houses numerous al-Qaeda and ISIS operatives, a fact that increases the risk that Africa-based groups may be planning operations beyond the continent.

In theory, international engagement in African security should help contain the rising terrorism threat. But the record thus far is not promising. During the war on terrorism, the United States, the EU, the United Nations, and international security organizations provided support for numerous counterterrorism campaigns on the continent. These efforts included backing the G5 Sahel—a counterterrorism initiative by Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Mauritania, and Niger—as well as support for regional counterterrorism initiatives, including in Mozambique and Somalia, as well as the Multinational Joint Task Force, which aimed to combat terrorism in the Lake Chad region. Most of these efforts failed or were not sustained long enough to change the trajectory, and the worsening security situations that they left in their wake fueled anti-Western sentiment. And now, with Russia and China pursuing interests of their own in many parts of Africa, involvement by outside powers is often driven by a desire for strategic minerals and a quest for geopolitical influence, interventions that have only further encouraged terrorist activity.

In the Sahel, the withdrawal of U.S. and French forces between 2022 and early 2025 created power and security vacuums. Russia has quickly exploited the situation, using bilateral defense pacts and mercenary and Africa Corps forces to gain access to minerals and natural resources that France had previously controlled. Indeed, in many parts of Africa, global competition for critical minerals has become a new driver in the spread of terrorism. In the Manono district of Congo, for instance, American, Australian, and Chinese firms are enmeshed in long-running disputes over lithium deposits, which has made counterterrorism operations in the same area more difficult: the various international players no longer want to coordinate on counterterrorism and terrorist groups are using external rivalries to strengthen their own operations. A similar scenario is playing out in other resource-rich areas.

It is now urgent that leading world powers adopt a different approach. A first step is to recognize the problem for what it is. Jihadi terrorism today is not a local or regional problem afflicting a handful of African countries; it is a worldwide threat. It can be effectively tackled only through concerted and sustained international action. Although internationally-backed counterterrorism efforts by the African Union and other regional forces are more needed than ever, the new scramble for resources has become an obstacle to international collaboration in Africa. In this regard, it will be crucial to embrace the common protocols and regulatory frameworks for critical minerals and other resources being developed by the AU and other regional African organizations—which seek to reduce the scope for illicit activities and ensure that that the countries benefit more from the resources through more inclusive and accountable governance structures.

Most important, outside powers need to engage with the root causes that have made the continent so prone to terrorist activity, even when it has been effectively countered elsewhere in the world. As the former UN Secretary-General Ban Ki-moon said in 2015, “Good governance is what will kill terrorism.” Northern Ireland after the Good Friday Agreement—whose institutional reforms such as power sharing, human rights protections, and community policing proved to be more effective than military operations—is a case in point; so is the 2016 peace agreement between the Colombian government and the FARC, which included governance reforms in previously neglected parts of the country as part of its stabilization measures.

The United States as well as governments in Europe and international bodies need to shift their focus from military operations to addressing the actual social and economic sources of state fragility that have made so many African countries fertile ground for conflict and extremist violence. This would of course involve strengthening state capacities, and it would require regional efforts as well as more targeted development assistance. None of it will be easy, but doing nothing would be far worse. Unless African countries and their international partners can begin to address the underlying dynamics that are driving Africa’s transnational terrorism problem, it is unlikely to stay in Africa for much longer.

Circle’s USDC Transaction Volume Rises 49% Even as Reserve Income Faces Pressure

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Circle reported $13.9 trillion of USDC onchain transaction volume in the third quarter of 2026, up 49% year over year, while average USDC circulation reached $73.3 billion. The issuer’s results show strong network activity, but that does not translate directly into equivalent revenue growth.

Stablecoin businesses are often judged by a single number: how many dollars’ worth of tokens are circulating. Circle’s latest earnings suggest that approach misses a substantial part of what is happening around USDC, and also oversimplifies how the company makes money.

In its November 4 SEC-filed results, Circle reported $13.9 trillion in USDC onchain transaction volume for the quarter, an increase of 49% from a year earlier. Average USDC in circulation rose 8% to $73.3 billion, while total revenue and reserve income increased 17% to $865 million.

A stablecoin can move between users many times without the amount outstanding increasing. That makes onchain transfer volume a useful measure of network activity but a poor substitute for payment revenue, spending by real-world customers or income retained by the issuer.

Some onchain activity also reflects exchange settlement, liquidity management and transfers between controlled addresses. It would be a mistake to present $13.9 trillion as a tally of retail purchases or to assume the growth rate proves stablecoins have replaced ordinary banking payments.

Circle’s net income from continuing operations reached $226 million, up 5% year on year. Adjusted EBITDA increased 85% to $317 million. Differences in the growth rates reflect a changing mix of operational, distribution and accounting factors; investors should assess the underlying disclosures rather than treating all three numbers as interchangeable signals.

Circle highlighted that its payments network reached $41.3 billion in annualised total payment volume during the quarter. It also announced plans to acquire Tazapay, a cross-border payments platform, with completion expected in 2027 subject to approvals. These initiatives aim to make it easier for businesses to move between traditional payment systems and digital-dollar settlement.

This strategy faces a practical challenge: issuing a token is relatively simple compared with connecting a broad set of banking, compliance and payout arrangements. Customers require dependable redemption, useful local currency access and clear rules. Onchain transaction volumes alone do not tell us whether those services are profitable.

The competitive environment is also changing. As NewsBTC noted when some stablecoins faced new European trading restrictions, distribution is shaped by regulation as well as technology. An issuer needs both.

Beyond the headline USDC supply, the key numbers are revenue after distribution costs, payment volumes generated by identifiable services, changes in the reserve-income environment and the cost of expanding infrastructure. Circle has shown activity and profit growth, but it must continue converting that network usage into durable economics.

The bigger story is a maturing business model. Stablecoins increasingly compete as financial infrastructure, not simply as exchange trading pairs. Circle’s quarter offers evidence that USDC is moving at scale, while also reminding readers that volume, adoption and shareholder returns are three different questions.

This article was written by the News Desk and edited by Samuel Rae.

Original Medicare Has No Out-of-Pocket Limit – Add One Before You Need Care

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Original Medicare puts no yearly limit on your out-of-pocket costs. Cap them with a Medigap policy like Plan G or a Medicare Advantage plan. If you choose Medigap, buy it in your six-month open enrollment window, when insurers can’t turn you down or charge more for your health.

Millions go without either one. KFF found that in 2023, 3.5 million people, 13% of everyone in traditional Medicare, lacked any supplemental coverage.

Without it, your share of Part B care is usually 20% of the Medicare-approved amount after the $283 deductible. By our math, a $100,000 year of outpatient cancer treatment leaves you more than $20,000 to pay. Part A adds a $1,736 hospital deductible in 2026 that you can owe more than once a year, plus daily charges for long hospital and skilled nursing stays.

Your Medigap window opens the first month you’re 65 or older with Part B, and it never repeats. Outside it, insurers in most states can ask health questions, then turn you down or charge more, unless a guaranteed issue right applies, such as your Advantage plan pulling out of your area.

To price Medigap, start with Mutual of Omaha. AM Best rates Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company A+ (Superior). Its online tool quotes Plan G and High-Deductible Plan G from your ZIP code, birth date, and sex, without asking for contact details. On Sept. 23, 2026, it quoted High-Deductible Plan G at $45.84 to $52.19 a month for a 65-year-old woman in Dallas (75201), Columbus (43215), and Phoenix (85004). Couples can save with its household discount where it’s available, though the amount and rules vary by state.

Plan G covers the same benefits at every insurer, so compare your Mutual of Omaha quote with Medicare.gov’s list for your ZIP code before you apply.

Buy Medigap in Your Six-Month Window – Don’t Count on Fall Open Enrollment

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Apply for a Medicare Supplement (Medigap) policy in the six months that start once you’re 65 or older and enrolled in Part B. In that one-time window, insurers can’t turn you down or charge more because of your health. In most states, Medicare’s fall open enrollment offers no second chance, so don’t wait for it.

Once your six months end, insurers can ask about your health, then turn you down or charge more, unless a guaranteed-issue right applies. Major insurers’ applications list even high blood pressure as possible grounds for denial, a KFF review found. Only Connecticut and New York give everyone 65 and older another chance year-round. Massachusetts guarantees one every February and March, and Maine once a year for Plan A.

Two different periods are called open enrollment. Your six months are the Medigap Open Enrollment Period. Medicare’s fall open enrollment, Oct. 15 to Dec. 7, is for joining, switching, or dropping Medicare Advantage and drug plans, and only Rhode Island and Minnesota tie limited Medigap windows to it. State birthday rules don’t help first-time buyers either, since they only let people who already have Medigap switch plans.

If you work past 65, the window opens when Part B starts, even while you have a job-based plan, and can close before you need Medigap. When that plan pays before Medicare, as it does at employers with 20 or more workers, you can delay Part B without a late penalty and save the window for when the coverage ends. Helping a parent? Their Medicare card lists the Part B start date under MEDICAL.

Losing a Medicare Advantage plan brings a separate right, covered in our guide to Medigap after an Advantage plan ends.

Mutual of Omaha is a strong place to apply. Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company holds an A+ (Superior) financial strength rating from AM Best. Its online tool prices Plan G from your ZIP code, birth date, and sex, no contact details needed, and you can apply online. Couples can save with its household discount where it’s available, though the amount and rules vary by state.

Our Mutual of Omaha review has sample quotes from three cities. Plan G benefits don’t vary by insurer, so compare your quote with Medicare.gov’s Plan G prices for your ZIP code, then apply while your window is open.

Nvidia perps slide as market reacts to CEO Jensen Huang’s Chinese spy scandal

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The AI chipmaker announced Sunday that its board of directors is investigating CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with a woman reportedly identified by intelligence officials as a Chinese spy.

Nvidia perpetual futures slid on Hyperliquid following the AI chipmaker's Sunday announcement that its board of directors is investigating CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with a woman reportedly identified by intelligence officials as a Chinese spy.

Nvidia's NVDA-USDC perps fell 3.8% over the past 24 hours to $180.50 on Hyperliquid as of 10:10 p.m. ET Sunday, with 24-hour volume reaching $170 million. The perps began sliding around 4 p.m. ET, hitting a local low of around $178.10 before recovering slightly.

Earlier Sunday, Nvidia announced that it is launching an investigation into Huang following reports that he had been a "target of a foreign intelligence effort."

"While the Board has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board's inquiry will examine all available information," the statement said.

Nvidia also published an internal memo from Huang to employees in which he admitted to being unfaithful to his wife.

"I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry," wrote Huang, adding that he supports the board of directors' investigation into the reported national security concerns and that he will fully cooperate.

The investigation came after the New York Times reported last week that a memo, circulated among top U.S. policymakers last spring, alleged that Alice Huang, a Chinese businesswoman, was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security to gather intelligence on Nvidia's founder.

"Today's news will not slow Nvidia. Jensen is committed to continuing to lead Nvidia as CEO without interruption," a company spokesperson told The Block.

On Polymarket, a prediction market indicated a 16% chance that Huang would announce his resignation or be removed by Dec. 31, 2026. A similar market on Kalshi put the odds of his departure by Jan. 1, 2028, at 18%.

Meanwhile, bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) fell 0.8% to $75,113, while ether Ethereum (ETH) lost 4.2% to trade at $2,140, according to The Block's price page.

Stock futures, however, edged higher late Sunday. The S&P 500 futures rose 0.6%, while the Nasdaq 100 edged up 0.7%.

Asian equities advanced on Monday morning. The Taiwan Weighted Index rose 5.4%, while Japan's Nikkei 225 rose 3.2%. South Korea's Kospi gained 1.7%.

Dan Khus, chief analyst at LVRG Research, told The Block that the slide in Nvidia perps "reflects a classic risk-off reaction," with the drop "likely amplified by leveraged positioning."

"Bitcoin's modest decline suggests limited direct spillover, while Ether's sharper fall points to broader altcoin sensitivity," Khus said.

Rick Maeda, head of markets at Laevitas, said that with U.S. cash markets shut, NVDA perpetual futures on crypto platforms such as Hyperliquid were the only place for price discovery, so "it took the first reaction on thinner liquidity."

"However, the overhang will last as long as the investigation does and the real risks are congressional escalation, tighter scrutiny of China-linked sales, or any sign of a leadership change, given how central Huang is to Nvidia’s story," Maeda added.

Meanwhile, Dominick John, analyst at Zeus Research, said the decline in Nvidia per "signaled weakening near-term sentiment and increased bearish positioning."

"Deeply negative funding suggests short positioning may be becoming crowded, leaving room for a relief rebound if underlying U.S. equities stabilize," said John.

Losing Your Medicare Advantage Plan Gives You a Guaranteed Right to Medigap

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

If your Medicare Advantage plan is ending, you have a limited-time right to buy a Medicare Supplement (Medigap) policy without being turned down or charged more for your health. Use it by returning to Original Medicare instead of joining another Advantage plan, and apply before your old plan ends so your coverage has no gap.

Your plan has to mail you a non-renewal notice at least 90 days before it ends, so by early October. In July 2026, one of the largest Medicare Advantage insurers told investors its 2027 plan exits would affect about 600,000 members.

Outside your one-time Medigap open enrollment window, federal rules let insurers ask about your health, then turn you down or charge more. A plan exit flips that. If your Advantage plan leaves Medicare or your area, or you move out of its service area, insurers must sell you a policy, cover pre-existing conditions, and can’t charge more because of your health.

The right covers Plan G if you became eligible for Medicare in 2020 or later, Plan F if earlier, but not Plan N. To claim it:

The right lasts until 63 days after your old plan ends, but applying late leaves a gap. Leaving Advantage by choice? Get approved for Medigap before you drop your plan.

Mutual of Omaha is a strong place to start. AM Best rates Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company A+ (Superior). Its online tool prices Plan G from your ZIP code, birth date, and sex, with no contact details required, and you can apply online. Couples leaving the same plan can each save with its household discount where it’s available, though the amount and rules vary by state. Our Mutual of Omaha review has sample quotes from three cities.

Get that quote the day your notice arrives, and since every Plan G pays the same benefits, compare it with Medicare.gov’s list for your ZIP code. Pass on this right, and in most states a later switch means answering health questions.

Mutual of Omaha Medicare Supplement Review – A Leading Insurer With A+ Ratings

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Choose Mutual of Omaha for your Medicare Supplement plan if you want a large, long-established insurer and can use its household discount where it’s available. Because every Plan G pays the same benefits, compare your quote on Medicare.gov before you enroll.

Original Medicare leaves you exposed. You owe the Part A hospital deductible, $1,736 per benefit period in 2026, plus 20% of most Medicare-approved Part B costs, and there’s no yearly limit on what you can spend. A Medigap policy, the private Medicare Supplement Insurance that fills those gaps, works the same way at every insurer in most states because the lettered plans are standardized. That leaves three things to compare between companies: the premium and how it’s priced, the discounts and extras that come with the policy, and the financial strength of the insurer.

Mutual of Omaha Medicare Supplement Insurance comes from a company founded in 1909 in Omaha, Nebraska, as Mutual Benefit Health & Accident Association. Medicare Supplement is still its largest product by revenue. In April 2026, the rating agency AM Best called Mutual of Omaha a leading Medicare Supplement insurer and affirmed an A+ (Superior) financial strength rating for Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company, the parent company. Mutual of Omaha has also been accredited by the Better Business Bureau since 1940 and holds an A+ BBB rating.

With the benefits fixed by federal law, Mutual of Omaha stands out for its household discount where available, its extra programs, and the financial strength behind it.

The lineup varies by state. In Dallas, Columbus, and Phoenix, Mutual of Omaha’s quote tool offered four plans to a 65-year-old new to Medicare in 2026. Plan F, and High-Deductible Plan F where it’s sold, remain open only to people eligible for Medicare before 2020.

Your premium depends on your ZIP code, age, sex, and household. Mutual of Omaha’s quote tool showed these monthly prices on Sept. 23, 2026, for a 65-year-old woman and man, before any discount:

Because every Plan G pays the same benefits, compare these quotes with the Plan G prices Medicare.gov lists for your ZIP code before you choose.

Pricing methods differ by state. Medicare.gov lists Mutual of Omaha’s Plan G with attained-age pricing in Texas and Ohio, which bases the premium on your current age, so it adjusts as you get older. In Arizona it’s issue-age pricing, which sets the rate by your age when you buy.

Where it’s available, the household discount can do the most for your bill. It lowers your premium if you live with your spouse or have shared a home with another adult for a year or longer, and the amount and the rules vary by state. In Ohio, the person you live with must also have or apply for a Mutual of Omaha Medigap policy. On our Sept. 23, 2026, quote for a 65-year-old woman in Dallas (75201), the discount lowered her Plan G premium from $215.65 to $189.77 a month.

Policyholders also get three discount programs, none of which is insurance. Mutually Well charges $29.99 a month for access to more than 10,000 gyms, with no sign-up fee or contract, and adds online fitness classes. EyeMed provides a $50 eye exam and 40% off frames up to $140, and Amplifon offers reduced hearing aid prices with a 60-day risk-free trial. You also save 15% on a Mutual of Omaha dental policy bought alongside your Medigap coverage. None of the three programs is available in New York.

Your timing decides your options. Your Medigap Open Enrollment Period is a six-month window that opens in the first month you’re at least 65 and enrolled in Part B. Inside it, Mutual of Omaha can’t turn you down or charge you more because of your health.

After the window closes, federal rules let insurers ask health questions, then decline an application or charge more. A new policy can also hold off on covering pre-existing conditions for as long as six months. Some states add protections. In specific situations, federal guaranteed issue rights also require insurers to sell you a policy without charging more for your health. They apply if employer coverage that paid after Medicare ends, for example, or if you leave a Medicare Advantage plan within a year of joining it at 65.

Medicare’s fall Open Enrollment, Oct. 15 to Dec. 7, doesn’t change those rules. It’s the window for switching between Original Medicare, Medicare Advantage, and Part D drug plans. Medigap applications are accepted year-round.

You can apply online after a quote, by phone with a licensed agent, or with a local agent. If you’re switching, keep your current policy until the new one is approved, because a canceled policy may not be available to you again. And since Medigap doesn’t include drug coverage, budget for a separate Part D plan.

Claims mostly handle themselves. With most Medigap policies, the insurer gets your Part B claim information straight from Medicare. Mutual of Omaha says it processes 99% of claims within 24 hours, based on its 2024 claims across its companies and insurance lines. Policyholders can pay premiums and update details through the Customer Access website.

The company behind a policy you may keep for decades matters too. AM Best rates Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company A+ (Superior), S&P Global rates it A+, and Moody’s rates it A1. Mutual of Omaha lists all three on its financial strength page. The company that issues your policy depends on your state. Our Texas and Arizona quotes named Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company, and the Ohio quote named its affiliate Omaha Supplemental Insurance Company.

As with every standardized Medigap policy, your coverage renews automatically as long as you pay the premium, even if your health changes.

Medicare Advantage is the different path, and Mutual of Omaha doesn’t sell it. These private plans take the place of Original Medicare and cover your Part A and Part B services. They cap your yearly costs for covered services, often charge low or no premiums beyond Part B, and usually include drug coverage. In exchange, you may need to stay in the plan’s network and get referrals or approval before some care. Original Medicare with a Medigap policy lets you see any doctor or hospital nationwide that takes Medicare. You can’t pair Medigap with an Advantage plan, and if you start with Advantage and want Medigap later, expect health questions unless a guaranteed issue right applies.

Mutual of Omaha is a strong Medigap choice from a company that has made Medicare Supplement its largest business. Its parent company’s A+ ratings from AM Best and S&P Global, its household discount where available, online applications, and built-in discount programs add value beyond the standardized benefits.

Shoppers who care most about the lowest premium should compare Medicare.gov’s list for their ZIP code first. If you value a large, long-established insurer and can use the household discount, get a Mutual of Omaha Medicare Supplement Insurance quote during your six-month open enrollment window or while a guaranteed issue right applies.

Learn more about Mutual of Omaha Medicare Supplement Insurance.

Trump Reflects on Republicans’ Midterm Losses, Reacts to J.D. Vance’s Own Analysis in ’60 Minutes’ Interview

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The president offers a few reasons why he believes the Democrats gave his party a crushing defeat last week.

President Donald Trump reflected on the Republicans’ big losses last week with 60 Minutes correspondent Ross Douthat in his first sit-down interview since the midterm elections.

Douthat asked Trump for his analysis of what happened. “Well, No. 1, I was not on the ballot,” he replied. “But I campaigned hard for some people. And I think if I was on the ballot, it would’ve been a much different situation because you saw the level of popularity and love. There was love. We had massive rallies. You had to transfer those voters, however, to candidates that they didn’t like. But there’s something beyond that. They didn’t like the war. They didn’t like the fact that gasoline went up, ’cause I had it down at a low, and there’s a great dislike, incredibly, for Israel.” Related Stories TV Elon Musk Is Portrayed on 'SNL': "The Worst Thing South Africa Has Ever Done" TV James Talarico Crashes 'SNL' Cold Open, Apologizes for Texting Us So Much

The interview aired a few days after Vice President J.D. Vance posted his own lengthy and candid analysis of what he believed went wrong for his party. He outlined four things that disaffected Republicans wanted, which led to the Democrats reclaiming Congress: “End the war, focus on cost of living and inflation, put America first and change our relationship to Israel.”

Douthat asked Trump if he agreed with Vance’s analysis. “I don’t disagree with it,” Trump replied, adding: “He asked me what do I think. I said, ‘You write what you want.'”

Trump added that he does wish Vance would have included one thing he didn’t: “The only thing I wish he would’ve said there, if he felt it, and you’d have to ask him about that, but if you had to do it again, would you do it? You know, he’s part of a decision-making process. And my answer is an emphatic yes because you can’t let Iran have a nuclear weapon. I think, historically, this will be a very good decision. Is it a good decision for today and tomorrow? Maybe not. But you know, I have to go with what’s right for our country. If they had a nuclear weapon during my term, I would be excoriated far worse than what’s happened. … I’ve stopped them from having a nuclear weapon. That’s a big thing.”

Trump argued that the economy has been strong during his second term, even though Douthat noted that polls have shown Americans are unhappy with the economy. “Cost of living, inflation, and so on,” he said. “Is that all just the Iran war?”

Trump replied: “Well, it’s not really the Iran war because the numbers are phenomenal. I think it was bad messaging, maybe by me, but I would talk about it all the time. So I don’t know, how much can I talk about it? Look, the press doesn’t cover … positive news for Republicans. They don’t cover certainly positive news for me. So I have to do it myself. I have to get it out there. We have historically good numbers. We have the highest stock market in history.”

Asked what the “bad message” was, Trump replied: “We couldn’t get the word across.”

The interview also touched on Trump’s successor. Douthat asked the president if he will be endorsing a Republican nominee in two years. “Probably. Not necessarily,” he replied. As for how he decides whether to endorse a candidate, Trump said: “I don’t know, we have a tremendous bench. I mean, I have so many people that I have great relationships with.”

Besides Vance, Trump cited U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio as a “great” option and said there are “others” including “some senators that are excellent” but he declined to name names. I know ’em all, I like ’em a lot,” he said. ” So I may or may not get involved. I don’t know. Maybe I’m better off not getting involved.”

Asked if he thinks Democrats will try to impeach him a third time, Trump replied: “What have I done? I’ve had the best two years in the history of the presidency.”

60 Minutes‘ two-part Interview with Trump aired Sunday on CBS and Paramount+.
